# Decisions log

Decisions taken without asking while the user was away. Each entry lists every option that
was on the table, with its consequence and side effects, the option taken and why. Review and
overrule anything here; each entry says what changing it would take.

Entries are newest last.

## Where to start

The run covered phases 2.1 to 8 (D1 to D80). Most entries are choices inside a module. These
change what was planned or what a player notices, so they are worth reading first:

- **D72**: parts have no insert slots; each part has an effects share instead. The one line of
  the plan not delivered as written. Reviewed on 2026-11-13: insert slots are to be built (D81).
- **D79**: grains read the factory samples only, not another generator, which the requirement
  names. Reviewed: a granular effect on a part instead (D82).
- **D77**: the factory samples are generated by the program, not recordings. Reviewed: real
  recordings as multisampled instruments, at the best quality, in files beside the plugin (D83).
- **D75**: Supersaw, Juno Pad and Deep Field were changed in place to use two parts; Juno Pad is
  now two stacked Junos, left and right.
- **D70** and **D73**: how two parts share sixteen voices, and one arpeggiator for both.
- **D68**: the learned chord is five parameters and learning is a switch.
- **D66**: which twelve chords chord memory offers.
- **D62**: what Wood, Metal and Glass are in the modal generator.
- **D9**: matrix destinations that were left out.
- **D12** and the notes under D64, D69, D75: loudness changes to presets the phase did not touch.

## D1. Juno Pad moves from phase 2.1 to phase 2.2 (2026-11-13, phase 2.1)

The plan ships Juno Pad with the LFO. Its signature is pulse-width modulation from an LFO, and
an LFO reaches a destination only through the matrix, which is phase 2.2.

- **Move Juno Pad to 2.2 (taken, recommended).** It ships with the matrix, one chunk later,
  with the route the bank file will use for every later preset. No throwaway code.
- Add a hardwired "LFO1 to G1 pulse width" parameter now. Juno Pad ships an hour earlier, but
  the parameter is a dead end once the matrix exists and stays in the patch format.
- Ship Juno Pad without PWM. It would not sound like a Juno pad, which is the whole preset.

Changing it: nothing to undo; Juno Pad lands in 2.2 either way.

## D2. Tempo for synced LFOs: the host's, else the arp's internal tempo (2026-11-13, phase 2.1)

A synced LFO needs a tempo when the host gives none (the standalone never does).

- **Host tempo when the host reports one, otherwise the existing arp internal tempo parameter
  (taken, recommended).** One tempo for the arp and every synced modulator, so an arp and a
  synced wobble never drift apart. No new parameter.
- A separate part tempo parameter. More flexible, but two tempos that can disagree, and a
  second knob to set in every synced preset.
- Fixed 120 BPM without a host. Simple, but no way to set the standalone's tempo.

Changing it: give `PartSettings` its own tempo and read it in `Part::renderChunk`.

## D3. What the "16-step" LFO shape is (2026-11-13, phase 2.1)

The plan lists a 16-step shape without saying what it holds; the STEP modulator with editable
values is a separate source in phase 2.3.

- **A rising staircase of 16 equal levels per cycle (taken, recommended).** Predictable and
  useful on its own: through a pitch route it plays a stepped run, on a filter a stepped sweep.
  Bank name `steps`.
- A looping random 16-step pattern, new per note. Overlaps sample-and-hold and cannot be set.
- The STEP modulator's values. Couples two modulators and duplicates the STEP source.

Changing it: `LfoShape::steps` in `src/synth/v3/Lfo.h`.

## D4. LFO delay as well as fade-in (2026-11-13, phase 2.1)

The plan names a fade-in. Logic's and Halion's LFOs, and the Juno's, also have a delay before
the modulation starts (checked online: Logic Pro "Delay" and "Attack", Halion one-shot modes).

- **Delay plus fade-in (taken, recommended).** Delayed vibrato, the classic use, needs both. One
  more parameter per LFO (nine instead of eight).
- Fade-in only. Delayed vibrato would start rising at once.

Changing it: drop `delay` from the LFO group; presets then must not use it.

## D5. LFO behaviour details (2026-11-13, phase 2.1)

Smaller calls made while writing the class, each with what else was possible:

- **Free-running local LFOs advance only while their voice sounds (taken).** The alternative,
  advancing all 16 voices' LFOs all the time, costs CPU for no audible gain: with retrigger off
  the phase a note meets is arbitrary either way.
- **Delay and fade restart at every note even with retrigger off; retrigger decides only the
  phase (taken).** The alternative, no fade at all when free-running, loses the
  free-running-vibrato-that-fades-in patch.
- **A synced, free-running LFO follows the host's song position while the transport plays
  (taken).** The alternative, ignoring the song position, lets loops drift between passes.
- **Global LFOs default to synced and free-running; local ones to free rate and retrigger
  (taken).** Matches the design text ("tempo-synced, one phase shared by all voices"). The
  alternative was identical defaults everywhere.
- **Fade-in is a straight line (taken).** An exponential or S-curve fade was the alternative;
  the straight line is what the Juno and most plug-ins do and what the test checks.
- **Shape start points: sine and triangle at 0 rising, the saws and steps at their low end
  (saw down at its top), square high (taken).** The alternative, all shapes from their low
  point, makes sine and triangle start with a jump when routed to pitch.

## D6. How far a pitch route reaches (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

Matrix amounts run from -1 to 1. The design says pitch destinations are in semitones but not
what an amount of 1 means.

- **1 is 48 semitones (taken, recommended).** Four octaves cover the 808 drop (24), the sync
  sweep (24 to 36) and a riser; vibrato of a quarter tone is 0.005, still writable in a bank
  file. Matches the slot pitch span.
- 1 is 24 semitones. Finer for vibrato, but a three-octave sync sweep needs two slots.
- 1 is 96 semitones. Covers everything in one slot, but vibrato amounts get tiny.
- Amounts written in semitones for pitch slots. Most readable, but the amount parameter would
  have to know its slot's destination; a job for the real UI in phase 9.

Changing it: `matrixPitchSemitones` in `src/synth/v3/ModMatrix.h`; presets scale with it.

## D7. What the VCA destination does (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

- **Gain is 1 plus the offset, held between 0 and 1 (taken, recommended).** Negative amounts
  duck, a unipolar LFO with amount -1 gates (Outrun's Gated Pad), and modulation can never push
  a voice above its envelope, so headroom stays as measured. The gain ramps across each
  sub-block, so a square gate does not click (tested).
- Held between 0 and 2. Positive amounts could boost, but a voice could double in level and
  break the fixed headroom.
- An offset in decibels. Natural for tremolo, but a gate needs a very large negative amount.

Changing it: the VCA lines at the end of `Voice::renderSubBlock`.

## D8. Matrix curves (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

The design names linear, exponential and S-curve. Checked online: HALion and Waldorf split
sources into unipolar (0..1) and bipolar (-1..1); X-Stream offers logarithmic positive and
negative curves; Ultra and HISE multiply the amount by the aux (via) source.

- **Linear, Unipolar, Exponential, Logarithmic, S-Curve (taken, recommended).** Unipolar turns
  a bipolar LFO into 0..1, which gating and one-sided vibrato need; logarithmic is the inward
  curve next to the outward exponential. Curves keep the sign; the via source scales after.
- The design's three only. A unipolar LFO would need an extra slot with a constant offset,
  which the matrix has no source for.

Changing it: `ModCurve` and `applyCurve` in `ModMatrix.h`; add new curves only at the end.

## D9. Matrix destinations left for later (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

The design also lists envelope times and other matrix slots' amounts as destinations.

- **Defer both to phase 2.3 or later (taken, recommended).** Envelope times are read once per
  segment, so they need a note-on evaluation path, which comes naturally with the global
  envelope in 2.3. A slot amount as destination does what the via source already does.
  Destination numbers stay stable because new ones go at the end.
- Build them now. More work in 2.2 for no preset that needs them yet.

## D10. Juno Pad uses a global LFO (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

- **GLFO1, free-running, sweeping every voice's pulse width together (taken, recommended).**
  The Juno-106 has a single LFO for all voices (checked online), and the pad's shimmer comes
  from the voices moving together under the chorus.
- LFO1 per voice. Each note would sweep on its own, an ensemble feel, but not a Juno.

## D11. 808 Boom playing behaviour (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

- **Legato with 70 ms glide on overlapping keys, both envelopes one-shot (taken,
  recommended).** Overlapping keys slide without restarting the boom (the trap 808 slide),
  detached keys hit again, and a short tap still booms for its full 1.4 s.
- Mono with retrigger. Every key restarts the boom; no slides.
- Poly. Booms stack into mud on fast notes.

The recipe's pitch drop (24 semitones in 60 ms) is the modern 808-bass punch; the original
TR-808 sweeps much less (one study of samples found a median under a semitone), checked online.

## D12. Bank master gains corrected by 0.5 to 2 dB (2026-11-13, phase 2.2)

The loudness tool is not part of the default build, so its executable dated from phase 1.5.
Rebuilt, it raised every preset by 0.5 to 2 dB. A build of the tool at the commit before phase
2.1 asks for the same corrections, so the shift came from the earlier preset-leak fix, not from
the LFOs or the matrix (the one-voice level test is also unchanged).

- **Apply the corrections (taken, recommended).** The bank is back on its loudness targets; a
  second pass changes nothing.
- Keep the old masters. Every preset 1 to 2 dB under target, and the new presets louder than
  the old ones.

Also taken: the TODO now says to build the tool's target before running it. Adding it to the
default build was the alternative; it would make every build slower for a tool run rarely.

## D13. Riser ships before the noise generator (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

The recipe layers noise over a saw; noise is phase 3.1.

- **Ship Riser now with the saw stack and add the noise layer in 3.1 (taken, recommended).** The
  signature, an eight-bar rise in pitch, filter, width and an accelerating pulse, is all there.
- Wait for 3.1. Nothing to audition in the meantime.

## D14. What opens and closes the global envelope (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **The keys as played, before the arpeggiator (taken, recommended).** It opens with the first
  key of a phrase (none other held) or, with trigger Every Note, with every key, retriggering
  from its current level. It closes when the last key and the pedal are up and no latched arp
  notes remain. A riser under an arp keeps rising while the chord is held.
- The voices: open while any voice sounds. A release tail would hold it open, and every arp
  step would count as a new note.
- The arp's notes. The envelope would restart on every step.

## D15. Synced global-envelope times (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **One set of millisecond times, read as at 120 BPM and scaled by 120 / tempo when sync is on;
  bank files may write `8bars` or `2beats` (taken, recommended).** One parameter per stage; at
  120 BPM a bar is 2000 ms. The generic editor shows the 120 BPM milliseconds until the real UI.
- Separate bar and beat parameters for each stage. Twice the parameters, two ways to set the
  same thing.
- Times always in beats when synced, with their own range. The same parameter would mean
  different units depending on another switch, worse for automation.

## D16. Step modulator details (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **Values -1..1; slide per step with one shared glide (the share of a step a slide takes);
  retrigger on by default (taken, recommended).** With retrigger on it restarts at the first
  note after silence, like the global LFOs, so a pattern starts on the first step in the
  standalone. Off, it follows the host's song position while the transport plays.
- Retrigger off by default. Patterns would sit on the song's grid in a DAW without a setting,
  but in the standalone each phrase would start on an arbitrary step.
- A glide time per step. Sixteen more parameters for a rarely needed difference.

## D17. The chaos source (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **A random walk: a new random target every 0.5 to 1.5 periods of its rate, through two
  smoothing stages; one parameter, the rate (taken, recommended).** Wanders without corners
  (largest step 0.0013 per sub-block at 1 Hz), bounded to -1..1.
- A chaotic attractor (Lorenz, logistic map). More "chaotic" in name, but it orbits a fixed
  shape and needs scaling per parameter set.
- A second "smoothness" parameter. S&H on a global LFO already gives the stepped version.

## D18. Macros are smoothed (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **Each macro moves toward its setting with a 10 ms time constant, per sub-block; the first
  block starts at the setting (taken, recommended).** A macro on a cutoff or level does not
  zipper when automated or turned.
- No smoothing. Host automation would step the destination once per block.

## D19. Envelope-time destinations read once per note (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

- **Evaluated when a note starts or retriggers, from that note's velocity, key and random value
  and the part's current sources; an amount of 1 is 16 times longer (taken, recommended).** The
  uses are velocity and key to envelope time, which are per-note values anyway. No cost while
  the note plays.
- Continuous modulation, rescaling the running segment every sub-block. An LFO could wobble a
  decay, but it needs a segment rescale with an exponential per change.

## D20. The loudness tool measures risers at their top (2026-11-13, phase 2.3)

The tool measures 4 s of each preset; Riser takes 16 s to rise.

- **When a matrix slot uses the global envelope, the tool first renders through the envelope's
  delay, attack, hold and decay (up to 40 s) and measures the 4 s after (taken, recommended).**
  Riser is judged at its loudest, so its top does not come out 10 dB above the bank.
- Measure the first 4 s. Riser's top would be far too loud.
- Leave risers out of the tool and set their master by hand. One more exception to remember.

Found while testing, before commit: the global envelope read a block's settings after that
block's events, so the first note after a change started it with stale times (the same ordering
trap as the earlier preset leak). The settings are now applied before the events.

## D21. Synced delay note values (2026-11-13, phase 2.4)

- **Fourteen values from 1/32 to one bar, with dotted and triplet values, default 1/8; the
  longest delay grows from 1 s to 4 s, a bar at 60 BPM (taken, recommended).** Covers every
  common echo (1/8, dotted 1/8, 1/4) at any usable tempo. The free time knob keeps its 1 s range.
- Reuse the LFO divisions. They run to eight bars, useless for an echo, and miss 1/2. and 1/2T.
- Keep the 1 s line. A synced 1/4 note would cut off below 60 BPM, a dotted 1/4 below 90.

Tempo comes from the host when it reports one, else the arp's internal tempo (as in D2).

## D22. How ping-pong works (2026-11-13, phase 2.4)

Checked online: the E-MU Proteus X manual and Steinberg's Multi Delay sum the input to mono into
the left line and cross-couple the two lines; pyo's example warns that a block-based version
runs the right side one block late.

- **Mono sum into the left line, the lines cross-coupled per sample through the same darkening
  feedback path as tape mode (taken, recommended).** Echoes land left at T, right at 2T, left at
  3T (tested), each bounce scaled by the feedback and a little darker, so ping-pong and tape
  sound like the same echo unit. Per-sample coupling has no block lag. With no feedback there
  is a single echo on the left.
- A clean, unfiltered feedback path for ping-pong. Brighter, but a second voice for the delay.
- Stereo cross-feedback without the mono sum (Retrologue's "Cross"). Keeps the input's stereo
  image but a centred source no longer bounces.

Changing mode clears the lines, since the two leave very different echoes behind.

## D23. The delay starts at its settings (2026-11-13, phase 2.4)

Found while writing the tap test: after prepare, and after the delay is switched on, its time
glided from the default 250 ms to the setting over about 80 ms and its mix ramped from zero.
The first echoes of a freshly loaded patch bent in pitch, and at full mix the dry signal
leaked through for 20 ms.

- **Start at the settings on the first block after a reset or after switching on; keep the
  tape-style glide for changes while it runs (taken, recommended).**
- Leave it. Every preset load would bend its first echoes.

An older effect test (disabling time-based effects clears their tails) had relied on that
leak; it now renders long enough for the first echo to arrive.

## D24. Outrun and Clock Tower authoring choices (2026-11-13, phase 2.4)

- **"UNISON-N" in Neon Brass and Sunset Strings is the VA generator's own unison (2 and 4 per
  saw), not the part's Unison play mode (taken, recommended).** The play mode is monophonic;
  brass and strings must stay polyphonic.
- **Gated Pad gates through one global square LFO at 1/16, restarted with the first chord and
  starting open (taken, recommended).** Every voice chops together. A per-voice LFO would chop
  each note on its own grid, which smears the gate. Retrigger off would lock to a DAW's grid
  but start at a random point in the standalone.
- **Polyrhythm Pad: pulse width from LFO1 at 1/4 (free-running, so in a DAW it locks to the
  song) and cutoff from GLFO1 at 1/4T, three against two (taken).**
- **Bank files `03-outrun.csbank` and `10-clock-tower.csbank`, numbered by the room order of
  `SOUND_BANK_V3.md` (taken).** Grid Keys waits for four-operator FM (3.3), Chord Trigger for
  chord memory (7.1).
- Random Walk's voice level was raised so it reaches its loudness target under the +6 dB master
  cap. Euclid Pulse and Ratchet Run are held below target by the tool's -3 dBFS peak ceiling,
  as plucks are.

Also met: a stale debug database of the embedded bank library made the linker reject it
(LNK1236) after two bank files were added; deleting `build/CoolSynthFactoryBank.dir/Debug`
fixed it. Nothing to decide, noted in case it returns.

## D25. How the noise generator makes its four kinds (2026-11-13, phase 3.1)

Checked online: the NESdev wiki and emulator sources give the NES noise channel as a 15-bit
shift register with feedback from bit 1 (32767 steps) or bit 6 (93 steps, "short mode"); the
musicdsp pink-noise page notes that fixed pinking coefficients degrade at higher sample rates.

- **Pink from seven first-order shelves designed at the running sample rate, poles every two
  octaves from 10 Hz, zeros an octave above (taken, recommended).** Measured within 0.67 dB per
  octave from 62 Hz to 16 kHz at the 96 kHz voice rate. The alternatives: Paul Kellet's fixed
  coefficients (made for 44.1 kHz, they drift at 96 kHz), or Voss-McCartney (cheap, but rippled).
- **Band noise: a state-variable band-pass on the note, its gain set from the resonance and the
  note so the level stays even (taken).** Measured within 0.4 dB from 200 Hz wide to 3 kHz narrow.
  Without the compensation a high, narrow band would be 20 dB quieter than a low, wide one.
- **Chip: the register clocked at 93 times the note, so Short mode plays the note; Clock moves it
  in semitones like the NES period divider; every register step band-limited with the VA's
  4-point BLEP (taken, recommended).** A Short register at 375 Hz repeats every 256 samples to
  within 0.001. A naive register aliases badly once the clock passes the sample rate. Keeping
  the aliasing as "chip character" was the alternative; the crusher (4.2) can add it on purpose.
- **Every kind sits about 3 dB under a saw at the same slot level (taken).** Noise sounds louder
  than a saw at equal RMS, and the presets set their own levels anyway.

## D26. Noise runs on across notes (2026-11-13, phase 3.1)

- **Each generator instance gets its own noise sequence at prepare and never reseeds (taken,
  recommended).** Every note is a different burst, as with analogue noise; two voices never
  sum the same noise coherently.
- Restart the noise at each note. Every hit identical, like a sample; fine for a drum machine,
  wrong for breath and wind.

## D27. Phase 3.1 preset choices (2026-11-13, phase 3.1)

- **Tape Flute's breath is band noise on the note, with a 160 ms chiff from ENV3 (taken).** The
  recipe said LP4 noise and "SVF band-pass for the breath"; the band type in the noise
  generator is that band-pass.
- **Solar Wind: two key-independent noise bands drifting on separate slow global LFOs (0.07 and
  0.045 Hz) over two octaves (taken).** One band was the recipe; the second, off-phase, keeps the
  sweep from sounding like one filter going back and forth.
- **Static Hit uses Short mode (taken).** The recipe says "pitched periodic noise"; Long mode
  would be a pitch-coloured hiss instead.
- **Riser's noise layer is wide pink noise whose tone and level the global envelope opens
  (taken).**
- **New bank files `01-bedroom`, `02-arcade` and `04-observatory`, numbered by room order
  (taken).**

## D28. The loudness tool waits for slow swells too (2026-11-13, phase 3.1)

- **When the amplifier's delay, attack and hold exceed half a second, the tool renders through
  them (up to 10 s) before its 4 s measurement (taken, recommended).** Solar Wind (3 s attack)
  is judged at full swell; measured during the swell it came out 3 dB too loud.
- Measure as before. Every slow pad would be pushed above the bank.

Random Walk's measured loudness moves by about 1 dB between runs because its arp pattern is
random, so the tool may nudge its master by 0.5 dB on a rerun; nothing to fix.

## D29. Link errors on the embedded bank library (2026-11-13, phases 3.1 to 4.2)

Corrected a second time in phase 4.2, below the original entry: the cause is the incremental
linker, not CMake reconfiguring. After 06-basement.csbank was added, the failure came back even
with the library deleted and built on its own first; deleting the `.ilk` files fixed it, and
every failure had followed a new bank file (a new library member). Taken: incremental linking
off in Debug (`/INCREMENTAL:NO`; little to gain with an optimised build), the TODO note now says
to delete the `.ilk` files if it ever returns. The alternative, keeping incremental linking and
deleting `.ilk` files by hand after each new bank file, was rejected as a trap. The original
entry follows.


The embedded bank library failed to link four times (LNK1236, once LNK1106). Every time, CMake
had reconfigured itself in the middle of the build, which a new bank file or a CMakeLists change
triggers. That build can write the library truncated, and later builds update the broken archive
in place, so they keep failing. Deleting `build/Debug/CoolSynthFactoryBank.lib` and building
again always fixed it. (Corrected on the second occurrence: at first I blamed a stale .pdb.)

- **Configure explicitly after adding a bank file (`cmake --preset vs2022-debug`), and delete the
  library if the error appears; both written in TODO's standing notes (taken, recommended).**
- Delete the library before every build with a pre-build step. Never fails, but every build
  would relink the plugin, the standalone and the tests, even with nothing changed.
- Embed the bank in the plugin's own sources instead of a JUCE binary-data library. Removes the
  library, but replaces JUCE's generator with one of our own.

The data-only libraries now also build without debug info, which they never needed.

## D30. The two-operator FM generator (2026-11-13, phase 3.2)

- **A type of its own, "FM2", with the four-operator generator a separate type in 3.3 (taken,
  recommended).** Two operators fit the eight slot parameters with room to spare (ratio, fine,
  index, feedback, carrier ratio, mix); four operators need paging. One FM4 type for both was the
  alternative, but a two-operator patch would carry two unused operators and the paging.
- **Ratio is continuous (0.25 to 16, logarithmic), not stepped to DX ratios (taken).** Integer and
  half ratios are typed exactly in bank files, and a sweep of the ratio through the matrix
  makes the inharmonic glides that stepped ratios cannot.
- **Feedback through the average of the modulator's last two outputs (taken).** Reported online
  (Native Instruments forum, on emulating Yamaha chips; the thread itself calls it unproven) as
  what keeps high feedback from buzzing at half the sample rate. Measured: full feedback leaves
  -72 dB above 40 kHz.
- **Phases restart at a fresh note and run in double precision (taken).** Every attack is the
  same, as on a DX; free-running phases would make each note's attack differ.

## D31. Keeping FM from aliasing (2026-11-13, phase 3.2)

Checked online: CCRMA's FM notes suggest limiting the index to about (fs/2 - carrier) /
modulator; forum advice is to oversample.

- **Limit the index where the first sideband that would fold into the audible band, after the
  voice's decimation, has a Bessel amplitude under 1e-4 (taken, recommended).** Measured: the
  largest alias below 20 kHz -75 dB against the strongest sideband at a 2 kHz carrier with index
  10 asked for (held at 3.0). Low and middle notes are never limited.
- The Carson-rule cap from CCRMA. Tried first: -57.6 dB, since the Bessel tail just past
  Carson's bandwidth still folds back.
- Oversample the FM generator again internally. Clean at any index, but twice the cost and a
  second decimator per voice.
- No limit. DX-style grit at high notes, but uncontrolled.

## D32. Phase 3.2 presets (2026-11-13, phase 3.2)

- **Glass Bell has a second, quieter FM pair (ratio 5 on a carrier at 2, 7 cents sharp) for a
  shimmering high partial (taken).** The recipe's single pair sounded plain against the plate.
- **FM Knock plays mono, so every note knocks; its level and sustain were raised so it reaches
  the loudness target under the +6 dB master cap (taken).**
- **New bank file `05-greenhouse.csbank` with Glass Bell, Rain Chime and Crystal Pluck (taken).**

## D33. The four-operator FM generator (2026-11-13, phase 3.3)

The plan asks for six algorithms with "per-operator level and ratio across P1..P8 with paging".

- **Eight parameters, no paging: algorithm, the ratios of operators 2 to 4, their levels and
  feedback on operator 4; operator 1 is the carrier at the note (taken, recommended).** Paging
  needs UI support the throwaway editor does not have; the slot's own octave, semitone and fine
  move operator 1. Per-operator fine detune is the price; a ratio like 14.02 still detunes one.
- Paging over two pages of eight. More control, but the generic editor would show sixteen
  generic parameters for one slot and the bank syntax would need pages.
- **Six algorithms: stack, two into one, stack plus one, two pairs, one into three, organ
  (taken).** They cover the classic four-operator families (the DX7's 32 six-operator algorithms
  reduce to these shapes on four operators).
- **A Level is the index (1 is 8 radians) where the operator modulates and its output level where
  it sounds; carriers are summed and normalised by their total level (taken).** One knob per
  operator whatever the algorithm; the output never exceeds 1 (tested on all six at full).
- **Every modulation is limited with the two-operator generator's alias rule for the operator it
  drives (taken).**

Grid Keys (Outrun) ships on the Two Pairs algorithm: a 1:1 body with velocity bark and a 1:14
tine that rings for 250 ms.

## D34. The factory wavetables (2026-11-13, phase 3.4)

The plan asks for an embedded factory set: classic, digital, vocal, chip 4-bit.

- **Four tables of 16 frames, computed from their spectra at first use, not stored as audio
  files (taken, recommended).** No third-party tables to license, nothing in the repository but
  code, and every frame is exact. Classic blends sine, triangle, saw and square spectrum by
  spectrum with the fundamental in phase (no thin spot mid-morph); Digital is a hard-synced saw
  whose slave ratio climbs from 1 to 8; Vocal is a saw through the formants of a, e, i, o, u on
  a 110 Hz harmonic grid (the formants move with pitch, as in every wavetable synth); Chip is
  32-step 4-bit waves (pulse widths 1/8 to 1/2, the NES triangle, a stepped saw, four SCC-style
  waves).
- Embedded WAV tables. Richer at once, but they need a source and a licence.
- User table import. Later, with the real UI.

## D35. Band-limited playback (2026-11-13, phase 3.4)

Checked online (KVR, Electric Druid): one band-limited table per octave by FFT, clear, inverse
FFT, 2048 samples, is the common practice; hard switching between octave tables can crackle.

- **11 octave levels per frame; the level is chosen so harmonics stay under 0.75 fs, so what
  folds back lands above fs / 4 and the voice's decimator removes it; a 4-point Hermite read
  (taken, recommended).** At the 96 kHz voice rate a level switch only ever removes harmonics
  above 36 kHz, so switching is inaudible and needs no crossfade. Measured: the largest alias at
  A7 is -118.6 dB.
- Two or four tables per octave, or crossfading levels. More memory or more reads for nothing
  audible here.

## D36. The wavetable generator's parameters (2026-11-13, phase 3.4)

- **Table, Position (crossfading neighbour frames, ramped across each sub-block), Unison 1 to 5,
  Detune, Spread, Phase Rand (taken).**
- **No hard sync input (taken).** A reset without band-limited correction would alias; the VA
  generator does sync properly. Combine FM moves the phase of every copy; ring and AM as usual.
- **The table bank is built in `Part::prepare`, off the audio thread (taken).** Building it the
  first time a slot switches to Wavetable would cost the audio thread about 20 ms once.

## D37. Phase 3.4 presets and test fixes (2026-11-13, phase 3.4)

- **SCC Lead steps its pulse width with the step modulator, eight values that land exactly on
  the chip table's eight pulse-width frames at 1/16 (taken).** An LFO with the Steps shape would
  fall between frames and crossfade. The 4-bit crusher of the recipe waits for 4.2.
- **Nebula Pad layers a vocal stack and a digital stack, each drifting on its own per-voice LFO
  (20 and 27 s a cycle), through the ladder in LP12 until the state-variable filter (4.1) and the
  plate's pre-delay (5.1) arrive (taken).**
- Tests corrected, not the engine: an old bank-error test used "Wavetable" as its example of an
  unknown type; the every-preset-sounds test gives presets whose arp skips steps at random four
  seconds instead of one (Rain Chime once skipped every step of a second); the fuzz test waits
  out each preset's own release instead of a fixed two seconds (Glass Bell releases for 2.5 s).

## D38. The state-variable filter (2026-11-13, phase 4.1)

- **Andy Simper's trapezoidal SVF, 12 dB per octave, LP, HP, band-pass at a peak gain of 1 and
  notch; resonance from Q 0.5 to Q 40, growing exponentially (taken, recommended).** The SVF is
  the clean, precise filter next to the ladder's character: high-passing supersaws, breath and
  wind. At Q 40 it rings long but always decays (tested); self-oscillation stays the ladder's job.
- A self-oscillating SVF (Q to infinity). Duplicates the ladder and needs a level control.
- **A soft limit on the band state, 4 tanh(v / 4), and drive as an input stage like the ladder's
  (taken).** Keeps a fully resonant, fully driven filter bounded (largest output 12 at full-scale
  noise with the cutoff jumping every sub-block) and does nothing at normal levels. A fully
  nonlinear SVF (Oberheim SEM style) was the alternative: more colour, but the clean filter is
  the point of this type.
- 24 dB cascades. The ladder covers four poles.

## D39. Phase 4.1 presets (2026-11-13, phase 4.1)

- **Supersaw's high-pass moves to the SVF at 120 Hz, resonance 0.1, as the recipe says (taken).**
  It also costs less: Supersaw went from 28 % to 20 % of real time.
- **Nebula Pad's soft low-pass moves to the SVF (taken).**
- **Deep Field ships on the ladder, 400 Hz, breathing on a 20-second global LFO (taken).** The
  recipe asks for LP4, which is the ladder.

## D40. The crusher (2026-11-13, phase 4.2)

- **Sample-and-hold at Rate, then rounding to 2^bits levels with the bit count continuous, and a
  dry/wet mix; the top of the Rate knob is no reduction (taken, recommended).** A Bits sweep
  moves through the steps without jumps; the aliasing of the rate reduction is the sound, as on
  every crusher. At full wet the crushed value is output exactly.
- Dither before rounding. Smoother at high bit counts, but crushing is meant to be rough.
- Band-limit the rate reduction. Clean, but not a crusher any more.

## D41. The wavefolder (2026-11-13, phase 4.2)

Checked online: Parker, Zavalishin and Le Bivic (DAFx 2016) introduced antiderivative
anti-aliasing; Esqueda, Pontynen, Parker and Bilbao apply it to the Lockhart and Serge
wavefolders.

- **A sine folder, sin(pi/2 u), with first-order antiderivative anti-aliasing; Drive 0 to 30 dB,
  Symmetry as an offset with a DC blocker after, the dry signal delayed half a sample to match
  (taken, recommended).** Measured on a 4 kHz sine at 30 dB of drive: the worst alias below
  20 kHz -94 dB, against -72 dB for the same folder without it.
- A triangle folder (Buchla style). Brighter, but its corners alias more and its antiderivative
  is piecewise.
- Circuit models of the Lockhart or Serge folders. More character, several times the cost.
- Second-order ADAA or extra oversampling. Cleaner still, at more cost, for aliasing that is
  already below -90 dB.

## D42. A quantized curve in the matrix (2026-11-13, phase 4.2)

Hyper Lead's recipe asks for a sample-and-hold to pitch "quantised to semitones".

- **A sixth curve, Quantized: the slot's whole contribution is rounded at its destination, to
  whole semitones on pitch destinations and to sixteenths of the range elsewhere (taken,
  recommended).** Works for any amount and any source; added at the end of the curve list, so
  saved curves keep their meaning.
- Quantizing the source before the amount. It would only land on semitones for some amounts.
- A scale-aware pitch quantizer per voice. More musical (scales, keys), but a module of its own;
  a later addition if presets need it.

## D43. Basement and Hyper Lead (2026-11-13, phase 4.2)

- **Siren has every generator Off: the ladder sings at full resonance from its own -110 dB noise
  floor, its cutoff dragged by a 0.1 Hz global saw LFO, into an 0.8-feedback echo (taken).**
- **Rust Bass folds per voice, the fold deepening with ENV3 and velocity, then a ladder (taken).**
  This is what the recipe's "FOLD per voice from env" asks; the global drive would smear chords.
- **Concrete Pad: state-variable high-pass at 200 Hz, ladder at 1 kHz, crushed to 12 bits at
  22 kHz (taken).** The lo-fi of the recipe waits for 5.2.
- **New bank file `06-basement.csbank` (taken).** Metal Hit waits for 4.3, Furnace Drone for the
  comb (4.4).

## D44. The per-voice drive (2026-11-13, phase 4.3)

- **A Drive processor with three curves, Soft (tanh), Hard (clipper) and Tube (biased tanh, even
  harmonics), each with first-order antiderivative anti-aliasing; Drive 0 to 36 dB over the
  ladder's headroom (0 dB nearly clean); peak-normalised by the larger of its two peaks; Tone
  and Mix (taken, recommended).** Per voice, so chords do not intermodulate as they do through
  the global drive. Measured on a 4.1 kHz sine at 30 dB: hard curve's worst alias -55 dB, against
  -41 dB for a naive clipper.
- Keep only the global drive effect. Chords smear (the requirement this module answers).
- Oversample the shapers again. Cleaner, at twice the cost, where ADAA already gains 14 dB.
- More curves (fuzz, foldback). The folder covers folding; more can come with presets that ask.

Found by the tests: normalising the Tube curve by its positive peak let its harder negative
side reach 2.1; it is now normalised by the larger peak.

## D45. The combine audit (2026-11-13, phase 4.3)

The plan asks to audit ring and FM combine for level and aliasing.

- **No change (taken).** Measured with VA generators at 96 kHz after the decimator: a 3 kHz sine
  ringed by a 1.37 kHz saw sits at rms 0.40 (the product of two unit waves) with its worst alias
  below 20 kHz at -79.9 dB; the same sine under combine FM at half amount stays at unit level
  with its worst alias at -93.6 dB. Both are now regression tests.
- Band-limit the ring product with extra oversampling. Not needed at these levels.

## D46. Phase 4.3 presets (2026-11-13, phase 4.3)

- **Metal Hit: a saw ringed by a saw at 2.76 times (oct +1, semi +6, fine -42 cents), hard
  driven per voice, decaying in 250 ms (taken).**
- **Neuro Growl moves to 4.4 (taken, recommended).** The plan lists it in 4.3 "formant pending";
  its talking quality is the formant filter, so it ships once, with the formant, instead of
  twice.

## D47. The formant filter (2026-11-13, phase 4.4)

- **Three band-passes in parallel on the first three formants of a male a, e, i, o and u; Vowel
  morphs between neighbours (frequencies in the log domain); Size scales all formants 0.5 to 2;
  Resonance sets Q 3 to 25 with a make-up gain of about sqrt(Q) so the loudness holds; Mix
  (taken, recommended).** Tested: each vowel peaks at its formants (a at 730 and 1090 Hz, i at
  270 and 2290 Hz), Size 2 moves them an octave down, a vowel sweep has no clicks.
- A cascade of peaking filters. Closer to a vocal tract, harder to keep level.
- Five formants. More realistic vowels, five filters per channel for a synth growl that does not
  need them.

## D48. The comb (2026-11-13, phase 4.4)

- **A feedback comb tuned to the note (or C4) plus Tune, a one-pole damping low-pass and a soft
  limit in the loop, positive or negative polarity, a Hermite read with the delay ramped per
  sample, down to 20 Hz, feedback up to 0.98, Mix (taken, recommended).** Tested: echoes every
  note period within a sample, negative polarity alternating in sign, Tune +12 halving the
  period, full feedback bounded.
- A feed-forward comb. A flanger-like notch pattern, not a resonator.
- Allpass interpolation. Flatter in the top octave, but it clicks when the delay is modulated,
  and Furnace Drone sweeps it.

## D49. Phase 4.4 presets (2026-11-13, phase 4.4)

- **Vinyl Choir: two pulse waves with opposed PWM through the formant, its vowel wandering on a
  smooth-random LFO (taken).** The recipe's slow vowel sweep, less predictable than a sine.
- **Furnace Drone: two low saws and dark noise through a resonant comb whose tuning a 0.05 Hz
  global LFO drags over a fifth, then soft drive per voice (taken).** At 30 % of real time for
  an eight-note chord it is the heaviest preset; it is a drone and is played with one or two
  keys.
- **Neuro Growl: a digital wavetable and a sub-octave FM pair through the formant, its vowel on
  a 1/8 synced LFO, tube drive and the SVF; the wheel doubles the talking rate (taken).**

Phase 4 is complete.

## D50. The plate's additions and shimmer (2026-11-13, phase 5.1)

The plan asks for plate pre-delay, input high-pass, modulation trim and a shimmer type. Checked
online: Eventide's ShimmerVerb puts its pitch shifters inside the feedback loop; on the Csound
list a reverb-plus-shifter loop "quickly goes into distortion", and shifting inside the reverb's
own loop was the suggested fix.

- **Pre-delay 0 to 250 ms, Low Cut a one-pole high-pass from 20 Hz (open) to 1 kHz, Modulation
  0 to 1 with 0.5 the paper's excursion, so existing presets sound as before (taken).**
- **Shimmer as an amount, 0 a plain plate, instead of a separate type plus an amount (taken,
  recommended).** One knob does both; a plate with Shimmer 0 is the old plate exactly.
- **The shifter inside the tank's loop: the tank output band-limited to 180 Hz to 5 kHz, an
  octave up through a two-head delay-line shifter (45 ms window, sin-squared crossfades),
  soft-limited and fed back into the tank input (taken, recommended).** Every pass climbs
  another octave; the band limit keeps the doubled signal below Nyquist and the lows from piling
  up; the tank's own decay keeps it from running away. Measured: the octave sits 45 dB higher in
  the tail than without shimmer, and the tail keeps falling at size 0.8 and shimmer 0.8.
- Shimmer as a separate effect after the reverb. It shifts the tail once, a thinner effect.
- A phase-vocoder or granular shifter. Cleaner, several times the cost, and the delay-line
  shifter's slight warble is part of the shimmer sound.
- A choice of interval (fifth, two octaves). Possible later; the octave is the classic.

## D51. Phase 5.1 presets (2026-11-13, phase 5.1)

- **Shimmer Drone: a dark saw and sine under a plate with shimmer 0.6, pre-delay 40 ms and a
  120 Hz low cut (taken).**
- **Satellite Ping: sine blips two octaves up, a third of the eighths at random, into a
  dotted-quarter ping-pong echo at 0.7 feedback and a large plate (taken).** The one Observatory
  preset allowed a fast attack.
- **Nebula Pad gets the recipe's 80 ms pre-delay and an 80 Hz low cut (taken).**

## D52. The lo-fi effect (2026-11-13, phase 5.2)

- **One global stage after the drive and before phaser, chorus, delay and reverb (taken).** The
  recipes put tape before the chorus and the plate; a per-voice tape would cost sixteen times
  as much for no audible gain.
- **Wow at 0.45 Hz with a slow random drift (up to about 14 cents) and flutter at 6.5 Hz (up to
  about 6 cents) on a short delay line; Bandwidth a 2-pole low-pass from 20 kHz to 1.5 kHz; Bits
  and Rate as on the crusher (taken).** Measured: wow and flutter at full spread a 1 kHz sine's
  cycle lengths by 47 cents.
- **Hiss and Crackle follow the signal through an envelope (5 ms attack, 0.4 s release), so
  silence stays silent (taken, recommended).** A constant noise floor is what real tape does,
  but the plugin would hiss with no note playing.
- **A fixed 6 ms delay on the tape path, with the dry signal delayed as much (taken).** A
  partial mix never comb-filters; the price is 6 ms of extra latency while Lo-Fi is on, which the
  host is not told about.

## D53. Phase 5.2 presets (2026-11-13, phase 5.2)

- **Bedroom Cassette Keys, Blanket Pad and Bedroom Bass ship with their recipes and the lo-fi
  (taken).** Blanket Pad's three-cent wobble is a 0.2 Hz global LFO on pitch.
- **Vinyl Choir gets crackle and a narrow top; Concrete Pad a dirty tape (taken).** Tape Flute
  stays without lo-fi, as its recipe has none.

Phase 5.2 brings the bank to 60 presets.

## D54. The pump (2026-11-13, phase 5.3)

- **A ducker at the end of the rack that follows the beat: the host's song position while it
  plays, otherwise its own count at the tempo, restarted at the first note after silence
  (taken, recommended).** In a DAW the duck sits on the grid; in the standalone a chord pumps
  from its first beat. The gain drops by Depth within 2 ms and recovers along a squared curve
  over Release, a share of the division (1/4 by default, the delay's note values).
- A square LFO to the VCA through the matrix. Already possible, but it works per voice and
  leaves the reverb tail unducked; the pump ducks everything after it.
- A real sidechain input. A synth has no kick to listen to.

Last in the chain, after the compressor and the EQ, so nothing behind it evens the duck out.

## D55. The EQ (2026-11-13, phase 5.3)

- **A low shelf at 150 Hz and a high shelf at 6 kHz (each +-12 dB) and a tilt of +-6 dB pivoting
  at 1 kHz, as RBJ biquads after the compressor (taken, recommended).** Three knobs that shape a
  preset's balance; the tilt is a complementary pair of shelves at 1 kHz. Tested against its own
  computed response.
- A three-band parametric. Nine parameters for a finishing EQ.
- Tilt only. Cannot lift the air or trim the lows separately.

## D56. The flanger (2026-11-13, phase 5.3)

- **A 0.3 to 5 ms delay swept by a triangle LFO, the right channel a quarter cycle ahead,
  feedback from -0.95 to 0.95 through a soft limit, Mix 1 as equal parts dry and delayed; between
  phaser and chorus (taken).** Tested: 25 dB between a notch and a peak with the delay held,
  bounded at full feedback on noise.
- No preset uses it yet: no recipe in the bank asks for a flanger. It is there for the
  remaining rooms and for the user.

## D57. Future Chord, played (2026-11-13, phase 5.3)

- **A soft Classic wavetable (between triangle and saw) in four unison voices, a state-variable
  low-pass, pump on the quarter note at depth 0.6, chorus, a bright EQ (taken).** The one-key
  minor seventh needs chord memory (7.1); until then it is played as a chord.

One CPU reading of 154 % for a single block of Future Chord did not repeat in three further
runs (17 to 26 % worst block, 11 to 12 % average); the machine was busy.

Phase 5 is complete: 61 presets.

## D58. The plucked string (2026-11-13, phase 6.1)

Checked online: Jaffe and Smith's extensions of Karplus-Strong tune the loop by its phase delay
at the fundamental and set brightness with a low-pass on the pluck (CCRMA, "Extended
Karplus-Strong Algorithm").

- **A delay line of one period minus the damping low-pass's phase delay at the fundamental, read
  with a 4-point Hermite interpolator and ramped per sample (taken, recommended).** Measured:
  within 1.78 cents from C2 to C7 at light and heavy damping. An allpass fractional delay, the
  paper's choice, has a flatter top octave but clicks when the pitch moves, and this string must
  bend, glide and take vibrato.
- **Decay is the time to fall 60 dB; Damping a one-pole low-pass in the loop (taken).** Measured
  30 dB a second at Decay 2 s.
- **Pick Position as a comb on the output, the same filter as one on the pluck (taken).** No
  second buffer for the excitation; position 0.5 removes the even harmonics (tested).
- **The pluck's brightness is a cutoff counted in harmonics of the note, 1.5 to 40 (taken).**
  Found by the loudness tool: a white-noise pluck put its energy in the top octaves, which die
  within a few loops, leaving a loud tick and a faint string. The click excitation was raised to
  twice full scale for the same reason.
- **Release Damp shortens the decay when the key goes up, from no effect to 30 ms; a retriggered
  voice plucks the ringing string again (taken).** Both need the module to hear about the key:
  see D59.

## D59. Modules may hear note-off and a re-strike (2026-11-13, phase 6.1)

- **Two optional module hooks, `noteOff()` and `strike()`, which a slot calls only on the types
  that have them (taken, recommended).** The voice calls them at release and at retrigger. No
  existing module changes; the string uses both.
- A gate flag in the per-sub-block parameters. Every module would carry a value one uses, and a
  retrigger inside one sub-block would be missed.

## D60. Phase 6.1 presets (2026-11-13, phase 6.1)

- **Kalimba: a heavily damped string an octave up, struck near its middle with the click
  excitation, plus a 5 ms puff of noise for the thumb (taken).**
- **Harp String: a bright pluck a fifth of the way along, ringing for seven seconds while held,
  damped in about 100 ms on release, brighter with velocity (taken).**

Both are held at the tool's peak ceiling, as plucks are.

## D61. The modal generator's resonators (2026-11-13, phase 6.2)

- **Twelve rotating complex resonators, z = (z + x) r e^(j theta) (taken, recommended).** The
  ring is a decaying sine of exactly the set frequency and 60 dB time (measured: every partial
  within 0.01 cent, 30 dB a second at Decay 2 s), and frequency or decay can move every
  sub-block without a click or a jump in level, so the bar bends and takes vibrato. A partial
  that has rung out is cleared and costs nothing.
- A bank of band-pass state-variable filters, as Rings does. The same sound, but the filter's
  peak drifts from its set frequency towards the top of the band and its ring time has to be
  derived from Q per partial.
- Twenty-four or more partials. The requirement allows 8 to 24; beyond the twelfth the partials
  of these materials lie above 20 kHz for most of the keyboard, where they are left out anyway.

## D62. What Wood, Metal and Glass are (2026-11-13, phase 6.2)

Checked online: the free-free bar's modes are 1 : 2.76 : 5.40 : 8.93 (BYU acoustics, "Free-Free
Bar"; University of Colorado PHYS 1240 lab handout); measured ratios of tuned bars and a wine
glass are in the Csound manual's "Modal Frequency Ratios" table (compiled by John Bower for
Scott Lindroth), which also warns that some sets only sound right in their own register.

- **Wood is a tuned (undercut) bar, 1 : 3.93 : 9.54 : 16.7; Metal a uniform bar, 1 : 2.76 :
  5.40 : 8.93; Glass a wine glass, 1 : 2.32 : 4.25 : 6.63 : 9.38 (taken, recommended).** A
  marimba sounds like one because its second partial is tuned two octaves up; a glockenspiel
  bar is close to uniform. Between two materials every ratio moves geometrically, which between
  Wood and Metal is what cutting a bar's arch deeper does. The measured rows give five or six
  ratios; the rest follow their trend and are marked so in the code.
- Wood as the uniform bar and Metal as a plate. My first draft: its "plate" ratios turned out to
  be a drum membrane's (the zeros of Bessel functions), and a uniform bar with wooden damping is
  a wood block, not a marimba.
- One inharmonicity control from a string to a bar, as Rings has. A continuous sweep, but no
  position on it is a tuned bar.
- **Each material also sets how fast upper partials die: a partial at ratio q rings q^-e times
  as long as the fundamental, e = 1.4 for wood, 0.5 for metal, 0.8 for glass, and Damping adds
  up to 1.2 (taken).** Measured 200 ms after a hard strike: the second partial is 38.6 dB under
  the fundamental on wood and 6.9 dB under on metal.

## D63. The mallet (2026-11-13, phase 6.2)

- **Hardness sets the length of one raised-cosine pulse, from half the note's period down to one
  sample, scaled to sum to one (taken, recommended).** The fundamental's level stays within 2 dB
  whatever the hardness (tested) while a hard mallet raises the third partial by more than
  20 dB, so velocity to Hardness reads as the same note struck harder. The length follows the
  note, so a soft mallet is soft on every key.
- A fixed contact time in milliseconds. Closer to a real mallet, but the same setting would be
  dull at the top of the keyboard and bright at the bottom.
- A low-pass on a one-sample strike. The same result with one more filter to tune.
- **A strike excites partial k by sin(k pi position), and by one over the square root of its
  ratio (taken).** Without the tilt a hard strike is white: equal partials up to 20 kHz. With
  it the hardest strike peaks between 1.25 and 2.16 across six octaves and three materials.
- **Release Damp defaults to 0: a bar rings on when the key goes up (taken).** At 1 the key's
  release stops it in 30 ms, for a damped vibraphone.

## D64. Phase 6.2 presets (2026-11-13, phase 6.2)

- **Marimba: Wood, Decay 1.1 s at middle C, falling to about 0.4 s two octaves up and rising to
  about 3 s two octaves down (key to Decay), mallet hardness 0.1 to 0.55 with velocity, and 8 ms
  of band-passed noise at the note's pitch for the knock (taken).** The Csound table's note that
  wooden objects only sound wooden with a noise component is why the knock is there.
- **Dusty Mallet: Metal with Damping 0.7 and a felt mallet, so a round note keeps a glint of
  the bar's chime; lo-fi crackle 0.45, wow, flutter, a narrow top and a small room (taken).**
  The recipe's "kalimba-like" is met by the short, damped metal bar rather than by the string
  generator, which already carries the Greenhouse Kalimba.

Both are held at the tool's peak ceiling, as plucks are. The loudness tool moved three presets
with random elements by half a decibel when the two new ones changed the order of its random
numbers; those three were put back.

## D65. Where chord memory sits and how it keeps track (2026-11-13, phase 7.1)

- **A stage of its own in front of the arpeggiator that rewrites one chunk's events: every key
  remembers the notes it started, and a count per note says how many keys hold it (taken,
  recommended).** A release ends exactly what that key started, even if the chord type changed
  while it was down or chord memory was switched off; a note two keys share is struck again by
  the second and ends with the last. The arpeggiator, the play modes and the allocator are
  untouched: they see keys.
- Expanding inside the allocator. Fewer events, but the arp would not see the chord, which the
  requirement asks for, and mono and unison would each need their own chord handling.
- **The global envelope still follows the keys as played (taken).** The part merges three
  streams in time order (arp steps, chord notes, played keys); a key is tracked just before the
  first event it caused. With chord memory off the order of every call is what it was (the
  existing note-layer tests pass unchanged). Alternative: let the envelope follow the chord's
  notes, which with "every note" and a strum would restart it on every strummed note.
- **A key struck again without a release keeps the notes both chords share and lets the others
  go (taken).** With chord memory off that is one note-on, as before.

## D66. The twelve chord types (2026-11-13, phase 7.1)

Checked online: Plaits' chord table (pichenettes/eurorack, `plaits/dsp/chords/chord_bank.cc`)
is Octave, Fifth, sus4, m, m7, m9, m11, 6/9, M9, M7, M, each as four notes; its alternative
table adds 6th, dominant 7th and diminished chords.

- **Octave, Fifth (root, fifth, octave), Major, Minor, Sus2, Sus4, Major 7, Minor 7,
  Dominant 7, Major 9, Minor 9, Minor 11, in close position with every chord tone (taken,
  recommended).** Plaits' eleven without 6/9, plus Sus2 (the Chord Trigger recipe asks for it)
  and Dominant 7 (the one basic seventh chord its main table lacks). Triads are three notes, not
  four with the octave: the arp's octave range adds octaves, and a chip arpeggio wants three.
- Plaits' table as it is. Its four-note voicings drop the fifth from the ninths and suit an
  oscillator; a keyboard chord memory is expected to play the whole chord.
- More types (6th, 6/9, diminished, augmented). The requirement says twelve; the learned chord
  covers the rest.

## D67. Strum (2026-11-13, phase 7.1)

- **One time, from each note to the next, -200 to 200 ms: positive strums from the bottom up,
  negative from the top down (taken, recommended).** One parameter gives both directions.
  Notes still waiting when the key goes up never sound. Strummed notes reach the arp as they
  arrive.
- A strum time and a separate direction choice (up, down, alternating). One more parameter for
  one more mode.
- A total strum time divided over the notes. A six-note chord would then strum faster per note
  than a triad.

## D68. The learned chord (2026-11-13, phase 7.1)

- **The learned chord is five parameters, `pA.chord.n1` to `n5`: semitones above the played
  key, up to 36, 0 unused (taken, recommended).** Presets and bank files can carry it, the state
  saves it like any parameter, and the host can automate it; nothing extra in the patch format
  (the design note about storing it beside the parameters is superseded).
- A list kept outside the parameters. No limit on size, but a second kind of state to save,
  validate and undo.
- **Learning is a switch, `pA.chord.learn` (taken).** While it is on, keys play plain notes and
  are collected on the audio thread; when the last key goes up the lowest becomes the root, up
  to five others within three octaves become the intervals, and the message thread writes them,
  selects Learned and switches Learn off (through the processor's existing async bridge). The
  real UI can drive the same switch. Alternative: learning only as an editor function, which
  the throwaway UI does not have.

## D69. Phase 7.1 presets (2026-11-13, phase 7.1)

- **Chip Chord: a square wave, mono, Major, arp up at 1/32 with gate 1 (taken).**
- **Chord Trigger: Sus2 through the arp up and down over two octaves in eighths, a plucked saw
  and pulse under a ladder, ping-pong dotted-eighth echo (taken).** Its own level was raised
  (generator levels, a little sustain) after the tool wanted more than the master's +6 dB.
- **Future Chord gets `chord = type=minor7`: one key now plays what the recipe describes
  (taken).**
- **The loudness tool plays one key instead of its four-note chord when chord memory is on
  (taken).** Otherwise it would measure sixteen notes and turn the preset down too far.

The tool again moved four presets with random elements by up to 1.5 dB (Rain Chime, Random
Walk, Furnace Drone, Riser); they were put back, as in phase 6.2.

## D70. Two parts and their voices (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

- **The engine owns two parts, each with its own sixteen voices, and hands out the shared
  budget of sixteen as limits (taken, recommended).** With both parts playing, each may use what
  its patch asks for (its polyphony, one voice for mono and legato, its unison count), is sure
  of eight, and gets more when the other asks for fewer: 16 and 16 become 8 and 8; a poly part
  over a mono sub gets 15 and 1. One part alone keeps all sixteen. The part and its allocator
  are unchanged, and a part the mode leaves out is not rendered once it has rung out.
- One pool of sixteen voices that both parts allocate from. What the design sketches, and no
  second set of voices in memory, but the allocator, the voice state and every play mode would
  have to learn about a second owner; the sound is the same.
- A fixed 8 and 8. Wastes seven voices under a mono sub layer.
- A cap parameter per part. One more setting; the part's own Voices setting already says what it
  wants.

## D71. Part modes, ranges and transpose (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

- **`part.mode`: A, Layer, Split, B. Layer plays every key on both parts; Split plays a key on
  each part whose key range holds it (ranges may overlap); a velocity range applies whenever both
  play; one part alone ignores both ranges (taken, recommended).** The ranges default to a split
  at middle C, so switching to Split does something at once.
- Key ranges also in Layer. Then Layer and Split would be the same mode.
- **Transpose moves the keys a part is sent (taken).** The engine remembers the note it sent for
  every key, so a release ends that note even if Transpose, the ranges or the mode changed while
  the key was down. Fine detune is added to the part's pitch. Alternative: transpose as pitch,
  which would leave key tracking and key ranges on the played key.
- **A part the mode switches off lets go of its notes and rings out; switching it on again does
  not start the keys already held (taken).**
- **Everything that is not a note (bend, wheel, pedal) reaches both parts (taken).**

## D72. Part mix, and no insert slots yet (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

The requirement asks for three insert slots per part. Effects are not slot types yet: the
effect-slot rebuild planned for phase 5 was put off there, and the chain is still the fixed rack.

- **Each part gets a level (-60 dB is off), a balance and an effects share: that share of the
  part passes through the rack and the rest joins after it (taken, recommended).** It covers
  what the bank needs from inserts today, a sub layer that stays out of the chorus, delay and
  plate, with one parameter. Part gains move across the block, so a change never steps.
- Build effect slots now (inserts and master slots). The real answer to the requirement, but it
  means rewrapping ten effects as modules and renaming every effect parameter in 67 presets; it
  belongs with the effect-slot work, which is still open. **Review: this is the one part of the
  7.2 plan line that is not delivered as written.**
- No per-part routing. The Supersaw's sub would sit in the plate.
- **Pan is a balance: the far side fades, the near side stays at unity (taken).** A
  constant-power law would raise a panned part by 3 dB into the master's clipper.

## D73. One arpeggiator setting, a switch per part (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

- **The arp's parameters stay where they are and drive both parts; `pA.mix.arp` and
  `pB.mix.arp` say whether a part follows (taken, recommended).** A split can keep a bass under
  an arpeggiated right hand.
- An arpeggiator per part with its own parameters. More flexible, and 17 more parameters per
  part that no preset needs yet.

## D74. Part B in parameters, bank files and the audition UI (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

- **Part B is the same parameter group under `pB.`, with `pA.mix.*`, `pB.mix.*` and `part.mode`
  added: 851 part parameters in all (taken).** State saves both parts as it saves everything.
  State written before today is rejected, as every earlier V3 state is.
- **Bank files write part B with a `b.` prefix on the same lines (`b.g1`, `b.env1`, `b.mod`,
  `b.mix`), `mix =` for a part's mix and `parts = layer` for the mode (taken, recommended).**
  Each part resolves modulation destinations against its own slot types. Patch-wide lines with a
  prefix are errors. Alternative: a second `[Name:B]` section, which would duplicate the header
  and scatter one preset over two places.
- **No part selector in the audition UI (taken).** The generic editor already shows Parts,
  Part A and Part B as groups; a selector belongs to the real UI in phase 9.
- **Chord learning works per part (taken).**

## D75. The layered presets (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

- **The three presets are upgraded in place; the bank stays on its way to 68 (taken,
  recommended).** Alternative: add "layered" copies beside the originals, which would take the
  bank past the 68 the plan fixes.
- **Supersaw: part B is one sine an octave under the lowest key held (legato, low priority,
  25 ms glide), 5 dB down, effects share 0 (taken).**
- **Juno Pad: two Juno patches stacked as a Jupiter's Dual mode would, part A left, part B
  right, seven cents sharp with more saw and its own slower LFO (taken).**
- **Deep Field: part B is three triangles an octave up through a drifting band-pass, arriving
  six seconds into the note (taken).** The loudness tool's render ends before part B has risen,
  so the preset is a little louder at full bloom than the tool measured.

The tool moved the four presets with random elements again (Rain Chime 6 to 5, Random Walk 6 to
5.5, Furnace Drone -5 to -4.5, Riser -1.5 to -2); a second part changes the order of the random
numbers. The same readings came back three runs in a row, so this time they are kept.

## D76. Preset changes and state loads with 900 parameters (2026-11-13, phase 7.2)

Found by a timing test after the parameter count doubled: a preset change took 45 ms and a state
load 125 ms.

- **A preset sets only the parameters whose value changes; a program change is one bulk edit
  without an undo snapshot per parameter; the state check looks IDs up in a map (taken).**
  Measured: preset 0.7 ms, state load 9.5 ms, and the test suite runs faster than before parts.
- Leave it. Audible as a pause when browsing presets, and it grows with the square of the
  parameter count.

## D77. The factory samples are made by the program (2026-11-13, phase 8)

- **Five samples generated at first use: Glass (a bowed wine glass), Choir, Strings, Air and
  Harp; each four seconds of mono at 48 kHz, tuned to C4, levelled alike, closed into a
  seamless loop, and kept at three band limits (20, 10 and 5 kHz) (taken, recommended).**
  Nothing to record or license, nothing added to the installer; building them takes a few tens
  of milliseconds once per process and 11.5 MB of memory shared by every instance. The loop is
  made by folding the half second after the end into the start, so a loop over the whole sample
  and a grain crossing the end never click. The band levels let a sample play up to four
  octaves above C4 without audible aliasing.
- Recordings stored in the binary. The usual meaning of "factory samples", but there are no
  recordings to use: they would have to be made or bought, and cleared.
- No factory set; wait for user import. Then the grain generator has nothing to play and the
  bank cannot be completed.
- Glass follows what a rubbed glass does (checked online: a singing glass is driven by stick and
  slip like a bowed string, Jundt et al., "Vibrational modes of partly filled wine glasses",
  UNSW; its free modes are 1 : 2.32 : 4.25, Csound modal table): a nearly pure sustained tone
  with weak harmonics, a slow beating as the pattern follows the finger, a faint ring of the
  free modes and a breath of friction noise.

## D78. The sample generator (2026-11-13, phase 8)

- **Sample, Start, Loop, Loop Start, Loop End, Loop Fade, Reverse (taken).** C4 is the sample's
  own speed; the slot's key tracking switch gives "pitch tracking off". A loop fades what lies
  before Loop Start in under the last part of the loop (up to half of it), equal power; measured
  on a 0.2 s loop of Glass, the largest step is 0.56 without the fade and 0.0106 with it, which
  is the sample's own largest step. A one-shot fades out over its last 10 ms. The read blends
  two band levels by how far the note transposes the sample.
- A fade that needs room before the loop start in the file. Not needed: the samples are loops,
  so there is always something before any point.
- User samples. The requirement puts import later; it needs a file dialog, a place in the state
  and a loader off the audio thread.

## D79. The grain generator (2026-11-13, phase 8)

Checked online: grain clouds are levelled by a gain that compensates the overlap of the grains
and their window (Riedel et al., "Perceptual evaluation of listener envelopment using spatial
granular synthesis", arXiv 2301.10210), commonly the square root of the number of active grains;
"spray" is the usual name for a random offset of each grain's read position.

- **Up to twelve grains per voice from a factory sample; Position, Scan, Size, Density, Spray,
  Pitch Spray, Spread (taken, recommended).** The level follows one over the root of the
  overlap (measured: 0.093, 0.121 and 0.149 RMS for overlaps of 1, 5 and 12). The window is
  (4x(1-x)) squared, within a few percent of a Hann window at three multiplications a sample.
  Spray scatters the read position up to a second either way and loosens the timing by up to
  half an interval; at 0 the grains fall on a grid, which is a sound of its own.
- **Freeze is Scan at 0 (taken).** The eight parameters are used up; Scan runs from -2 to 2
  times the sample's speed and its middle is the frozen cloud.
- **No grains from another generator yet. Review: the requirement names it.** Each voice would
  need about a second of memory per generator slot to record into, and slots are sized for
  their largest type: several megabytes per voice across 32 voices. It needs a buffer pool the
  part hands out, which is a piece of work of its own.
- More grains per voice. Twelve already cost 14 % of real time for eight notes in this build;
  thicker clouds come from longer grains.

## D80. Granular Sky (2026-11-13, phase 8)

- **A frozen cloud on Glass (40 grains a second of 250 ms, Pitch Spray at 5 cents, wide), a
  thinner cloud an octave up that drifts through the sample, and the sample itself quietly
  underneath, through a low-pass into the long plate (taken).** The recipe asks for "density
  high": 40 a second of this length already keeps ten grains sounding, near the limit of twelve.
  The bank is complete at 68 presets.

# Decisions taken with the user

From here on the entries record choices the user made from a pick list, with the options that
were offered, so the reasons stay with the code.

## D81. Effects per part: three insert slots, the rack stays as the master (2026-11-13)

Review of D72. Offered:

- **Three insert slots per part, each holding one effect type; the fixed rack stays as the
  master section (chosen by the user; recommended).** Each part can have its own effects. All
  presets keep working; the effects share stays as the send into the master rack.
- Rebuild everything as slots (inserts and six master slots). Free order and the design as
  written, but every effect parameter is renamed and all preset effect lines rewritten.
- Leave the effects share as the only per-part control.

## D82. Live granulation becomes an effect on a part (2026-11-13)

Review of D79. Offered:

- Leave it out for now (recommended): nothing in the bank needs it and it can be added later.
- **A granular effect on a whole part: one recording buffer per part and a Freeze switch
  (chosen by the user).** It freezes and smears whatever the part plays, chords included;
  grains are not per note. It becomes an insert type, so it follows D81.
- Per-voice input, as the requirement words it: a buffer pool for voices, and Position and Scan
  meaning something else for live input.

## D83. Factory samples: recorded instruments, best quality, in files beside the plugin (2026-11-13)

Review of D77. Offered for the factory set: keep the generated samples and judge by ear
(recommended), **add real recordings (chosen by the user)**, or build user import now. Offered
for how far the sample side goes: single recordings as raw material (recommended), **compact
multisamples (chosen)**, or full sample instruments.

The user then set two rules that change the "compact" part of that choice:

- **"Quality is paramount. I must have the best possible quality."** So the instruments are not
  reduced to mono, short loops and a few zones; they keep what the recordings offer.
- **"Hundreds of MB of external data is ok. We'll give install options."** So the audio lives in
  files beside the plugin, not in the binary, and the installer can offer instruments to choose.

Sources checked online: VSCO 2 Community Edition and VCSL are both CC0 (each repository's
licence file; github.com/sgossner). The University of Iowa samples may not be redistributed
as-is and are not used. Both libraries are downloaded in full into `/audition` (ignored by
git) for the user to listen to; which instruments ship, and in what format, are the user's
picks after listening. The generated samples stay until then.

## D84. No part selector in the audition UI (2026-11-13)

Review of D74. Offered: **leave the generic list as it is (chosen by the user; recommended)**,
move the Part B group up under Part A, or rewrite the list with an A/B switch. The UI is
thrown away in phase 9.

## D85. Reference recordings: real instruments and classic synths (2026-11-13)

Offered: real instruments only (recommended), **instruments and synths (chosen by the user)**,
or no measuring. The physical-model presets are compared with CC0 recordings of the real
instruments (partials, decay times) and retuned where clearly off, each change logged. Classic
synth recordings are compared too, but numbers say little about evolving timbres, so those
findings are suggestions for the user's ears. Copyrighted material stays in `/audition` and is
never built in.

## D86. Order of work (2026-11-13)

Offered: **samples and the audition page, then the insert slots, then the granular effect
(chosen by the user; recommended)**; samples only and then stop; or inserts first.

## D87. The repository moved to `E:\github\CoolSynth` (2026-11-13)

On the user's instruction: drive C: had 9 GB free, E: has 483 GB. Everything except the build
folder was copied and verified (same commit, clean status, 5,558 files); the build was made
again from scratch at the new place. The project memory was copied to the folder a session in
the new location reads.

## D88. A Museum room of named classic synth sounds (2026-11-13)

The user's idea: a category apart from the Places with recreations of famous presets of real
synths, admitted only when exact or nearly exact. Offered:

- **A small room, "The Museum": 8 to 12 famous sounds, each built from the original's published
  patch settings where they exist, checked against a reference recording, and admitted only
  after the user accepts it in a side-by-side on the audition page (chosen by the user;
  recommended).** The bank grows past 68. Brand names in preset names are fine for private use
  and would need renaming if the synth is published. The presets are built after the insert
  slots, because a classic often needs its own chorus or delay.
- A large classics bank across many synths. Many entries could only be approximate
  (six-operator FM, sample-based and wavetable-specific synths).
- No new category; correct the four homages in Studio 1982 (Juno Pad, Prophet Brass, OB
  Strings, Moog Bass) against published settings and recordings.
- Not now.

## D89. The audition page (2026-11-13)

- **Previews are made from the raw recordings and nothing else: every recorded pitch of a middle
  dynamic for 1.3 s each, one long note, and one pitch at every dynamic; each file is only
  levelled (taken).** The user judges the recordings, not my processing. 148 instrument
  folders in 12 families; releases, legacy sets and unpitched percussion are left off the page.
- The page lives in `/audition` with the libraries (15 GB, ignored by git) and is served by a
  small local server (`.claude/launch.json`, name `audition`, port 8765), because the app shows
  local files as snapshots without sound. Picks are ticked on the page and pasted into the chat.

## D90. The modal materials, corrected from recordings (2026-11-13)

Follows D85 and replaces the numbers of D62. Measured on the CC0 recordings in `/audition`
with `tools/audition/measure_struck.py` (pitch, partial ratios, 60 dB time per partial):

| Instrument | Partials (ratio to the fundamental) | Fundamental's 60 dB time | Upper partials |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Marimba (VCSL) | 1 : 4.00 to 4.02 : 10.09 to 10.15 : 18.8 to 19.4 : 30.7 | 8.7 s at C3, 3.5 s at F4, 2.2 s at C5, 0.8 s at F6 | 4x lasts about a quarter as long, 10x about a tenth |
| Xylophone (VCSL) | 1 : 3.0 : 6.6 to 6.9 | 2.1 to 2.9 s at G4, 0.6 s at C7 | |
| Glockenspiel (VCSL) | 1 : 2.9 to 3.0 : 5.4 to 5.7 : 8.9 to 9.0 | 8 to 12 s at G5, 4 s at G7 | 1 to 1.9 s |
| Vibraphone (VCSL) | 1 : 4.00 : 10.4 to 10.7 | 8 to 20 s | 4x lasts about a quarter as long |
| Kalimba (VCSL, two) | 1 : 6.4 to 6.9 : 17.8 to 18 | 2 to 6 s in the middle | |

Checked online: marimba bars are tuned with the second partial at 4:1 and the third at 10:1
(Percussion Clinic Adelaide, "building a marimba").

- **Wood becomes 1 : 4 : 10.1 : 19.1 : 30.7 (taken, recommended).** D62's ratios (3.93, 9.54,
  from the Csound table's "xylophone" row) put the second partial 34 cents and the third 99
  cents flat of a real marimba's.
- **A partial at ratio q now lasts q^-1 as long for wood, glass and tine and q^-1.2 for metal
  (taken).** Measured: a bar rings for about as many cycles whatever the partial. D62 had 1.4
  for wood (upper partials gone three times too fast) and 0.5 for metal (ringing far longer
  than a real glockenspiel's or vibraphone's).
- **A fourth material, Tine: a bar clamped at one end, 1 : 6.27 : 17.5 : 34.4 (taken).** A
  kalimba's overtones are those of its tines; no setting of a string model produces them.
  Material now runs 0 to 3. Alternative: leave the kalimba to the string generator, as D60 did.
- Metal keeps the theoretical uniform bar (2.76, 5.40, 8.93). The one glockenspiel measured
  sits a little above it (2.9 to 3.0), which is that instrument, not the rule.
- The generated Glass sample's harmonics drop to 30 to 40 dB under the fundamental, as measured
  on the recorded wine glasses (D77 had them at 18 and 26 dB).

## D91. Presets corrected from recordings (2026-11-13)

Each is on the audition page three times, same notes, no effects: the recording, the preset
before, the preset now. The user's verdict there decides whether the change stays.

- **Marimba: Decay 4 s at middle C (was 1.1 s), halving per octave up (key to Decay -0.7, was
  -0.5), Damping 0 (was 0.3); the amp envelope only bounds the voice (16 s) (taken).** The
  recording rings 3.5 s at F4 and its 60 dB time times its pitch is nearly constant. The bank's
  recipe says "short, D400"; that was written before there was a recording to measure, and it
  describes how a marimba is heard in a mix, where the tail is masked.
- **Kalimba: the modal generator on Tine, Decay 3 s, a soft pluck (was a damped string an
  octave up) (taken).** The thumb's noise puff stays.
- **Harp String: Pick Position 0.45 (was 0.2), Decay 5 s at middle C and longer in the bass (key
  to Decay -0.4; was 7 s everywhere) (taken).** The recorded harp's second harmonic lies 15 to
  29 dB under the fundamental and its third only 2 to 15 dB under: a string plucked near its
  middle. Its 60 dB times run from 15 to 19 s in the bass to about 4.5 s in the middle.
- **Dusty Mallet: Damping 0.12 instead of 0.7 (taken).** It keeps the upper partials' decay it
  had; the number changes because metal's own exponent rose from 0.5 to 1.2.

Kalimba and Marimba came out 2.5 and 1 dB louder and were levelled again; Rain Chime's master
was put back after the tool's usual random wobble.

## D92. A render tool and the model check (2026-11-13)

- **`CoolSynthRender` renders a preset playing given notes to a WAV file through the real
  plugin, with an option to switch all effects off; `tools/audition` holds the scripts that
  build the previews, the measurements, the three-way comparisons and the page (taken).** It is
  what puts every claim about a sound in front of the user's ears, and the Museum room (D88)
  needs the same side-by-side. The scripts read the libraries in `/audition` and write only
  there.

## D93. How the insert slots are built (2026-11-13)

Carries out D81. Choices inside the build:

- **Each insert slot owns a rack of its own and switches on only the stage its type names
  (taken, recommended).** An insert is the same code as the same effect in the master section,
  with the same smoothing: a test renders each type once as an insert and once in the master
  rack and finds them equal to 0.00001. Cost: six more racks in memory (a 4 s delay line and a
  plate each), 7 ms of preparation instead of 2; an empty slot costs nothing while playing.
- Rewrap each effect as a slot module with its own arena. The form voice modules have, but ten
  effects to rewrite and a second copy of their smoothing logic to keep in step.
- **All ten rack effects can be inserts: Drive, LoFi, Phaser, Flanger, Chorus, Delay, Reverb,
  Compressor, EQ, Pump (taken).** A pump on one part and not the other is the main reason for
  the last one.
- **Insert parameters are slot parameters: `pA.i1.type`, `pA.i1.p1` to `p8`, named and ranged by
  the type; bank files write `i1 = Delay time=375 mix=0.25` and `b.i2 = Chorus ...` (taken).**
- **Both parts are rendered before either goes through its inserts (taken).** The beat that
  synced delays and pumps follow starts again with the first note after silence, and that is
  known only once the voices have run. The master pump now takes its phase from the same beat
  count; changing its division while it runs free makes it jump to that division's phase.
- An insert's tail is cut when its slot is emptied or its type changes, and when the part mode
