  leaves the part out and its voices have rung out.

## D94. The user's verdicts on the model check (2026-11-13)

- **Marimba: the new one is closer. Kalimba: the new one is closer.** D90 and D91 stand.
- **Harp String: neither is close.** The correction of D91 is not enough; the preset stays as
  corrected until a better one is found and goes back to the audition page.

## D95. The user's sample picks (2026-11-13)

74 instrument folders ticked on the audition page, 2,368 files, 4.1 GB as recorded
(`tools/audition/picks.txt`): the Steinway B grand and two uprights, two harpsichords, folk
harp, dan tranh, bowed strings in ten sustained and short articulations, brass, six organ
stops, recorders, ocarinas, saxophones and other winds, the mallet instruments and bells, a
kalimba, the wine glasses, three TX81Z sounds and three timpani. How they are stored, loaded
and installed is the next decision; D83 already allows files beside the plugin.

## D96. How the recordings are kept, loaded and stored (2026-11-13)

Three pick lists, after the picks of D95 and under the rules of D83 (quality first; files
beside the plugin). The user's choices:

- What is kept of each instrument: **everything (chosen, recommended)**: every recorded pitch,
  every dynamic layer, every take, at full length, in stereo. 2,368 recordings, 4.1 GB as
  recorded. Offered instead: a compact set (fewer pitches, one or two layers, looped short),
  smaller and audibly less than the recordings.
- How a patch gets its audio: **loaded into memory (chosen, recommended)** by a background
  thread when a patch names an instrument; playing never waits for the disk. Costs memory while
  the instrument is in use (a grand piano is about 600 MB as 32-bit floats; the whole library
  6.5 GB, never all at once). Offered instead: streaming from disk while playing (little
  memory, but a disk thread per voice and a risk of dropouts on a slow disk).
- The file format: **FLAC, lossless (chosen, recommended)**: bit for bit the recordings at
  about a third of the size (1.4 GB for the whole library), in packs by family so an installer
  can offer them one by one. Offered instead: WAV as recorded (4.1 GB) or a lossy format
  (smaller again, no longer the recordings).

## D97. The Conservatory: family, instrument, style (2026-11-13)

74 picks are too many for one menu, and many are the same instrument played another way.

- **A room of plain presets, "Conservatory", one per instrument (chosen by the user,
  recommended).** Offered instead: no presets (the recordings only as a source in the Sample
  generator), or designed presets that dress the recordings up.
- **Family, instrument, style (chosen by the user, recommended).** Seven families (Keys,
  Strings, Plucked, Brass, Winds, Organs, Mallets and Bells), 40 instruments, and the playing
  style (sustain, tremolo, pizzicato; a mallet; an organ stop) as a switch inside the
  instrument, at most four positions. The preset menu shows the room, then the family, then
  the instrument. Offered instead: one preset per pick (74 in a flat list), or one preset per
  family with the instrument as a switch (hides what the room holds).
- `resources/instruments/catalogue.txt` is the list: family, instrument, style, whether the
  style sustains, and the folder it is built from. An instrument's number and a style's number
  are saved in patches, so lines are only ever added.

## D98. How the sampled instruments are built (2026-11-13)

Carries out D96 and D97. Choices inside the build:

- **The Sample generator plays them: its Sample parameter lists the five generated samples and
  then every instrument, and an eighth parameter is the Style (taken, recommended).** One
  generator, so a recorded instrument can be mixed, filtered and modulated like any other
  source, three to a voice. A separate "Instrument" generator type would have doubled the code
  for the same job.
- **A note takes the recording nearest its pitch (the lower of two equally near), the dynamic
  layer its velocity falls in, and the next take in turn; it is read at the speed that gives
  the note's pitch (taken).** No crossfade between neighbouring recordings or layers: two
  recordings of one note are not in phase, and mixing them thins the tone.
- **Reading is a 16-point windowed sinc with 256 phases, interpolated, at three cutoffs for
  notes played above their recording (taken, recommended).** The four-point Hermite read the
  generated samples use is cheaper and audibly duller and less clean on a piano two octaves
  up; quality comes first (the user's rule).
- **Sustaining styles get a loop from the builder (taken): over the steady part of each
  recording, crossfaded for up to half a second, its end moved to where the wave best matches
  the start.** Checked on 9 s notes of cello and organ: no step in level, no click. Struck and
  plucked styles ring out as recorded. Hand-set loops would be better still and are 900 files
  of work; no loops would end a held string note after a few seconds.
- **One gain per style, applied as it is read, brings the style's loudest recording to 0.9 of
  full scale (taken).** The balance between an instrument's notes and dynamics stays as
  recorded. Normalising every file would flatten that balance; no gain at all leaves the pipe
  organ 27 dB below the piano. Whether notes should be evened out across the keyboard is open:
  see D99.
- **The library is looked for in the folder named in `COOLSYNTH_LIBRARY`, else the one written
  in `CoolSynth/library.path` in the user's application data, else `CoolSynth/Library` in the
  common application data (taken).** An installer writes the second; the first is for testing.
- **Memory: at most 3 GB of instruments; one nothing has asked for in a minute and a half goes
  first (taken).** A note asked for before its instrument is in memory is silent, never half an
  instrument; the next note sounds.
- **A name need only be unique within its room (taken for now; the user is asked).** The
  Greenhouse has a modelled Marimba and Kalimba, the Conservatory the recorded ones. Program
  names carry the room ("Conservatory: Mallets and Bells: Marimba"), and the render tool takes
  "Room: Name". The options: this; rename the models ("Marimba Bar", "Kalimba Tine", as "Harp
  String" already is); or rename the recordings.
- **The loudness tool plays a sampled instrument in the middle of the notes it was recorded
  at (taken).** At the tool's usual C4 chord a flute is below its lowest note and a double bass
  at its top.
- **Tests never read the real library (taken).** A stand-in is written to a temporary folder:
  every style of the catalogue as five quarter-second sine recordings with two layers, two
  takes and a loop. One small FLAC file in `tests/data` checks the FLAC path.

## D99. Faults found in the library as first built (2026-11-13)

The first build places each recording by the note in its file name, moved by whole octaves to
where a pitch detector hears it. Checked afterwards against the mapping files the library's
author publishes (github.com/sgossner/VSCO-2-CE, branch SFZ) and by measuring partials:

- The horn sits an octave too high (the author: `MOHorn_sus_A0` is A1; the detector caught
  the second harmonic of most notes). Tubular Bells A sits two octaves too high and B probably
  one (a bell's strike note is not its lowest partial). The Renaissance organ's 4' stop is
  placed by its sound, an octave above its key, and so no longer sounds an octave up.
- The timpani files are numbered drums, not named notes, and the detector spread each drum's
  hits over several wrong notes. The drums are also between notes as recorded (the first
  set: F#2, B2 -21 cents, C#3 +48, E3 +44, G3 -42).
- The didgeridoo folder is three drones, five phrases and four calls, not notes.
- A flute's low C is 20 dB below its top C as recorded, and neighbouring recordings differ by
  up to 3 dB. The author's own mapping evens levels out by hand, per recording.

To be corrected in the builder next: the octave given per style where a detector cannot be
trusted; drums and unnamed sets grouped and placed by their measured main partial; a tuning
correction per recording for sets that are not in tune; and, the user's choice, whether levels
are evened out across the keyboard.

## D100. The library is built from the author's own mapping files (2026-11-13)

Correcting the faults of D99 turned up what makes them moot: the recordings' author publishes
his instruments as SFZ mapping files, 258 of them (github.com/sgossner/VCSL branch `sfz`,
github.com/sgossner/VSCO-2-CE branch `SFZ`; `tools/audition/fetch_sfz.py` downloads them). For
each recording they give the note it sounds, the keys and the velocities that play it, a level
correction and often a tuning correction, and for each instrument a release time and whether
loudness follows velocity. A note in the Steinway's folder says its dynamic layers are
levelled alike and need a velocity curve to get their dynamics back. The first build knew
none of this: it guessed notes from file names and a pitch detector, split velocities evenly,
and played level-matched layers through an envelope that gave them 6 dB of dynamics.

- **The builder takes notes, keys, velocities and levels from the author's mapping files
  (taken, recommended).** 74 of the 76 styles have one. Every fault of D99 goes with it: the
  horn is A1 to F5, the tubular bells C4 to F5, the timpani are five drums on five notes.
  Keeping my own mapping and correcting it by hand where it was found wrong would leave the
  faults nobody found. The Old Trombone has no mapping file and is still placed by its file
  names, its layers splitting the velocities evenly and its recordings levelled alike.
- **The Sample generator plays a sampled instrument with the law those levels are made for:
  level = velocity squared, as far as the instrument follows velocity at all (not at all for
  the organs and harpsichords) (taken).** The Conservatory presets switch the amplifier
  envelope's own velocity off. The alternative, the envelope's straight-line law, gives a
  piano whose softest note is 6 dB below its loudest, or needs every preset to undo the
  author's levels.
- **A zone is the keys and velocities the mapping gives a recording; a note outside every zone
  takes the nearest recorded note (taken).** Where the author crossfades two dynamic layers
  (marimba, folk harp, glockenspiel, the second timpani) the layers switch at the middle of
  the fade: D98 stands, two recordings of a note are not mixed.
- **Tuning is measured here, not taken from the mapping files (taken).** Their corrections
  come from a tuner that is steady on wind and string tones and wanders on drums and bars:
  six hits of one kettledrum get corrections from -12 to +78 cents, while its main partial is
  steady within 2. Measured here per recording from the first eight harmonics (winds,
  strings, brass, organs: within 1 to 6 cents of the author's values where he gives any), from
  the first partial alone (bars, tines, glasses: xylophone and glockenspiel are 15 cents
  sharp as recorded, the kalimba up to 50 off its notes), and per drum from its main partial.
  Left as recorded: the pianos and harpsichords (stretched on purpose), the tubular bells (a
  bell has no partial at its note) and the three TX81Z patches (exact as they are). Taking
  the author's corrections is a builder option (`tune=author`) and is not used.
- **An instrument sounds the key that is played, also where the author maps it otherwise
  (taken; the user is asked).** His mappings put the xylophone an octave and the
  glockenspiels an octave below where they sound, as they are written; the TX81Z electric
  piano sounds an octave below its key and the Clavisynth an octave above (his note says so);
  the tubular glockenspiel's file names are not its pitches. All are moved to where they
  sound (`shift=` in the catalogue). The other option: leave them as the author mapped them,
  as a player of the written part expects.
- **The didgeridoo is three styles, not the author's kit (taken).** His mapping puts its twelve
  recordings on twelve keys. Here the three drones are one pitched, looped instrument on C#2,
  and the phrases and the calls are styles of their own.
- **The second timpani set is the author's "Scale" mapping (taken):** 54 of its 204 hits,
  chosen to make a scale. That mapping is hand-made and not consistent with itself (one
  drum's two layers are given pitches 135 cents apart), so each drum's pitch is measured and
  the keys stay his. Four of its nine drum tunings differ from his by more than 40 cents;
  the user's ear decides.
- The release of each Conservatory preset is the author's (0.4 s for the grand, 0.8 s for
  bowed strings, 15 to 30 s for mallets, bells, harp and timpani, which ring on after the key
  as the instruments do; the envelope allows 20 s), and its attack is 2 ms: the recording
  brings its own.
- **Levels (taken).** Recordings levelled alike by the author do not peak alike, so a style's
  gain puts the middle one of its loudest notes at half of full scale instead of its single
  highest peak at 0.9 (which left whole instruments 10 dB down). A preset has one level and
  an instrument several styles, so each style after the first carries a trim, measured by the
  loudness tool (`CoolSynthPresetNormalizer --styles`, written to the catalogue by
  `apply_trims.py`): -4.4 dB for the bowed vibraphone, +5 dB for the lower recorders; after
  it every style needs the same preset level within 0.1 dB. The loudness tool leaves a
  sampled preset room for fortissimo: its test chord at velocity 100 may peak at -6.2 dB, so
  the same chord at full velocity stays under the output's soft clipper. At a normal touch
  the pianos, mallets and harp therefore sit about 10 dB under the synth presets, bowed and
  blown instruments 3 to 6 dB under (the user is asked whether that is wanted).
- The demonstrations on the audition page (`make_demos.py`) are measured by `check_demos.py`:
  every style gets louder with velocity (26 dB from velocity 45 to 120 on the grand), notes
  are in tune within 5 cents in the middle of each style, and no file peaks above -1.5 dB.
- The library after the rebuild: 2,216 zones in 76 styles, 2,213 files, 1,393 MB;
  `verify_library.py` compared 2,213 files with their recordings, none different.

## D101. Harp String, second try: a finger's pluck (2026-11-13)

The user found neither version of Harp String close to the recorded harp (D94). Measured
harmonic by harmonic against the same recording (VCSL Concert Harp, nine notes A2 to E5), the
reason is plain: the recorded harp is dark, mostly its fundamental and third harmonic, its
sixth to tenth harmonics 30 to 80 dB down, with almost nothing above the twelfth even in the
attack. The preset's pluck was a burst of low-passed noise: at its brightness setting the
harmonics up to the tenth were within 20 dB of the strongest, different on every note, and
nearly all of the first 30 ms lay above the twelfth harmonic. The pluck position and decay
corrected in D91 were not what was wrong.

- **Harp String uses the string generator's Click excitation, a smooth pulse, made wider:
  half a period at Pick 0, a twentieth at Pick 1, at equal energy (taken, recommended).** A
  raised-cosine pulse a quarter to half a period wide has the recorded harp's fall of
  harmonics. No preset used Click, so nothing else changes; the pulse was at most a quarter
  period wide before.
- Keep the noise pluck and give it a steeper filter: it would change what Pick means for every
  string preset, and a noise pluck is still different on every note, which a finger is not.
- Drop the model and point the preset at the recorded Folk Harp: the Greenhouse is the room
  of modelled instruments, and the Conservatory already has the recording.
- The preset: Pick 0.3 at middle C, falling with the key (the recording's pluck is softer the
  higher the string), rising a little with velocity; Pick Position 0.44; Damping 0.3; Decay
  8 s at middle C, longer in the bass (key to Decay -0.5); a 12 ms breath of noise for the
  finger. Against the recording the first, third and fifth harmonics are now within 1 dB in
  the middle of the nine notes (11.5 dB before), and their levels a second later within 5 dB
  (10 dB before). Still different: the recording has more above the twelfth harmonic in its
  first 30 ms (by 3 to 17 dB), and its upper harmonics beat.
- It is on the audition page as "Harp String, second try": the recording, the version the
  user rejected, and this one. The user's ear decides.

## D102. How the granular effect is built (2026-11-13)

Carries out D82: live granulation as an effect on a whole part. Choices inside the build:

- **A twelfth insert type, "Grain", with code of its own (`src/synth/v3/GrainInsert.h`) beside
  the slot's rack (taken, recommended).** The other eleven types are the master rack's stages;
  the rack has no such stage and the master section was not asked to get one. Adding it as a
  rack stage would have put it in the master too and touched the rack every preset uses.
- **Each Grain slot keeps its own four seconds of stereo (taken).** D82 says one recording per
  part; a part with Grain in two slots gets two, each of what reaches that slot, so a cloud of
  a cloud works. 1.5 MB a slot, taken when the engine is prepared; nothing is allocated while
  playing.
- **Eight parameters (taken): Size 10 to 500 ms, Density 1 to 80 grains a second, Position (how
  far back, to 3 s), Spray (up to a second further back at random), Pitch -24 to +24
  semitones, Spread (stereo), Freeze, Mix.** Freeze stops the recording, and the cloud keeps
  playing what was caught for as long as it is on.
- **A grain never crosses the point where the recording is written (taken).** A grain pitched
  up reads faster than the recording grows and would catch up with it; one pitched down falls
  back toward the oldest sound. Each starts where it has room for its whole length, which may
  be further back than Position asks.
- **Grains are read with the sampled instruments' windowed sinc (taken, recommended), narrower
  when pitched up.** The kernels moved to `SincKernels.h` for both. A four-point read costs a
  tenth as much and folds a part's treble back into the cloud when it is pitched an octave or
  two up; quality comes first (the user's rule). Up to 32 grains.
- **Level (taken):** grains of different sound add in power, so the level follows the root of
  the overlap, as in the Grain generator. With no Spray and no Pitch every grain plays the
  same moment of the past and they add in amplitude: there the level follows the overlap
  itself, else a dense, unscattered cloud would be 12 dB too loud. When a cloud is made
  thinner the level comes down at once and goes up only over a grain's length, because the
  thick grains are still playing.
- Not built: tempo-synced grains, a reverse switch, feedback into the recording. None was
  asked for; all three fit the eight parameters only by replacing one.
- Four clips on the audition page (`tools/audition/make_graindemo.py`): Glass Bell as it is,
  with a cloud, with the cloud an octave up, and smeared. Freeze cannot be shown in a
  rendered clip; it is tested (a note frozen while it sounds is still there three seconds
  after it stopped).

## D103. The user's answers on the Conservatory and the Museum (2026-11-13)

Four pick lists at the end of the day's run:

- Instruments that sound an octave off the key their author mapped them to. Offered: where
  they sound (recommended, and what D100 had built), or **as the author mapped them (chosen)**.
  So the xylophone, both glockenspiels and the tubular glockenspiel play an octave under their
  sound, as they are written; the TX81Z electric piano sounds an octave below its key and the
  Clavisynth an octave above, as the patches do. The builder's `shift=` is no longer used;
  `sounds=12` tells it where such an instrument's tuning is to be measured, so the bars and
  tubes stay corrected (they are 15 cents sharp as recorded).
- Marimba and Kalimba in two rooms. Offered: **the same name in two rooms (chosen,
  recommended)**, rename the models (Marimba Bar, Kalimba Tine), or rename the recordings.
- How loud the recorded instruments sit. Offered: room for fortissimo (recommended, and what
  D100 had built), **half way (chosen)**, or as loud as the synth presets. So a sampled
  preset's test chord at velocity 100 may now peak at -2 dB, just under the output's soft
  clipper, about 4 dB up on before; chords played harder than about velocity 110 reach the
  clipper and their attacks are rounded off slightly. A style's gain puts the middle one of
  its loudest notes at 0.8 of full scale (it was 0.5), so the presets' master gains still fit
  their range.
- The Museum's first sounds (several could be picked): **all four groups chosen**: the DX7's
  E.PIANO 1 and BASS 1; the Prophet-5's sync lead and brass; a Minimoog bass and lead; and
  the Alpha Juno hoover ("What the?"), the TX81Z LatelyBass and Juno-106 factory patches.
  Sources found so far: the DX7's full voice data (the `dx7-patches` collection, from Dave
  Benson's DX7 pages: every operator's ratio, level, envelope and scaling; E.PIANO 1 is
  algorithm 5, three two-operator pairs, which is three FM2 generators in one voice here);
  the Prophet-5's 40 factory patch sheets, scanned from its manual (midi.org, "Original
  Prophet 5 Patch Sheets"); Tom Rhea's 1974 "Sound Charts" for the Minimoog and Moog's own
  Model D patch sheets. Not found yet: published values for the hoover, LatelyBass and the
  Juno-106 patches; they need their patch dumps decoded, and a sound without a source cannot
  enter the Museum (D88).

## D104. The Museum's first candidate: DX7 E.PIANO 1 (2026-11-13)

Carries out D88 and D103. How a Museum sound is made and where it waits:

- **Candidates wait in `resources/museum-candidates/museum.csbank`, which is not built into the
  plugin (taken).** D88 admits a sound only after the user's side-by-side; a candidate in the
  factory bank would already be shipped. On admission its lines move to
  `resources/presets/12-museum.csbank`. The side-by-side is rendered from the candidates file
  (`tools/audition/make_museum.py`) and shown at the top of the audition page.
- **The DX7's numbers are converted, not matched by ear (taken).** After the open DX7 emulation
  msfa (Dexed's engine, Apache licence, read for its formulas) and Chowning and Bristow's index
  table: a level step is 0.75 dB; an envelope falls (4 + q mod 4) * 2^(q div 4) * 0.063 dB a
  second with q = rate * 41 div 64 plus the key's rate scaling (a rate of 0 then takes 374 s
  for the full range; 342 s is reported measured on the instrument); a modulator at output
  level OL reaches pi * 2^(33/16 - (99 - OL)/8) radians; detune is about 1.2 cents a step at
  middle C; velocity moves a level by sensitivity * (table - 239) / 8 half steps.
  `tools/audition/dx7_show.py` prints a voice this way.
- **E.PIANO 1 in this synth's terms (taken):** algorithm 5 is three carrier-modulator pairs.
  Two pairs at 1:1 are an FM4 generator on "Two Pairs" (indices 5.5 and 2.3 radians at
  velocity 99, the second pair detuned -8.4 and +8.4 cents, with feedback), the tine pair is
  an FM2 generator with its modulator at 14 times the note (0.38 radians). The DX7 has six
  envelopes and a voice here three: the amplifier's serves the three carriers and, through a
  square-root curve, the two body indices (they fall 4 dB a second at middle C, as the
  carriers do); the tine's two-stage fall (18 dB in a seventh of a second, then 24 dB a
  second) is the sum of the two others. Each index follows velocity by the DX7's law, as two
  matrix slots: one scaled by velocity, one fixed. Left out: the first pair's faster carrier
  (8 against 4 dB a second), the 3 dB drop of the body indices in the first quarter second,
  and the sixth operator's level scaling above D3.
- **One number is fitted (taken): the feedback, 0.6 of this generator's range.** Computed from
  the emulation's arithmetic it would be 0.39. A search over the body index, the tine index,
  the steepness of the velocity law and the feedback, scored on the first sixteen harmonics of
  C3 and C4 at velocities 60, 80 and 100 over the first 0.8 s, found the converted indices
  best as they are (scaling them up by a quarter doubles the gap) and the fit flat between
  feedback 0.6 and 1.0: a middle gap of 3.9 dB at the chosen values, 3.0 dB at the best of 42
  combinations. The converted numbers are kept; the Museum's claim is that it is built from
  the published settings.
- **The reference is FreePats' FM Piano 1 (CC0): the E. Piano 1 voice recorded from Hexter,
  another open DX7 emulation, at twelve notes and three velocities.** Not the hardware; no
  recording of the hardware with a licence that allows keeping it was found. The page says so.
- BASS 1 (algorithm 16: one carrier under three modulator branches) fits a voice here as an
  FM2 generator modulating an FM4 generator's carrier; it waits for a reference recording.
  The Prophet-5, Minimoog and Roland sounds of D103 are not started.

## D105. The user's verdicts on the listening page, and what they turned up (2026-11-13)

All 43 sounds judged. 39 of the 40 Conservatory instruments: good. Four remarks:

- **Recorders: "a bit oversteer at the end when multiple notes are playing".** The output is
  not clipped there (the four styles' demonstrations peak at -4.5 to -9.8 dB, nothing above the
  soft clipper's knee). But a chord is not the sum of its notes: rendered with the preset's
  reverb, the chord differs from the sum of its four notes rendered alone by -40.5 dB; without
  effects by -124 dB. The cause is in the old effects rack: the reverb's input went through
  `tanh` ("soft-clip the input to the reverb"), and a recorded instrument runs hot inside the
  engine before its master gain brings it down, so the reverb heard every chord saturated.
  **The clip is removed (taken):** the plate is linear and cannot run away, and its shimmer
  path has a limit of its own. With it gone the same chord differs from the sum of its notes
  by -121.7 dB with the reverb on. Every preset with reverb gets its reverb clean; twelve
  masters moved by half a decibel or a whole one in the loudness pass (the reverb is slightly
  louder where its input used to be squeezed). Keeping the clip with a higher threshold was the
  other option: it protects nothing a linear reverb needs protecting from.
  Whether this was all the user heard is for the user's ear: the recorders' own tones, four at once,
  also make combination tones in the ear at high level.
- **DX7 E.PIANO 1: "an extra buzzing sound on top".** In the reference the detuned operators
  lie 0.2 to 0.35 Hz apart per harmonic; in the rebuild 1.3 Hz, so its upper harmonics beat 7
  to 11 times a second. The two open DX7 emulations disagree on what a detune step is: Dexed's
  formula gives 1.2 cents a step at middle C, Hexter (which made the reference) 1/32 Hz a step
  at any pitch, a fifth of that. **The candidate now takes the reference's figure (taken):**
  the detuned pair 1.5 cents either side at middle C, moved with the key by two matrix slots
  so it stays near a fifth of a hertz; the fit of the first sixteen harmonics is as before
  (the feedback of 0.6 is confirmed, 0.39 is clearly worse). Which emulation is right about
  the hardware is not settled here; the Museum's test is the side-by-side.
- **Harp String, second try: "closer, but not there: not enough echo".** The recording is in
  stereo and in a room (its channels are 0.6 to 0.96 alike; one note -0.38); the model check
  plays the preset without effects, in mono (1.00). With the preset's own reverb its channels
  are 0.65 to 0.83 alike. **The check gets a fourth clip, the preset as it is played, in its
  room (taken).** The preset itself is not changed.
- **Granular effect: "too sharp sometimes".** Measured on the page's clips: the cloud replays
  note attacks, and in the clip an octave up the replays are as strong as the played attacks
  and brighter (bursts above 3 kHz at 1.96, 2.10 and 3.78 s, where no note starts). What the
  effect should do about it changes how it behaves or what it controls, so it is put to the
  user as a pick list.

## D106. The granular effect softens the attacks it replays (2026-11-13)

The user found the effect "too sharp sometimes" (D105). Offered:

- **Soften replayed attacks (chosen by the user, recommended).** As it records, the effect marks
  where the sound's level jumps: its peak level against the same level smoothed over 20 ms
  (a steady sound gives 1; a mark begins at 1.2 and is full at 1.7). A grain passing over a
  mark is turned down by up to 20 dB, within 2 ms and a little ahead of the attack, and comes
  back over a tenth of a second: it swells into the note instead of striking it. The part's
  own sound is not touched. Cost: the cloud can no longer stutter or echo attacks; a rhythmic
  repeat is the delay's job.
- A Tone control in Spread's place: a low-pass on the cloud; no adjustable stereo width, and
  the replayed attacks only duller.
- Both.
- Leave the effect and make the page's clip gentler: it would still be sharp on struck sounds.

On the page's clip an octave up, the bursts of treble at the replays (1.96, 2.10 and 3.78 s,
5.7 dB over the clip's level, as strong as the played attacks) are gone: the strongest left
away from a played note is 0.9 dB. A test feeds a note that starts with a burst: its replay
comes out at less than a third of the burst's level, the held part as it was. The old clip
stays on the page beside the new one.

## D107. The user's second round of verdicts (2026-11-13)

The four sounds changed after D105 and D106, judged again.

- **DX7 E.PIANO 1: "admit it".** It moves from `resources/museum-candidates/museum.csbank` to
  `resources/presets/12-museum.csbank` and so into the plugin: the room "Museum" exists, with
  one preset, and the bank has 109. No effects on it; master -2.5 dB from the loudness tool.
  The note still on its card ("an extra buzzing sound on top") is read as left over from the
  round before, as the one on the granular card is: both verdicts changed, both notes did not.
- **Granular effect: "good".** Its clips move to the archive page.
- **Harp String, second try: "closer, but not there: too much echo this time"** (dry it had
  "not enough echo", D105). Measured again against the recording, each note's first six
  harmonics at three moments (60 to 260 ms, 450 to 750 ms, 1.0 to 1.4 s), three things in the
  string itself were off, more than the reverb:
  - the real harp's upper harmonics die two to four times faster than the preset's in the
    middle of the instrument (third harmonic of B3: 27 dB in a second against 6): a second
    after the pluck it is almost a pure tone. The string's loop filter loses treble per
    period, so a low string needs more of it for the same loss per second: Damping 0.52,
    falling with the key (0.75 at A2, 0.28 at E5);
  - its second and fourth harmonics are 8 to 20 dB weaker: Pick Position 0.47, moving to the
    exact middle going up;
  - its pluck has about 8 dB more noise between 2 and 8 kHz in the first 40 ms: the finger
    burst is now pink noise, 25 ms, at 0.6 (was white, 12 ms, at 0.25).
  **All three are set from the recording (taken):** a search over eight settings, one at a
  time, brought the robust mean difference over notes, moments and harmonics from 5.3 to 2.4;
  early harmonics 1, 3 and 5 stay within 1.2 dB, the late ones go from 5.4 to 3.7 dB off.
  The room: in the recording the treble after a pluck is strong for 40 ms (6.5 dB under the
  pluck), decorrelated between the channels, and at the noise floor after a quarter of a
  second: a small room's reflections. The preset's reverb is a plate: its treble sits 15 to
  20 dB under the pluck from the start and stays there for over a third of a second at any
  size, mix or damping tried. **The preset's reverb goes to its shortest (size 0, mix 0.25,
  taken)**: less echo, 10 dB less tail after a third of a second, still not that kind of room.
  Not taken: only turning the reverb down (the string's own mismatch was the larger one), and a
  short delay as a stand-in for reflections (a few discrete echoes colour the sound). A room
  of the recording's kind needs a new effect; that is put to the user as a pick list.
- **Recorders: verdict unchanged, "a bit oversteer at the end when multiple notes are
  playing".** The clips the user heard were rendered after the reverb fix. Measured on the
  four styles' chords: each is the sum of its notes to -119 to -123 dB; no clip peaks above
  -4.4 dB; no click or burst anywhere; and the envelope within each third-octave band
  fluctuates by 2 to 5 % (the flute chord, judged good: 30 %; the ocarinas: 4 to 7 %). The
  sound itself shows nothing. What is left is hearing: four loud, steady, near-pure tones
  close together make combination tones in the ear and in small loudspeakers, and in equal
  temperament they do not agree: the soprano chord's neighbours lie 263, 274 and 246 Hz apart,
  so the difference tones beat against each other 11 to 27 times a second, a rough buzz under
  the chord. Tuned pure they fall on one note (262 Hz), which is why recorder players tune
  thirds by them (UNSW Music Acoustics, "Tartini tones and temperament",
  https://newt.phys.unsw.edu.au/jw/tartini-temperament.html; Wikipedia, "Combination tone").
  This is a hypothesis about the user's ear, not a measurement of it. **A listening test goes
  on the page (taken)**, `tools/audition/make_eartest.py`: the soprano chord four times,
  equally loud: as the preset plays it; without reverb; without reverb and tuned pure (each
  note rendered alone, retuned by a windowed sinc and added: differences of 261.6 Hz each, each
  note's pitch taken as the mean slope of its fundamental's phase, since a blown note wanders);
  and four sine tones at the same pitches and levels, with no recorder and no synth in them.
  Which of them have the overdrive says whether it is the reverb, the tuning, the recordings
  or the ear. Not taken: lowering the preset's level or darkening it on a guess.

The listening page now shows what waits: the listening test, the harp's third try, and the
Conservatory. What was accepted is on the archive page; a verdict on a card that has left the
page stays in the browser's store.

## D108. The user's answers: a Room effect, and every Museum sound chosen (2026-11-13)

Two pick lists after D107.

**The harp's room.** Offered:

- **A new Room effect (chosen by the user, recommended):** a thirteenth insert type; the plate
  stays for halls.
- A Plate/Room switch on the reverb: fewer types, but a slot is one or the other, and Shimmer
  and Modulation mean nothing in a room.
- Hear the third try with the plate at its shortest first.
- No reverb on the harp.

Built as `src/synth/v3/RoomInsert.h`, in two stages after Moorer (1979). Early reflections:
the 24 image sources of the first and second order of a box-shaped room (5.0 x 3.9 x 2.7 m at
the middle of Size), as two microphones a little apart hear an instrument 1.3 m away: taps on
a delay line, each as late as its path is longer and as much weaker (distance and one loss per
wall). Late field: sixteen delay lines through a Hadamard matrix, each losing what gives one
decay time on all of them, with Jot's one-pole filter for a faster decay in the treble
(Damping). The reflections feed the late field with a weight (5) found on a prototype so that
the decay runs straight from the first reflection (within 2 dB of a straight line down to
-30 dB). Size is the decay time, 0.1 to 1.2 s; the room's dimensions grow with its root and
the walls' loss follows from Sabine's formula, so a larger room is also a livelier one. The
wet sound is brought to one level over Size and Damping (a parabola fitted on the prototype;
a test holds it within 2 dB). A change of Size fades the room out, rebuilds it and fades it
in: delay lines cannot change length while they sound. Parameters: Size, Damping, Width, Mix.
Measured decay times: 0.08, 0.24, 0.51 and 1.07 s at Size 0, 0.4, 0.7 and 1 (asked: 0.10,
0.27, 0.57, 1.20). Sources: Moorer, "About This Reverberation Business", Computer Music
Journal 3(2); Jot and Chaigne, "Digital delay networks for designing artificial
reverberators", AES 1991; J. O. Smith, "Physical Audio Signal Processing", Early Reflections
(https://ccrma.stanford.edu/~jos/pasp05/Early_Reflections.html).

**Harp String with it.** Set from the recording's reflections: Size 0.55 (0.39 s), Damping
0.3, Width 1, Mix 0.42; the plate is off. The treble after a pluck now falls as in the
recording (7.8, 14.0 and 20.4 dB under the pluck in the stretches 15 to 40, 40 to 80 and 80 to
160 ms; the recording 6.5, 13.4 and 21.2), and between 1.5 and 5 kHz the two channels are as
alike as the recording's (0.68 to 0.75; the recording 0.68 to 0.79).
Checking the room showed one more thing in the string: between 1.5 and 5 kHz the real harp
rings for about a third of a second after the pluck (50 dB a second), 10 to 17 dB above the
preset for the first 160 ms. One pluck pulse cannot be both dark in its first six harmonics
and bright above them, so **a second string carries the bright ring (taken)**: the same note,
a narrow pluck, a decay of one second, at a tenth of the level, quieter going up and brighter
when played harder. The mismatch in that band falls to a third (robust mean 3.1 to 1.0 dB)
with the harmonics as they were (2.4). The finger noise goes back to 0.4: with the second
string the first 40 ms are within 1 dB of the recording in all four bands from 1 to 16 kHz.
Not taken: a brighter single pluck with more damping (tried: it trades the harmonics for the
band and stays 10 dB short on the low strings).
The render tool's `--no-fx` now empties the parts' insert slots too; it used to leave them
on, which would have put the room in the clip marked dry.

**The Museum's next sound.** Offered: DX7 BASS 1 (recommended), a Minimoog bass and lead,
Prophet-5 sync lead and brass, or none now. **The user: "all of the above"**: all three groups
are built as candidates, each for a side-by-side.

## D109. The Museum's second candidate: DX7 BASS 1 (2026-11-13)

**Reference.** No renderer had to be built from an emulation's code: FreePats publishes
"Synth Bass 2", the BASS 1 voice recorded from Hexter, CC0
(https://freepats.zenvoid.org/Synthesizer/synth-bass.html), the same source as the E.PIANO 1
reference. Nine notes, one velocity, not stated. A file named C3 sounds 65.4 Hz: the voice is
transposed an octave down and its carrier runs at half the note, so it sounds two octaves under
the key; the decay rates of the files confirm which key made which (rate scaling follows the
key).

**The voice** (ROM 1A, voice 15, from https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/dx7-patches@1.0.1):
algorithm 16, one carrier with three modulators, two of them with a modulator of their own.
In this engine: an FM4 on "Stack Plus One" (carrier, a single modulator, and a stack) two
octaves down, and an FM2 before it, silent itself, that drives the FM4's carrier through the
FM link (the third modulator and its own, with the voice's feedback). Three envelopes for six
operators: env1 is the carrier's decay (5.1 dB a second at key C4, 1.8 times faster per octave:
keyTime 0.875) and the single modulator's index follows it, half through its root and half
directly, which falls 1.5 times as fast (the voice: 1.4); env2 serves the stack, the lower
modulator through its root and the upper one directly, twice as fast (the voice: 24 and 57 dB
a second; taken: 28 and 57); env3 the click's modulator (130 dB a second). Velocity laws are
straight lines through the root of the voice's level law per sensitivity. The third
modulator's level scaling (1.25 dB less per key below key 85) is a parabola in the key here:
within 4 dB from key 66 to 96, where it is small anyway.

**Fit.** Nothing in the patch is fitted. Searched: the velocity the reference was played at
(64, 80, 100, 115, 127: 100 fits, and 95 and 105 are within 0.5 dB of it) and a scale on all
indices (0.8, 1.0, 1.2: 1.0 fits). Over seven keys and five moments up to two seconds the
first sixteen harmonics are 1.3 dB off in the middle (mean 2.0), the level over time 1.0 dB.
The feedback amount makes no difference below the top octave (0.6 and 1.0 score alike); it is
at full, as feedback 7 is.

It waits in `resources/museum-candidates/museum.csbank` for the user's side-by-side.

## D110. Museum candidates from a Minimoog and a Prophet-5, and what a played reference asks for (2026-11-14)

The user asked for all the Museum sounds (D108). Two research passes found the published
settings and recordings of real instruments; everything copyrighted stays in the git-ignored
audition folder (the charts and manuals as PDFs, the recordings as short stretches), with its
sources in `audition/museum/minimoog/SOURCES.txt` and `audition/museum/prophet5/SOURCES.txt`.

**Minimoog: "Emerson's Fat Bass" and the "Trilogy" lead.** Both from Tom Rhea's Sound Charts
for the Minimoog (Moog Music, 1974, page 15), which prints the numbers in the knobs
(https://www.drtomrhea.com). Calibration from the service manual (archive.org,
synthmanual-moog-minimoog-service-notes): cutoff -1.1 on the dial is 440 Hz at the lowest key,
about 0.9 octave per division, both keyboard switches together track one octave per octave;
the decay switch makes the release as long as the decay. Reference: a real Model D set to
these two charts and played (SynthMania, "Minimoog Sound Charts by Tom Rhea", YouTube; one
minute fetched).
The reference is a played phrase, not single notes. Taken: **the rebuild plays the same
phrase**. `tools/audition/transcribe_mono.py` follows the fundamental in 5 ms steps (the
candidate note whose first ten harmonics carry the most spectrum, marked down when its odd
harmonics are empty) and tells struck notes from legato ones by the rise of the treble;
`lib/phrase.py` turns that into notes that make a single-trigger, low-note-priority preset
move as the player's hands did; `phrase_fit.py` compares reference and rebuild in ten octave
bands in 10 ms steps and searches the values the documents leave open. Not taken: a
side-by-side on different notes (it would compare the playing), and single notes cut from the
phrase (there are hardly any).
- Fat Bass: the chart's three sawtooths (32', 16', 16') at full level. The search left the
  cutoff exactly where the chart and the service manual put it (752 Hz at middle C). Fitted:
  the contour's reach (3.1 octaves for "3.5"; the manuals say 1.4 to 2.2), emphasis 5 (0.13:
  little, with three oscillators driving the filter), the contours' times (the legend is
  close to the time a contour takes to finish), sustain 5 (0.8). Bands from 22 Hz to 1.4 kHz
  are within 3 dB in the middle. Above 1.5 kHz the real one has 8 to 17 dB more, 38 dB and
  further under its level: the chart also lets oscillator 3 shake the filter at audio rate by
  the wheel, and this engine has no such path. Saturation after the filter was tried and did
  not help.
- Trilogy lead: the chart's 2' oscillator at 8 with two 16' ones at 2. On the recorded
  instrument the "square" has a strong second harmonic and almost no third: a pulse of about
  a third. Fitted: pulse widths (0.34 and 0.31), cutoff (3 kHz at middle C; the dial alone
  gives 1.4), emphasis 6 (0.65), the contour (1.4 octaves, slow), glide (25 ms: the notes
  change within a few hundredths of a second). Bands from 176 Hz to 11 kHz are within 3 dB in
  the middle. That instrument is 30 cents sharp; `CoolSynthRender --tune` plays the rebuild at
  the recording's pitch (a held pitch bend).

**Prophet-5: Sync II.** Factory program 3-2, the sound of The Cars' "Let's Go" by SynthMania's
and others' account (no statement of the band's was found). The settings are Sequential's own
numbers: the 2020 Prophet-5's factory bank holds the forty original programs
(https://sequential.com/support/download/prophet-5-10-sounds/, decoded with the MIDI
implementation document), and the 1978 patch sheets agree but for one knob. Calibration from
the Rev 3 service manual and its circuit values. Reference: SynthMania's recording of a real
Rev 3 playing program 32 (MP3, copyrighted).
The transcriber cannot follow a synced oscillator: the swept slave misleads it at every note.
Taken: **single notes cut out of the recording** (`make_museum.py`, "excerpts"), only those
whose pitch holds still at five moments; the player bends most high notes with the wheel, so
the set is three low notes and one C#5. From the data: oscillator pitches, pulse width (52 %;
fitted 53.5), Poly-Mod depth (1.97 octaves; fitted 2.04), envelope sustain and attack.
Fitted, where units differ and the documents disagree: the filter envelope's reach (4.1
octaves; the sheet says 3.2, the stored data 4.9), the cutoff (0.7 octave above nominal), the
decay (1.2 s time constant; 1.7 nominal). All bands from 176 Hz up are within 1 dB in the
middle.

**Prophet-5: Brass. Not built.** Program 1-1's data is in hand, but no usable reference: the
recording found plays short stabs and chords, two other walkthroughs of real instruments play
chords, and from the documents alone the sound comes out 8 dB off and far duller than the
recording (the filter envelope's reach is where two readings of the circuit differ by a
factor of two). A fit on badly cut stabs wandered to values no unit has. It is put to the user.

**The page went dead.** The app stopped the listening page's server while the research agents
ran (one had opened a browser tab), and the user was sent to a dead page. Restarted; agents
are told to keep out of the browser, and the page is checked with a request before anyone is
sent to it.

  in the three recorder clips (with reverb, without, tuned pure) and not in sine tones at the
  same pitches and levels. That rules out what D107 supposed (the ear's own combination
  tones), the reverb and the tuning: it is in the recordings.
  Looked at, in the soprano's thirteen recordings: none is clipped (peaks 12 to 35 dB under
  full scale in the files); the fundamentals flutter less than the flute's, which was judged
  good. But the chord's third, E6 (the author's file SopRecorder_Sus_E5), is a different kind
  of note from its neighbours: its second and third harmonics lie 12 and 14 dB under the tone
  (the C6 beside it: 31 dB under and less), it stands 26 dB over what lies between its
  harmonics (the others of the chord: 37 to 41), it has side tones 20 to 40 Hz from its
  fundamental, and with the author's gain of 18 dB it comes out the loudest of the four by 3
  to 10 dB. An overblown note, edgy and a little rough. F#6, A#6 and C7 measure alike; G6 is
  clean.
  **A second listening test goes on the page (taken):** the chord's four notes one at a time,
  as loud as they are in the chord, the chord without the E6, and the chord with the E6 played
  from the recording a tone below. If the user's answer confirms it, what to do about it is
  the user's choice (it is how that instrument sounds in its top register; the alternatives
  change the recording's notes or their balance). Not taken: changing the instrument now on
  this measurement alone.

Built as a candidate: one sawtooth, no resonance, both envelopes from Sequential's factory

its decay at 0.7 of the nominal time; Brass takes both. The page plays it alone (five single

## D113. The user's fourth round: the joins between notes (2026-11-14)

All five waiting items were judged; the run before had ended on "I wait" with no pick list,
which the user rightly objected to. Verdicts, the user's choices (pick lists) and what was done:

- **Recorder test, note by note: "none of these has it".** No single note, and not the chord
  without the E6 or with the E6 from the neighbouring recording. So the overdrive is the E6
  recording's strong overtones against the other notes of a chord. Offered: replace E6 and
  F sharp 6 from their clean neighbours (recommended), only E6, all four overblown top notes
  (the top two stretched three and five semitones), or leave it. **The user: E6 and F sharp 6.**
  `build_library.py` takes `skip=E5,Fs5` (the author's file names; s for a sharp); their keys
  fall to the nearest recording (E6 from D6, F6 and F sharp 6 from G6). The soprano's demo clip
  is new; the test's clips are kept as heard.
- **Minimoog Fat Bass: "completely wrong. notes start sometimes muffled ... you analyze
  better", and then: "I think it is the transition from one note to the other".** Measured at
  every join, reference against rebuild (`tools/audition/transitions.py`: the loudness dip, the
  jump in brightness, the time the pitch takes): in the recording nearly every note starts 11
  to 24 dB brighter than the last one ended (the filter's sweep starts again); the rebuild had
  that at two thirds of them and 0 to 4 dB at the rest, because the transcriber had called
  those notes joined. Its test read a fixed band over a 110 ms window; it now reads the share
  of energy above a multiple of each note's own pitch from 20 ms windows. All thirty notes of
  the two clean stretches come out struck, and the rebuild's joins have the jump. The middle
  stretch (45.3 to 51.5 s) has pitch-wheel bends that had been transcribed as wrong notes; it
  is left out. Refitted on the first stretch: cutoff again exactly the chart's 752 Hz; the
  bands from 22 Hz to 1.4 kHz within 0.7 dB in the middle but one (2.6).
- **Minimoog Trilogy lead: "in the real instrument a much clearer sharp start often, while in
  your version it is smoothed".** At the joins the recording's pitch arrives within 0 to 10 ms;
  the rebuild took 25 ms (the glide I had kept because the chart has glide on, against the
  fit, which wanted none). And the recording stays bright for seconds, which a tune of joined
  notes with a filter sweep that sustains at nothing cannot do: its notes are struck, but the
  sweep lasts longer than the notes, so a struck note is hardly brighter than the last and the
  transcriber's test cannot see it. Three readings of the struck notes were fitted and
  compared: only the first note (5.9 dB off), where the loudness sags at the join (4.7), every
  note (4.4). **Every note struck, no glide (taken).** Joins that disagree on brightness: 4 of
  64 (were 12).
- **Prophet-5 Sync II: "very close but there is an extra small note in between the real
  notes".** The cuts of the reference ran to where the level fell, which on a phrase is into
  the next note, and kept a quarter second more. A cut now ends where the pitch leaves the
  note (three moments in a row within 15 cents, from the end backwards), with a 25 ms fade.
- **Prophet-5 Brass: "sounds like a midi pc sound card".** Offered: fit it to the real
  recording by working out its chords (recommended), drop it, or keep guessing from documents.
  **The user: fit it.** The recording's long events are held chords on which the player brings
  in the wheel's vibrato after about 0.7 s; read over their whole length the chords came out as
  clusters of semitones. `tools/audition/transcribe_chords.py` now reads the peaks of a short
  window with a tuning per chord, and five chords were checked by hand (A minor seventh, F,
  E minor seventh, C, E minor), each cut before the vibrato. Fitted: the filter's reach stayed
  exactly at the factory data's 5.6 octaves; cutoff 0.2 octave above nominal, the filter's
  times 0.6 of nominal, sustain 0.4. Bands from 176 Hz to 5.6 kHz within 1.5 dB in the middle.
  The candidate has a side-by-side now. `CoolSynthRender --cc` moves a controller in time
  (not needed in the end: the cuts stop before the vibrato).
- **"i don't know what attack and decay mean".** The page's verdicts say it in plain words
  now ("how notes start", "how notes fade or end", "how one note passes into the next").

Order, the user's choice: Minimoog first, then Sync II, recorders, Brass.

## D114. Oscillator 3 shakes the ladder's cutoff (2026-11-14)

End-of-run pick list after D113, while five redone sounds waited for the user's ear. Offered:

- **Give the Fat Bass its growl (chosen by the user, recommended):** the chart has the
  filter-modulation switch on with the wheel a little raised, which this engine could not do.
- Build nothing until the verdicts are in.
- LatelyBass next.
- Search for hoover and Juno-106 sources.

**The path.** The ladder gets a seventh value, `fm` (0 to 3 octaves, squared travel, 0 by
default): generator 3's signal, at unit level whatever its level in the mix, scales the
cutoff's coefficient by 2^(fm * signal) every sample, held under the filter's highest cutoff.
The voice hands generator 3's signal for the sub-block to every processor
(`SlotParams::modulator`). Not taken: a matrix route (the matrix runs once per sub-block, 3000
times a second, and its sources are not audio), and a free choice of the source generator
(the Minimoog has one, oscillator 3; a second value for a choice nobody asked for).
A test: at 0, or with no signal in slot 3, the output is bit for bit what it was; shaken by
two octaves, a 2 kHz tone through a ladder closed to 1 kHz gets side tones 110 Hz either side
at -43 dB that were at -174 dB, and comes through 12 dB louder; at full swing and full
resonance on noise it stays finite and bounded.

**Fat Bass with it.** Fitted with the comparison reaching down to bands 58 dB under the
reference's level: the shake comes out at a tenth of an octave (the chart only draws the wheel
"a little up"), with the cutoff at 900 Hz at middle C (the manual's reading: 752), the
contour's reach 2.5 octaves. The band from 1.4 to 2.8 kHz closes from 4.2 dB short to 1.3.
Above 3 kHz the rebuild stays 10 dB short of a sizzle that sits 50 to 60 dB under the note:
more shake does not supply it (the fit refuses it), so it is not the modulation; a Minimoog's
oscillators leak a little past its filter. Left as it is and said so on the preset; adding a
leak by layering unfiltered oscillators 50 dB down is possible if the user hears the lack.

## D116. Minimoog: the notes' edges, and more of the shake (2026-11-14)

On the two verdicts of D115.

- **Trilogy lead, "the beginning in the reference instrument is more of a ramp up curve".**
  Measured on the notes that start from silence: the recording's loudness is up within a
  millisecond, but its band above 3 kHz takes 7 to 48 ms; the rebuild's was up in 1 ms. The
  filter contour cannot start at once (the manual gives 10 ms as the shortest attack; the
  dial's 0 is that). **Filter attack 10 ms (taken, from a fit with the attack free; the two
  onsets alone would allow 5 to 35).** The same look at the notes' ends: the recording falls
  20 dB in 82 to 92 ms after a key goes up, the rebuild took 330 (the chart's decay time read
  as a release time with the decay switch on). **Release 300 ms in this engine's terms
  (taken).** Emphasis comes out at 0.4 with the attack in.
- **Fat Bass, "too dull or muffled".** With the comparison reaching 66 dB under the
  reference's level the fit takes four times more of oscillator 3's shake (0.4 octave) and no
  saturation after the filter (offered to it, refused): the bands up to 2.8 kHz are within
  1 dB in the middle, 2.8 to 5.6 kHz is 6.6 dB short (was 10.8), above that 12. Share of
  energy above 1.4 kHz at a note's start: within 2 dB of the recording's. What stays short is
  a floor above 3 kHz that the recording keeps through the whole note at about -61 dB.

## D117. LatelyBass, and the engine grows for it (2026-11-14)

The user chose "LatelyBass next (Recommended)" and pointed at Dexed.

- **Dexed has no LatelyBass and could not hold one.** Its built-in library (1,056 voices) was
  searched; the FreePats "LatelyBass" (CC0) is an imitation made on Dexed's six sine operators.
  The real voice needs the TX81Z's operator waves. **Taken: the real voice.**
- **The voice's data** comes from the instrument's own preset table in its firmware
  (github.com/raybellis/TX81Z, preset C15): algorithm 2, feedback 7, transpose -12; operators at
  0.5, 0.5, 1, 1 times the note, levels 99, 74, 71, 79, the third on wave 5. The chip's rules
  come from ymfm's OPZ core (github.com/aaronsgiles/ymfm): a modulator at full level swings a
  phase by 8 pi, feedback 7 by 4 pi through the mean of the last two outputs, 0.75 dB a level
  step, decay 0.1067 (4 + (r & 3)) 2^(r >> 2) dB a second. The key-velocity law is the
  firmware's own table: (15 - 2 KVS) + floor(map[velocity] KVS / 8) steps of 0.375 dB, so a
  velocity-sensitive operator is 0.4 to 4.9 dB under its level even at the hardest key. The
  reference is a recording of a real TX81Z playing this voice (one note, github.com/cesaref/TX81Z,
  the material of the talk "Emulating the TX81Z", ADC 2024); a plain model of those rules
  (`tools/audition/lib/tx81z.py`) matches it within a decibel or two per harmonic, and stands in
  for the instrument at other keys and velocities.
- **The engine could not make it: its FM operators were sines and every slot type was full at
  eight values.** The user, asked nothing, wrote: "expand the engine (12 slots etc) if needed".
  **Taken: a slot type may use twelve values.** Options weighed: (a) twelve values for every
  slot, the first eight keeping their IDs and matrix numbers, P9 to P12 added at the end (taken:
  nothing saved changes meaning; costs 72 more host parameters); (b) a separate "FM4 with
  waves" generator type (refused: two types for one thing); (c) one packed "waves" value
  (refused: 4,096 combinations cannot be a choice a person reads).
- **FM4 operator waves (taken): the TX81Z's eight**, W1 the sine, W2 the sine squared with its
  sign, W3 and W4 their first halves, W5 to W8 the same at twice the pitch in half the cycle.
  W2 is drawn pointed in the instrument's manual; a recording of the instrument in the talk's
  slides shows it round-topped, as ymfm has it, and that is what is built. A carrier on a wave
  with an average (W3, W4, W7, W8) would put a step under every note: the plain wave's average
  is taken off and a 5 Hz high-pass takes what modulation adds. The real instrument blocks the
  step only after its envelopes, so its notes thump; ours do not. Patches of sines are
  bit-for-bit as before.
- **The feedback follows operator 4's level on the chip** (it is taken after the level and the
  envelope: feedback 7 is half the operator's index). **Taken: the matrix does it in the preset**
  (the envelope that sets operator 4's index also sets the feedback, with a straight line through
  the velocity law). A built-in "feedback follows level" was refused for now: it would change
  the two admitted DX7 sounds or need a thirteenth value.
- **Result, nothing fitted.** Against the real note: the first sixteen harmonics within 0.9 dB
  on average (2.6 at most) over half a second, the level within 0.5 dB. Against the chip's
  rules over fifteen keys and velocities: 0.3 to 1.4 dB on average. The vibrato is the voice's
  own (pitch depth 7 of the chip's 127 at sensitivity 5: 5.5 cents either way; the recording's
  long notes swing 4 to 5 cents at about 3.5 Hz, taken for LFO speed 25).
- **The side-by-side** uses SynthMania's recording of a TX81Z playing C15 (copyrighted, kept in
  the audition folder only): three stretches, 36 notes. A new transcriber
  (`tools/audition/transcribe_fmbass.py`) was needed: this bass starts each note with the
  octave 14 to 21 dB louder than the note, and both pitch trackers lost the tune. It finds
  note starts by the jump of the spectrum, the pitch from spectral peaks, and each note's key
  velocity as the one whose tone fits by the chip's rules (the carrier ignores velocity, so only
  the tone says how hard a key was hit); the octave of a short note is settled by tone too.
  Thirty-four of the 36 notes fit the rules within 3.1 dB, most within 2. That recording has
  4 dB more of the band under 44 Hz than the rebuild and the direct recording has 2 to 4 dB
  less: the recording chains differ, so nothing was changed.
- **Left open, heard by nobody yet:** the real output converter (ten bits with a shifting
  range) leaves a haze 55 to 60 dB under the note; the rebuild is clean. Operator 3's detune of
  -3 (a tenth of a hertz) and operator 4's second, slow decay from 36 dB down are left out.

Asked the same day: the slow brass-like synth that opens Daft Punk's "Contact". It is a sample
of The Sherbs' "We Ride Tonight" (1981, album "Defying Gravity"; keyboards Garth Porter, who
confirmed in 2013 that "the bit they used was just my bit; the keyboard bit"). No source found
names the instrument: album credits say only "keyboards", interviews do not say, no forum
thread identifies it. The opening was cut from the user's own copy of the album into the
audition folder for study.

  sound again once another key went up. No polyphonic synthesizer does that, and the engine
  rightly does not. **The test's rule was wrong and is corrected (taken):** a key that gave its
  voice to a newer key stays silent while held; every other held key must sound; never more
  keys sound than the preset has voices. 1,257 key states checked, none failing.

## D120. The opening of "We Ride Tonight" ("Contact"): two synths, measured (2026-11-14)

The user's choice, on the record he supplied as a file, with: "there are two synths. The first
starting synth, and then the heavier second part. It would be nice if you can identify both."

**What is known from sources:** nothing about the instruments. The opening of "Contact" is a
sample of The Sherbs' "We Ride Tonight" (1981, album "Defying Gravity", keyboards Garth Porter,
who said in 2013 "the bit they used was just my bit; the keyboard bit"). Album credits say
"keyboards"; no interview, gear list or forum thread names a model. So both are identified by
measurement, and the result is a reading, not a fact.

**The record's timeline:** 1 to 3.5 s a fade-in; 3.5 to 26.4 s the first synth alone (level
steady, left and right 0.92 alike); at 26.45 s the level rises 5 dB and from 26.95 s left and
right are half alike or less: the second synth; cymbals from 37 s; the band at 44 s.

**The first synth, measured:**
- Every note is two lines of equal strength: G2 98.38 and 98.92 Hz, A3 219.46 and 221.72, B3
  246.60 and 249.02. One set sits 13 to 16 cents sharp of concert pitch on every note (the
  record's speed), the other 17 cents under it (9 on the G): two oscillators a voice, one
  detuned flat, with the small differences from voice to voice that tuned analogue oscillators
  have. Even harmonics as strong as odd: sawtooths.
- The strongest lines fall 6 dB an octave up to 3 kHz (a sawtooth, unfiltered), are 6 dB under
  that at 5 kHz and fall about 24 dB an octave more from 5 to 9 kHz: one four-pole low-pass.
- At each new note the band above 4 kHz jumps 10 to 14 dB within 40 ms and is back after a
  second: a filter contour with a fast attack and a slow decay. The loudness does not jump.
- Side signal 13 to 15 dB under the middle: a mono instrument (the rest is the room).
- Five or six notes at once at most, some of them harmonics of the bass.
**Reading: a five-voice, two-oscillator, four-pole, mono instrument: a Prophet-5 above all.**
A Yamaha CS-80 also has two detuned oscillators a note but 12 dB filters; a Jupiter-4 or
Polysix has one oscillator a voice.

**The second synth, measured (not built yet):**
- Far brighter: its lines hold within 9 dB from 300 Hz to 4.7 kHz and reach 9 kHz.
- Each note sits at its own place between the speakers, and the place changes when the note is
  struck again: D6 (1180.7 Hz) is left of centre at 27.3 s, right at 29.0 s, left at 31.0 s; B5
  right at 27.3 s, left at 34.8 s. Where a note is in both channels it has a different pitch
  in each (F sharp 6: 1485.75 Hz left, 1487.94 right): two voices, not one voice in two places.
**Reading: a polysynth whose voices are panned one by one and taken in turn: an Oberheim of
the OB series** (stereo from the outset, per Sound On Sound; the OB-X emulation OB-Xf models
its eight voice-pan knobs). An overdub of several takes panned apart would show the same in a
single chord but not the change of place from one strike to the next.

**Taken:** the first synth as candidate "Contact Opening I", built from the recording: two
sawtooths 17 cents apart, the ladder, a contour of 15 ms attack. Fitted to 22 seconds of the
record (60 notes read from it by the new `transcribe_held_chords.py`: chords that change
without a break, notes found as the non-negative mix of sawtooth keys closest to the spectral
lines, a note in two stretches running held across the change): cutoff 1.9 kHz at middle C
following the keyboard, the contour 2 octaves up, falling over 4.4 s to 0.3, emphasis 0.025.
The middle difference per band is 1.4 to 2.6 dB from 176 Hz to 5.6 kHz; the band under 88 Hz
is 6 dB short (bass notes the reading missed, or the record's low end). The card says that
notes may be wrong.

**Open:** the second synth needs a part of its own and the first under it for a fair
side-by-side from 26.5 s on; and the engine has no way yet to place each voice between the
speakers except a random pan per note through the matrix.

## D122. Trilogy's note beginnings; the ladder's leak; voice spread (2026-11-14)

On the verdict of D118 ("the beginning of a note in the reference instrument is still
different"), while the first Contact synth waited for the user's ear; then, on his choice
"Second Contact synth (Recommended)" (other options: "Hoover or Juno-106 next", "Stop after
these two"), the engine feature that synth needs.

- **Trilogy lead, measured over 38 struck notes:** the share of energy above eight times a
  note's pitch, against its value 40 ms in: the recording +2.4 dB at the start, -2.0 at 80 ms,
  -6.0 at 160 ms; the rebuild +5.2, -5.6, -13.7. Every figure of the rebuild is 2.2 times the
  recording's: the filter contour swept more than twice too far. The band fit could not see it
  (ten octave bands, the middle difference over the phrase: a refit with the top bands counted
  and the contour's resting level free moved nothing). **Taken: the contour's reach 0.7 octave
  (was 1.7), the cutoff 4.4 kHz at middle C (was 2.7), drive 3 dB (was 6):** the rebuild now
  reads +1.7, -1.9, -6.0, and the band cost is the same 4.95 dB. The chart's "amount of contour
  3" is a small sweep too. After 160 ms the recording's top holds where the rebuild's goes on
  falling for the band above six times the pitch (-3.0 against -11.1 at 320 ms): left open.
- **A leak past the filter was the first reading and is wrong for the lead** (it takes a fifth
  of the raw oscillators to hold the top up, which no Minimoog lets through). The ladder keeps
  the value **Leak** (0 to 0.25 of the input passes the filter untouched; a test for it): it is
  what the Fat Bass's faint sizzle is (a floor about 61 dB under the note, D116), but that sound
  is admitted and is not touched without the user's word.
- **Voice Spread (taken, for the second Contact synth):** a part's poly voices each have a place
  between the speakers (sides alternate at four distances; the second eight voices mirror the
  first), scaled by `play voiceSpread`; with the allocator's turn-taking a note struck again
  comes from somewhere else, as on the polysynths with a pan pot per voice. Weighed: a random
  pan per note through the matrix (exists already; refused: a struck-again note can land where
  it was, and the places are not the instrument's fixed ones). Tests in `tests/V3PartTests.cpp`.

## D123. The second Contact synth as a candidate (2026-11-14)

The user's choice after admitting the first ("Second Contact synth (Recommended)").

- **Telling the two apart in one record.** Both play the same harmony from 26.5 s on. The first
  synth's second oscillators sit 0 to 8 cents flat of the tempered scale, everything of the
  second synth 7 to 19 cents sharp, and the first synth is in the middle while the second is
  spread. So: the second synth's chords are read from the side signal (left minus right), lines
  4 to 24 cents sharp; its deep bass (B1, A1: in the middle) from the middle, same lines, the
  two lowest keys; the first synth under it from the middle, lines 14 flat to 3 sharp only.
  `transcribe_held_chords.py` got a `window=LOW:HIGH` option for this. 49 notes for the second
  synth, 19 for the first; both readings have errors and the card says so.
- **The comparison has both synths, as the record has.** Options: (a) the second synth alone
  against the record (refused: the record never has it alone); (b) against the side signal
  only (refused: nobody listens to a side signal); (c) both of mine together against the
  record, the admitted first synth playing its own notes underneath (taken). `phrase_fit.py`
  adds a rendered layer to every trial ("under", with its level as a fitted value);
  `make_museum.py` renders the layer from its own preset and notes.
- **The preset (candidate "Contact Opening II"):** two sawtooths 4 cents apart, the ladder at
  1.8 kHz at middle C following the keys by a third, a contour of 1.5 octaves resting at 0.65,
  the loudness rising over 90 ms, Voice Spread 1, sixteen voices. A key's loudness follows its
  velocity so the deepest bass (under E2) can be played soft, as it sits in the record.
  Fitted over 10.5 s: the middle difference per band is 1.7 to 2.5 dB from 176 Hz to 5.6 kHz.
  **Known gaps:** the band from 88 to 176 Hz is 6 dB short (the low B, G and A of the chords);
  left and right are 0.60 to 0.70 alike against the record's 0.46 to 0.51 (the record is
  wider: its two oscillators a voice may sit apart, or its room adds to it).

## D126. Trilogy: the note boundaries, seen at last (2026-11-14)

On the verdict of D125 ("still very very different. maybe you dont see note boundaries
properly"). The user was right; single boundaries of the recording were looked at directly,
pitch and level every 5 ms.

- **The pitch slides into every note.** 63 slides measured (the 2' oscillator's pitch every
  2.5 ms by autocorrelation): upward about 5 ms a semitone (2 semitones in 8 to 10 ms, a fifth
  in 22, an octave in 60), downward about 12 ms a semitone (a fifth in 52 to 85); the time
  grows with the interval, so it is a slew at a steady speed, not a glide of fixed time. The
  chart has glide on at 5. **D113/D116 took the glide out on a wrong measurement** (the new
  pitch was looked for in a band a semitone wide round each note, which cannot show the
  travel); every round since inherited it.
- **Engine: glide by rate (taken).** `voice glideMode=rate`: the glide time is the time for an
  octave; `glideDown`: a downward glide takes that many times as long. Refused: faking it with
  the fixed-time glide (a second and an octave would take the same time: wrong at both ends).
  Preset: `glide=59 glideMode=rate glideDown=2.4`. At a boundary of eight semitones the
  rebuild's pitch now follows the recording's within 0.5 semitone every 5 ms.
- **The notes start where the slides start.** The transcriber put a note's start where the
  pitch was nearest the new note, in the middle of the slide or after it. The comparison's
  notes are now read from the pitch track itself (`glide_stats.py`): a note starts where the
  pitch leaves the note before it.
- **The player lifts keys.** In 13 of 64 notes the level leaves its plateau before the next
  note and falls about 65 to 115 dB a second, so the next note begins after a dip of 7 to 14
  dB. The rebuild held every key to the next one. Key-up times are now read from the level
  (2 dB under the note's plateau and 5 dB under 40 ms later). **Release 600 ms in this engine's
  terms (taken: the rebuild's dips are then -7.0, -6.1, -9.3, -15.3 dB where the recording's
  are -6.7, -7.9, -10.4, -13.8; 300 ms gave -13 to -29, a straight-line 250 ms -3 to -7).** D116
  had 300 ms from the last notes of phrases, which die faster (20 dB in 85 ms): left as it is,
  the dips inside the phrase are heard at every lifted key.
- Left open: brightness from note to note differs from the recording's by up to 9 dB either
  way (how the cutoff follows the keyboard), and the top's hold after 160 ms (D122).

## D127. The second Contact synth's bass, again (2026-11-14)

The user, after hearing the changed clip: "I thought you were gonna change something with the
bass or something." The change of D124 was too small to hear as one (6 dB short to 2.8).

- **Why it stayed weak.** In the record's first chord the root B2 (124.5 Hz) is the strongest
  line of all, 3 dB above the D4; in the rebuild it was 5.8 dB under the D4. The note reader
  had the bass in pieces, some an octave low (B1, A1, E2, D2), and playing those soft or hard
  moved energy between the wrong bands.
- **Taken: the bass as a line of its own** in the comparison's notes: B2, G2, A2, B2 where the
  record's middle has them, at full velocity, each with a faint octave under it (the record's
  62 Hz line is 11 dB under its 124 Hz line); every other transcribed note under C3 is
  dropped; the chords above at velocity 50; the first synth under it at -2 dB. The band from
  88 to 176 Hz is 1.3 dB short of the record, 44 to 88 Hz 1.6 dB short; the bands from 1.4 to
  5.6 kHz are 1.5 to 2.2 dB lower than before. Left and right are 0.60 to 0.72 alike (were
  0.53 to 0.62): the strong bass voice narrows the picture.
- The admitted preset itself is unchanged: this is how the comparison plays it. A key's
  loudness follows its velocity in this preset, so a bass played hard under chords played
  softly is what the player does on the keys.

  0.60 dB apart on average; the D126 values 0.91; more reach only lengthens the bright stage,
