## D131. Recorders good; the notes looked up; the second synth's grit measured (2026-11-14)

Read from the page's verdict store, and the user's words in the chat.

- **Recorders: "good"** (the Soprano clip with E6 and F sharp 6 from clean neighbours, D113).
  Settled; it leaves the top of the page.
- **Second Contact synth: "still not right", "i dont know what it is but original is slightly
  grittier"; in the chat: "the second synth is very, very close."**
- **"your notes are different from the original. I don't care. Don't try to fix that by
  recognizing but look up the notes online ... The chords."** Looked up: no chord chart exists
  for The Sherbs' song; for "Contact" Hooktheory has a user-contributed transcription of the
  intro (hooktheory.com/theorytab/view/daft-punk/contact; its notes come from the site's public
  API): B minor, G for two bars, A for two, G, Em7, A for two, with the tune D, C sharp, D, E.
  That is exactly the eight bars Daft Punk sampled (3.3 to 19.05 s of the record). **Taken:
  the first synth's comparison plays those chords and that tune over those eight bars,** laid
  out on the keys as the record's lines show (bass doubled at the octave, A3 and B3 held, the
  tune in pairs over A); the bands from 176 Hz to 2.8 kHz are 1.3 to 2.4 dB apart in the
  middle, as with my own reading. The record's second phrase (19 to 26 s) and the stretch
  with the second synth are in no published source: the second synth's clip keeps my reading.
- **The grit, measured.** Between its spectral lines the record holds a floor that follows the
  sound: 43 to 45 dB under the strongest line of every band from 150 Hz to 8 kHz. The rebuild's
  second synth alone has 68 to 80 dB there. Tried on the admitted preset: the filter driven 9
  or 18 dB harder leaves the floor where it is (distortion lands on the lines, not between
  them); a noise generator in each voice raises it by 4 to 5 dB for every doubling (0.04 gives
  -52 to -58 dB), so about 0.15 would meet the record's. A floor that follows the signal by
  the same distance in every band is what tape and a lossy file leave (this record reached us
  as AAC at 128 kbit/s), more than what an instrument does. Whether it goes into the sound is
  the user's choice; asked.

## D132. The second Contact synth's gritty twin (2026-11-14)

Asked how to handle the grit (pick list: "Second version to compare (Recommended)", "Put it
straight into the sound", "As a switchable effect", "Leave it clean"), the user first asked
whether he could add an effect himself later. Answer given: yes (three effect slots a part,
Lo-fi has a hiss; a noise generator is a setting too), but only clumsily until the real
interface exists: the plugin has a throwaway panel and no way to save a preset. Asked again
("Leave it clean (Recommended)", "Also make a gritty twin", "Put the grit into it now"), he
chose **"Also make a gritty twin"**.

- **The grit belongs to the 1981 recording, not to the download.** The lossless "Contact" has
  the same floor between the spectral lines over the same bars (-46.8, -44.3, -46.4, -46.9,
  -42.4 dB under each band's strongest line) as the AAC file of the Sherbs record (-47.9, -44.6,
  -46.0, -43.8, -40.6). D131's guess at the codec was wrong.
- **Taken: candidate "Contact Opening II Grit"**, the admitted preset with a third generator,
  white noise at 0.2, through the voice's own filter and loudness contour. White against pink:
  pink falls 8 dB too far from the lowest band to the highest, white stays within 3. Level:
  0.12 leaves the floor 3 to 5 dB under the record's, 0.2 within 0.4 to 3.3 dB (-47.9, -45.9,
  -44.7, -42.6, -46.5 against -45.0, -44.9, -44.3, -43.4, -43.2). Its comparison has the first
  synth underneath, as the clean one's has. The clean preset is untouched and stays admitted.
- Noted for the interface (phase 9): effect slots and a preset's noise must be quick to reach,
  and a player must be able to save his own version of a preset; the user asked for exactly
  this ("can i later tweak the sound and add an effect myself easily").

## D133. The second Contact synth's grit: five candidates for the ear (2026-11-14)

The user on the gritty twin (D132): **"not yet: something else"**, "very very close, but the
reference one is slightly grittier (a bit distortion possibly?)". My measures could not tell
noise from distortion (line searches drown in the chord's own dense lines), so I proposed to let
his ear choose among versions; he answered "It's okay if you give me multiple candidates, like 5
or something, no problem."

What the record shows, measured for this round:

- **It is not hiss.** Before the first note the recording's spectrum lies at -81 to -95 dB
  against full scale; while the second synth sounds, what lies between its spectral lines is at
  -33 to -37 dB (-48 dB from 4 to 8 kHz). The roughness is there only with the sound.
- **It is as strong against the lines in every band.** The middle level between the lines, under
  the strongest line of the band, over three chords: 40.0 dB (80-500 Hz), 39.3 (500 Hz-2 kHz),
  37.2 (2-6 kHz). The clean preset: 54.0, 48.1, 44.7. The twin (white noise 0.2 in each voice):
  48.9, 43.2, 38.0: right at the top, 9 dB short at the bottom, which is where D132's band
  floors did not look.
- What fills the three bands, tried with the engine as it is (every clip at the admitted
  preset's loudness on the first synth, tone balance unchanged within 0.3 dB except where said):
  - overdrive of the summed chord (the part's first insert slot, Drive): fills the top more than
    the bottom. Amount 0.1 with the noise 45.5, 39.6, 36.0; 0.2 without noise 43.2, 37.2, 34.0;
    0.35 reaches the bottom (39.9) with the top 7 dB over and the highest octave 3.5 dB up;
  - driving each voice's filter (ladder drive 6 and 12 dB), a tube or soft Drive processor after
    it: nothing (48.9, 43.2, 38.0), as D132 found;
  - the noise shaking each voice's filter (ladder "G3 To Cutoff" 0.05 to 1.0): top only;
  - a fast random wobble of the pitch (sample-and-hold LFO at 50 Hz, 0.001 and 0.003): rises
    with frequency, 47.4, 39.3, 34.6 and 42.5, 31.4, 29.2;
  - **a fast random tremble of each voice's loudness** (sample-and-hold LFO at 50 Hz on the
    VCA): fills every band alike, as the record has it. Depth 0.25 with the noise: **40.6, 39.8,
    37.3**; without the noise the top falls short (40.8, 41.1, 41.9); at 25 Hz the bottom does
    (43.3); the smooth random shape at 0.4: 37.7, 38.5, 37.2.

Options: (a) take the measured match alone as the new twin; (b) the user's guess alone
(overdrive); **(c) taken: a listening test** "Contact, second synth: which grit?" with the real
clip, the clean preset, the twin he judged and five candidates, each a preset in the candidates
bank so the chosen one is admitted as it is: A tremble 0.25 (steps) with the noise; B tremble
0.4 (smooth) with the noise; C Drive 0.1 on the part with the noise; D Drive 0.2 on the part
without it; E tremble 0.2, Drive 0.05 and the noise. A measure of mine agreed with the record
once before (D132) and his ear did not, so the ear decides again.

- The render tool gained `--keep-inserts` (with `--no-fx`: the parts' insert slots stay on), and
  the Museum page renders with it, so a sound whose character is in an effect slot is heard as
  it is. No Museum preset has an insert today.
- The page: an open listening test is the first card of "Waiting for your ear". The twin's own
  card and the two Contact cards left the top (first synth "fine now"; the clean second synth
  and the twin are clips 1 and 2 of the test).
- Online: nothing names the instrument or a patch for it (searches for a recreation of the
  "Contact" intro and for The Sherbs' synth found none); forum and review texts on the Oberheim
  OB-X speak of "raw" only by ear, no measurement of its noise or distortion.
- Asked the same day whether the two Contact sounds are an existing instrument's or ours: ours,
  fitted to the record (D120, D124), already named with "Contact"; the first is built as a
  Prophet-5 makes such a sound, the second as an Oberheim OB-X does.
- The user also asked what the architecture still marks as not built, because effects decide
  the interface. Checked: the twelve effect types and the three insert slots per part are built
  (D81, D93, D102, D108); TODO item 35 still said otherwise and is corrected. Open: the master
  section as six free slots (it is the fixed chain of ten), key pressure, MPE and the expression
  pedal (listed as matrix sources, fed by nothing), the user's own samples and wavetables,
  grains from a live generator, the saw generator's cost.

## D135. The master section as six effect slots (2026-11-14)

Carries out the first part of D134. The design always had master slots F1..F6 taking the same
types as the inserts (DESIGN_V3.md section 8); D81 kept the fixed rack for the time being. Taken
without asking, under the standing rule to build what the engine needs and log the options:

- **Six slots, each any of the twelve effects, Room and Grain included (taken).** Alternative:
  only the ten the rack had. Room and Grain were inserts only because the rack had no such stage;
  a slot has no reason to refuse them.
- **A master slot is the insert's own `InsertSlot` (taken).** The same code as an insert, which
  was already proven equal to the rack effect by effect (D93); a test now proves an insert equal
  to the same effect in a master slot for eleven types. Alternative: rewrite the effects as slot
  modules with arenas; more work for the same sound. The six slots live on the heap: inline, they
  made a processor too large for the Debug test runner's stack.
- **Bank files write `f1 = Reverb size=0.6 mix=0.25` (taken), and the old lines (`reverb = on
  ...`) are an error that names the new form.** Alternative: keep reading the old lines into the
  next free slot; two ways to write one thing. All 125 effect lines in the banks were rewritten
  in the old chain's order (drive, lo-fi, phaser, flanger, chorus, delay, reverb, compressor, EQ,
  pump). Where a line left a value out, the old parameter's default was written in when it
  differs from the slot type's default: drive amount 0 (slot 0.3), lo-fi wow 0, flutter 0,
  bandwidth 1 (0.2, 0.1, 0.8), chorus depth 0.4 and mix 0.3 (0.5, 0.5), delay feedback 0.25 and
  mix 0 (0.3, 0.3), reverb mix 0.2 (0.3). No preset uses more than four effects.
- **Checked: every preset rendered before and after (a chord and a short line, 5 s, effects
  on).** 113 of 118 are sample for sample the same. Of the other five, four use randomness on
  purpose and differ between two renders of the old engine as well; the fifth, Contact Opening II
  Grit, differs by exactly +0.500 dB and nothing else (the residue is 127 dB down): the old render
  tool had been built before the loudness pass set its master to -9 dB. Lesson kept in GEMINI.md:
  the render and normaliser tools are not part of the `build-debug` preset and must be rebuilt by
  target after a bank or engine change.
- **States and patches saved before are carried over, not refused (taken).** Processor state
  format 10, patch format 9; a version 9 state or version 8 patch has its fixed effects moved into
  the slots: each effect that was on takes the next slot in the old chain's order, with its
  values. A MIDI knob learned on one of those values follows it into its slot; one learned on an
  on switch or on an effect that was off is dropped. Alternative: refuse them, as every earlier
  V3 state change did (D74); but the user plays the VST3 in projects that would lose their sound.
- **The plugin's tail is the longest of the six slots' (taken):** a delay by its time and
  feedback (synced at the internal tempo), a reverb 12 s, a room its decay, a grain cloud 4.5 s.
- MIDI learn accepts the slot values (`f1.p3`); `CoolSynthRender --no-fx` empties the six slots.

Also on 14 November, asked by the user: a short public page about the engine, opening with the
engine map: https://tomcoolpxl.github.io/CoolSynth/ (`site/`, built by
`tools/site/build_engine_map.py`, published by `.github/workflows/pages.yml` on every push that
changes `site/`). The repository is public, so the page is too; it holds nothing private and no
copyrighted material.

## D136. Key pressure and the expression pedal (2026-11-14)

The second part of D134. Asked first how far to go ("Full MPE (Recommended)", "Pressure and pedal,
MPE light", "Only key pressure and pedal"), the user asked instead whether MPE keyboards are
common and whether a slightly dearer keyboard than the MiniLab 3 would have been one. Checked
online: the MiniLab 3's keys send no pressure, its pads do, each its own (MusicRadar); the KeyLab
Essential mk3 has none on its keys, the KeyLab mk3 one pressure for the whole keyboard; MPE
controllers are a niche of their own (Exquis, ROLI LUMI and Seaboard Block M about $270 to $350,
LinnStrument and Osmose about $1,050 to $1,730). Asked again with that ("Pressure and pedal now
(Recommended)", "Full MPE as well", "Own samples first"), the user chose **"Pressure and pedal
now"**.

- **The Aftertouch source of a voice is the larger of the whole keyboard's pressure (channel
  aftertouch) and its own key's (polyphonic aftertouch) (taken).** A keyboard sends one kind or
  the other; taking the larger means either works and neither cancels the other. Alternative:
  two sources. One more name in every source menu for a difference no controller makes.
- **Smoothed per voice over 10 ms, the macros' time (taken).** Pressure arrives in 128 steps at
  uneven times; routed to the loudness, a step clicks (a test routes full pressure to silence and
  finds the first millisecond still over half the level). A note struck while pressing starts at
  that pressure, without a ramp from nothing; a key's own pressure starts at 0 with each new key.
- **A key's pressure follows what the key plays (taken):** the engine sends it to the note that
  key sent each part (its transpose applied), and chord memory to every note of the chord that
  key holds.
- **The expression pedal (CC 11) feeds Expression, 0 until the pedal moves (taken).** MIDI's
  reset value for CC 11 is full; as a source "how far the pedal is down" reads better from 0, so
  a preset that uses it does nothing until the pedal moves. Reset All Controllers sets both
  pressures and the pedal to 0.
- **MPE stays for later:** the MPE Pressure and MPE Slide sources stay in the menus, fed by
  nothing; per-note bend would need each voice to follow its own MIDI channel. TODO item 35.
- Checked: all presets render as before (none uses these sources).

## D137. The user's own samples and wavetables (2026-11-14)

The third part of D134. Asked where the files live ("A User folder, by name (Recommended)",
"Anywhere, by full path", "Copied into the sound") and which wavetables to read ("The common
WAV kind (Recommended)", "Any WAV, cut into frames"), the user chose **a User folder, by name**
and **the common WAV kind**. Checked online for the wavetable format: one WAV of 2048-sample
frames, the frame size optionally in Serum's "clm " chunk; Vital assumes 2048 and reads the
left channel (KVR forum threads, community notes on the format).

Taken within those choices:

- **The User folder is "User" inside the sample library's root (taken):** one place the plugin
  already knows on every machine. A preset names a file by its path inside it, with forward
  slashes. Alternative: a folder of its own in the user's documents; a second place to set up.
- **A generator slot names the file; the slot's type decides how it plays (taken).** A
  Wavetable slot plays it as a table, a Sample slot as one recording over every key, a Grain
  slot as its grains' source. The file loads once into all three forms, whichever slot uses it:
  a sample (its root note and loop from its sampler chunk, else C4 and no loop; level following
  velocity squared as the instruments'), a wavetable when it has at most 256 frames (the first
  channel; frames of 2048 samples or the clm chunk's size, each resampled to 2048; a shorter
  file is one cycle; its offset removed; the whole table scaled so its loudest frame peaks at 1,
  so frames keep their levels against each other; the factory bank's 11 band limits), and a
  grain source (the first minute in mono; the full band, then about a fifth and a tenth of the
  rate through a 63-tap Kaiser-windowed sinc, as the factory samples' 10 and 5 kHz at 48 kHz).
  Alternative: a type-specific choice ("User" among the tables, among the samples). It would
  change the stored meaning of the existing choices.
- **The name is not a parameter but part of the patch's state (taken):** a child USER_FILES of
  the parameter state, one property per slot ("pA_g1"), so it travels with presets, saved sounds
  and DAW projects without an automatable parameter for a file name. Bank files write it as the
  generator's `file` setting, quoted when it has spaces. A preset, the init patch and a state
  that names no file clear it.
- **Loading never blocks the sound (taken):** a background thread reads the file; the audio
  thread gets the sound or nothing (the slot is silent while it loads, or when the file is
  missing). Up to 32 files at once; a file no slot has held for five seconds is dropped and its
  memory freed two seconds later. At most five minutes of audio a file.
- **The throwaway editor has a row of six file buttons (taken),** one per generator slot, with
  "Choose a file...", "No file" and "Show the User folder"; a file chosen from elsewhere is
  copied into the User folder. Throwaway like the rest of that editor (phase 9 designs the real
  one), but it lets the user try his own sounds now.
- Checked: all presets render as before; tests read real WAV files written with and without the
  clm and sampler chunks, and play a table, a sample and grains at the right pitch.

## D138. The cost of playing: measured, and two exact speed-ups (2026-11-14)

Asked what to do after the interface's engine parts ("Start designing the interface
(Recommended)", "Try my own sounds first", "Back to the Museum", "Remaining engine odds and
ends"), the user chose **"Remaining engine odds and ends"**: the sawtooth generator's cost
(TODO item 35) and grains from a live sound.

- **Measured first (taken).** A new opt-in report (tests/V3ModuleCostReport.cpp, run with
  COOLSYNTH_CPU_REPORT=1; the test runner now takes COOLSYNTH_TEST_ONLY to run one test) times
  each module alone for an eight-note chord, two seconds, best of three. The VA saw was cheap:
  4.5 % of real time with the voices' own 2.9 %, so under 2 % itself. The cost of the "eight-note
  saw chord at 15 %" in TODO was the ladder after it (13.5 %), and the Drive processor cost
  11.5 %. Both were made cheaper instead; the VA was left as it is.
- **The ladder runs left and right side by side in one SSE register (taken).** Every operation
  of the one-channel code, in the same order, on two lanes: IEEE arithmetic gives the same
  result in a lane as alone, so the output is the same to the last bit. 13.5 % to about 8 %.
  Alternatives: process one channel when both are equal (only centred voices gain, and the
  generators' pan gains differ by a bit, so rarely exact); an approximation that changes the
  sound (refused: quality first).
- **The Drive processor keeps what does not change (taken):** its normalising peak is worked out
  once while the drive amount is steady (it took two tanh a sample), and the curve's
  antiderivative at the previous input is kept from the sample before (a log and an exp per
  channel a sample). The same values, so the same output. 11.5 % to about 6 %.
- **Checked: all 118 presets rendered before and after; the 114 that use no randomness are bit
  for bit the same** (the other four differ between any two renders).
- Next costs, from the same report: FM4 (about 10 %), the Comb (about 7 %).

## D139. Grains from a live sound (2026-11-14)

The second of the user's "engine odds and ends" (D138). D79 left it out because every voice
would need memory to record into; D82 gave the user a granular effect on a whole part instead.
This is the per-voice form the requirement names. Taken without asking, under the standing rule
to build what the engine needs and log the options:

- **Turned on as a new combine mode, Live, on a Grain slot in place 2 or 3 (taken).** Combine is
  already "how this slot takes the generator before it"; Live joins Sync, Ring, FM and AM at the
  end of the list, so a saved state keeps its index. Other types treat Live as Off.
  Alternative: "Live" as one more choice among the grain samples. That choice is stored as a
  share of its range, so adding to it would move every saved Harp to Live.
- **Each voice records the last second of that generator (taken),** at the host rate through
  the half-band filter the voice's own output uses (nothing that would fold back when a grain
  plays it an octave up), and at the factory samples' three band limits (the full band, then 4th-
  order Butterworth low-passes at about a fifth and a tenth of the rate) for grains transposed
  further. About 0.6 MB a voice at 48 kHz, held by the voice (on the heap). Only the first Live
  Grain slot of a voice gets it. Alternatives: a pool the part hands out (D79's idea: less memory
  when few voices use it, but a piece of lifetime work of its own); a longer recording (more
  memory for a reach few clouds use).
- **What the controls mean for a live source (taken):** Position is how far back the cloud
  reaches, from one grain's length behind now (a grain transposed up must not catch up with the
  recording) to a second ago; Spray scatters around that; Scan at 0 freezes: the recording stops
  and the cloud keeps what it holds, any other Scan keeps it running. The source already sounds
  the note, so only the slot's own transposition (octave, semitone, fine, pitch modulation)
  moves the grains; Pitch Spray works as before. A fresh note clears the recording.
- Tests: the recording keeps 1 kHz whole and loses what lies above the host's band; its darker
  levels are 20 dB down where they should be; a cloud on a sine sounds its pitch, an octave up
  with the slot at +12; frozen, the cloud keeps its pitch while the source bends up a tone, and
  running, it follows.
- No factory preset uses it yet; every preset renders as before.

## D140. The interface starts: the MiniLab 3 as the panel (2026-11-14)

Phase 9 begins, decided with the user. The interface copies the Arturia MiniLab 3's layout and
drives the real one: four buttons and two touch strips on the left, a screen with the clickable
main knob under it (larger in software, same place), knobs 1 to 8 in two rows, four faders,
eight RGB pads, a short row of keys. A small screen with four soft knobs whose job changes per
page, mirrored on the screen, as on the M-VAVE FM-1 firmwares; never every parameter at once. A
simple, modern surface on the deep engine; an advanced editor much later. Navigation uses two
knobs: the main knob (with its press) and knob 5. Research by three agents on 2026-11-14: the
MiniLab 3's protocol (Ableton's own remote script and an Arturia forum post), the FM-1 firmwares
(Felucca, Melodee, Groove OS, ChoralRoot, CoralFlower, SLOOP, fm1-dock), and hardware and
software synth navigation (Elektron, Hydrasynth, OP-1, Polyend, NKS, Push, Analog Lab). Choices
the user made from pick lists:

- **Knobs 1 to 4 on a sound's home page: fixed roles, named by the preset (taken).** Knob 1
  tone, 2 character, 3 time, 4 movement on every sound; each preset names them and sets their
  range (Neon Brass: Blare, Thickness, Swell, Shimmer). Alternatives: free per-preset macros
  (Analog Lab; its most common complaint is vague names that differ per preset), fixed classic
  controls (cutoff, resonance, attack, release; dead on sounds without a filter). Consequence:
  every factory preset needs four macros written; none has any today.
- **Faders: the mixer (taken).** Part A, Part B, effects amount, master, with pickup in the
  software (the hardware has none). Alternatives: the amp envelope; drive, echo, space, master.
- **Knobs 6 to 8: Glide, Brightness, Space, the same on every page (taken).** Brightness and
  Space need a small engine hook (a tone tilt over the whole sound, an amount over the master
  effects). Alternatives: left free for MIDI learn; three more page knobs.
- **Pads: 1 to 4 open four colour-coded sections, 5 to 7 kept for the recorder (taken).**
  Sound, Shape, Motion, Effects; Stop, Play, Record keep their printed meaning for the later
  recorder. Alternatives: all eight as sections now; pads play notes and Shift navigates.

Facts from the research that bind the design: only the MiniLab's DAW program lets software
write its screen (two short text lines plus knob and fader pop-ups) and set any RGB colour per
pad; there, knobs 1 to 8 are endless but report absolute values, and the software can set a
knob's stored value so a page change never jumps; the main knob sends turn, press, Shift+turn
and Shift+press as separate messages, and Shift has its own. Whether Hold, Oct and the strips
reach the software is not known, so nothing depends on them.

Three clickable mock-ups that differ only in navigation are in `docs/ui-v3-mockups.html`: A,
section pages (knob 5 picks the slot, the main knob its pages); B, the main knob always browses
sounds and knob 5 walks every page of the section; C, a signal map the main knob crosses and
knob 5 descends. The user steers from there.

## D140. The Comb and FM4 (2026-11-14)

Asked what next ("Start designing the interface (Recommended)", "A preset that shows live
grains", "Make FM4 and Comb cheaper too", "Back to the Museum"), the user chose **"Make FM4 and
Comb cheaper too"** and **"A preset that shows live grains"**, with the rule of D138: the sound
stays exactly the same.

- **The Comb (taken):** it found each of its four read places with an integer remainder, and
  both channels worked the same places out again: nine divisions a sample. Now the places are
  worked out once for both lines, and wrapped with a comparison (the delay lies inside the line,
  so one wrap gives what the remainder gave). About 7 % of real time for eight notes to under 4 %.
- **FM4 (taken):** operators 2 and 3 skip their sine when, all block, their output meets only
  zeros (no level where they sound, no depth where they modulate). Operator 1 always sounds and
  operator 4 runs its own feedback memory, so they always run. A small gain, and only in patches
  that leave an operator out.
- **A faster sine for FM4, measured and dropped.** Its cost is four double sines a sample per
  voice; a reduced polynomial (Taylor to r^19, within 1.5e-15 of std::sin over FM4's phases) took
  13.4 ns a call against std::sin's 14.2 ns in this build: 6 % for output that is no longer bit
  for bit the same. Not worth it, so the user was not asked.
- Checked: all 118 presets rendered before and after; the 114 without randomness are bit for
  bit the same.

## D141. Two presets for live grains (2026-11-14)

The user's other choice of D140, "A preset that shows live grains". Taken without asking (the ear
judges them on the listening page):

- **Live Shimmer, Observatory (taken):** a soft three-voice saw pad (12 cents of detune) and a
  Grain slot set to Live an octave up, a quarter of a second behind the playing, 30 grains a
  second of 200 ms, scattered, wide, a few cents of pitch spray; a gentle ladder, a slow chorus
  and the long plate. The chord grows a shimmer of itself. Loudness as the others (-15.2 LUFS).
- **Frozen Pluck, Observatory (taken):** a plucked string and a Live Grain slot whose Scan the
  third envelope holds open for the pluck's first 0.9 s and then closes to 0, so the cloud
  freezes on what it recorded and holds it as a pad while the key is down. The cloud reads half
  a second into the pluck, where the string is still full; at first it read 0.15 s before the
  freeze and sat 16 dB under the pluck, now 4 to 6 dB. A plucked string is quiet, so the part is
  raised 6 dB and the loudness pass added 4.5 (limited by the pluck's peak, as for the other
  plucks).
- **On the listening page as a listening card (taken):** each preset playing a short phrase,
  then the same with its grain slot silenced (rendered from a bank of variants by
  tools/audition/make_livegrains.py), so the ear hears what the live grains add. Options: both
  good, one of them, or neither with a note.
- The Observatory room has seven presets now; the factory bank 120.

## D142. The interface, round two of the navigation mock-ups (2026-11-14)

The user's notes on round one (D140, models A, B, C): they like the signal map (C), the coloured
section pads Sound, Shape, Motion, Effects and the slots (A), and the pages. The main knob should
navigate inside every view and never sit idle; many menus need a back button; choosing a sound
was not easy, but can sit one level above. The pads need not copy the MiniLab's printing (Arp,
Pad, Prog): print what they do. Three new mock-ups, `docs/ui-v3-mockups-2.html`, published at
https://claude.ai/artifact/2bUoUQmdY8dg4VCxwC6mwe:

- **Common to all three (taken from the notes):** a sound list at the top level (turn to look,
  press to load, knob 5 a room, knobs 1-4 the playing sound's macros); pad 8 printed Back, tap
  one level up, hold to the sounds, Shift + pad 8 part A/B (the Hydrasynth's EXIT and HOME and
  Elektron's NO key work this way); the main knob's current job printed beside it; a trail under
  the screen title (Neon Brass › Shape › P1 Ladder › Filter) whose marks show which part the main
  knob and knob 5 move; pads printed Sound, Shape, Motion, Effects, Stop, Play, Record, Back.
- **D, map then open a block:** sounds › map › block. The main knob moves at the current level,
  press goes in, Back comes out, knob 5 moves the level above (room, section, block).
- **E, one map per section:** sounds › section. A pad draws only its section, wired as in the
  engine (generators mixed, shapers in a row, inserts then master). Main knob: slot; knob 5:
  pages; press: type.
- **F, map edited in place:** sounds › whole map. Main knob: block; knob 5: pages; press: type.
  Round one's C with the notes applied.
- Open, for the user: which of D, E, F; and whether pad 8 as Back is the right control.

## D143. The interface: navigation E, and sounds in categories (2026-11-14)

The user's choice after trying round two (D142):

- **E, one map per section (chosen):** a pad opens its section, and the screen draws only that
  section's slots as they are wired. Main knob: slot; knob 5: the slot's pages; press: type;
  knobs 1-4 edit at once. Rejected: D (whole map, press opens a block, knob 5 the level above)
  and F (whole map always on screen, knob 5 pages, nothing to open); both stay on the mock-up
  page for comparison.
- **Sounds in categories (the user's request):** the top level is the list of categories (the
  rooms). The main knob picks one and a press opens it; inside, the first row is "‹ Back", then
  the sounds: turn to look, press to load. Knob 5 steps to the next category without leaving.
  Pad 8 (Back) leaves a category; at the category list it does nothing.
- Pads printed with what they do (Sound, Shape, Motion, Effects; ■ ▶ ● for the recorder; Back),
  flat knobs, knobs 1-4 ringed in the open section's colour (the user's styling notes).
- Pad 8 as Back was not questioned again; the user used it as built.

## D148. The interface built (2026-11-14)

The user asked to document and build what was designed. `docs/interface-v3.md` is the
specification. Built, each with tests, Debug build clean, full suite passing:

- `src/interface/InterfaceModel`: the logic, independent of drawing, owned by the processor and
  shared by the panel and the MiniLab driver. Taken without asking: section pages come from each
  slot type's descriptor in fours, named after their first value; generators add Mix (level,
  pan, octave, fine) and, for G2/G3, Link (combine, amount); envelopes get Envelope, Timing and
  Curves pages, LFOs LFO and Sync; choosing a type loads that type's defaults (a preset does the
  same); lists step one option per detent, other values 1 % (0.1 % with Shift); the Effects fader
  moves both parts' effects share; knobs 7-8 show "later" until their engine hooks exist.
- `src/plugin/PanelEditor`: the panel, replacing the throwaway AuditionEditor (kept in the
  sources). Its own-files buttons (D137) are not on the new panel yet.
- `src/standalone/MiniLab3Driver` and `src/midi/MiniLab3Protocol.h`: the standalone app owns the
  MiniLab's DAW port, so its pads never play notes; keys, bend, mod wheel and pressure go on to the
  sound. Not yet verified on the device: Shift sending CC 27, the pads on channel 10.
- The Debug build installs the VST3 again (`COOLSYNTH_ENABLE_VST3_USER_INSTALL=ON` in the build
  cache; it had been off).

## D149. First use on the MiniLab: fixes (2026-11-14)

From the user's first session with the built interface:

- The main knob skipped an item going up: one click left sends 62, read as -2. Fixed (value - 63).
- Shift on the MiniLab (CC 27) now lights and acts on the panel's Shift. Oct and Hold send nothing,
  so the software cannot use them.
- Pads 5-7 white, green, pure red (the LED showed #f0524f as pink), at full brightness.
- The mouse on the screen: list rows, slots and page tabs are clicked, the knob cards dragged;
  sections got page tabs like Home's live pages.
- Notes now and then much quieter, better when hammered. Offline renders (48 and 44.1 kHz, runs,
  overlaps, four sounds) play evenly, so the engine is not it. The MiniLab runs firmware 1.2.0,
  for which Arturia users report random velocity-1 note-ons. The driver replaces a velocity of 1
  or less with the previous note's and logs raw velocities, to confirm on the user's unit. The
  keyboard velocity curve the user chose (Soft / Normal / Hard) waits until the log shows whether
  it is still needed.
- No sound at first: the app's output was the computer's own speakers; the user chose the USB card.
- The render tool takes --rate and --block, to render as a live device runs.

## D150. The flight recorder (2026-11-15)

The user asked for tripwires and a log, so problems need no explaining after a session; it must
not slow the audio. Built as the standard pattern for real-time audio (a lock-free ring from the
audio thread to a logging thread, as JACK, PortAudio and CoreAudio hand data off):
`src/diagnostics/FlightRecorder.*`. Fixed-size records, one atomic add to claim a slot, no locks
or allocation; a background thread writes `%LOCALAPPDATA%\CoolSynth\logs\session-*.log` four times
a second, the newest 20 kept; when it falls behind the oldest records are overwritten and counted.
Tripwires: a block over half its time (slow) or over all of it (dropout) with the engine's slowest
stage; NaN or infinity in the output (silenced and counted); a summary each second (worst block,
voices, peak, samples into the clipper). Events: notes with velocity, presets loaded, instruments
asked for, read in (size, time) and dropped, MiniLab connected or unplugged, velocity-1 fixes. On
in the app and the plugin; in tools and tests with COOLSYNTH_FLIGHT_LOG set. The render tool also
gained --history (sounds played first, --history-chords, --history-period), --background-load and
block timing, to reproduce what a player does.

## D151. A choice never slides (2026-11-15)

Found with the flight recorder: after playing one sound, switching to a recorded instrument stalled
the audio 14 to 145 ms (Init Wavetable before Violins: 145 ms every time). Every slot value glides to
its new setting over 10 ms, choices too; the sample slot's "which instrument" slid from the old
value to the new one and asked the library for every instrument in between, among them the 979 MB
Steinway. Fixed in Voice::applySettings: a value whose unit is a choice jumps. Worst block after the
same switches: under 1.1 ms (limit 10.9 ms at 44.1 kHz, 480 samples).

## D152. No giant allocations while audio plays (2026-11-15)

Found with the flight recorder and --background-load: while the library read an instrument, the
audio stalled 115 to 142 ms, though the two threads share no code. The loader's one allocation of
the whole instrument (979 MB for the Steinway) took Windows 115 ms, and the audio thread waited
exactly as long. (First tried: reserving without zero-filling; no change, so not the cause.) Fixed:
each recording its own memory (LoadedStyle::addRecording). A synth playing through the Steinway's
read now peaks at 3.6 ms a block (alone: 1.2 ms). Recordings are read identically (Steinway's
render peak unchanged to 0.00001 dB). Streaming from disk, as large samplers do, is the later step.

## D153. One loudness for every preset, with room for chords (2026-11-15)

The user heard the Horn distort on chords high up (Octave + once, the top keys) and found some
instruments much quieter than others. Asked how other instruments do it (Kurzweil keeps about
6 dB for many voices; the Solaris has a Poly Level; Pianoteq's limiter is only a safety valve;
nothing divides a note's level by the number of notes), the user chose to level every preset
evenly and to choose that level so chords keep their room, the soft clipper kept as the safety
net: "do that for all". This replaces D103's "half way" for the recorded instruments.

The tool (`tools/NormalizePresets.cpp`) now measures:
- **Loudness**: the loudest 400 ms of the test chord (BS.1770 momentary loudness), not the 4 s
  average. A struck chord is heard by its attack; the 4 s average had judged the pianos and
  mallets 5 to 8 dB quieter than they sound, so they were held low and the pads high. (Studies
  of the Glasberg-Moore model point the same way: the maximum of short-term loudness predicts
  how loud a brief sound is heard.)
- **The test chord's peak** (four notes, velocity 100, middle of the range) at most -7.9 dBFS:
  6 dB under the clipper's knee (-1.9 dBFS).
- **New: the worst peak of a full-velocity chord at six places over the preset's range** (the
  recorded notes of an instrument, else C2 to C6), at most -2 dBFS, under the clipper. This is
  what the Horn broke: its test chord peaked at -5.9 dBFS, but at full velocity near the top of
  its range the same chord reached +6.6 dB over full scale before the clipper (12.6 dB more).
  Steinway +5.5, Old Upright +7.6, Trombone +5.8, Balafon +5.9.
- All levels are measured 40 dB down and lifted back, so the clipper never bends what is
  measured.
- Five takes of the test chord, the median loudness and the highest peak: presets that play
  random notes (Rain Chime, Random Walk, Satellite Ping) measured up to 5.5 dB apart from one
  4-second take to the next.

The common loudness: **-20 LUFS (chosen)**. Offered by the numbers: -18 (83 of 120 presets reach
it), -19 (93), -20 (101), -21 (108). The 19 that cannot reach -20 stay as loud as their two
limits allow: most within 3 dB (Horn and Trombone -22.6, Steinway -21.0); the percussive effects
lower (Static Hit -28.8, Balafon -26.8, Power-Up -25.7). Everything is quieter than before (the
synths about 4 dB, the pianos about 8 dB, the ones that clipped most): turn the speakers up.
The crest-factor boost of the old tool is gone: the momentary measure does that job.

## D154. Home blinks while an instrument loads; the keys wait (2026-11-15)

The user: a blinking Home "until the next instrument loads, and you can't do much while that's
happening. And it needs to be completely ready before you start. This is acceptable for loading
in wave instruments. We'll figure out later how to optimize." Built:
- Each block, before the keys, the processor asks whether a playing part's sampled instrument is
  still being read (`EngineV3::sampledInstrumentPending`; one not installed does not count).
  While it is, a key is ignored; notes already sounding go on. Checked before the keys, so even
  the first block after a preset change holds them back.
- Home blinks (about 1.7 times a second) on the MiniLab and on the panel; the screen's small line
  says "Loading..."; the panel's hint line says the keys play once Home stops blinking.
- The flight recorder logs when loading starts and ends, and how many keys were ignored.
- Taken from the real-time review on the way: the sample library is built when the part is
  prepared, no longer on the audio thread at its first use.

## D155. Sample memory as memory banks (2026-11-15)

The user asked how hardware instruments hold their samples. Kurzweil K2600: factory samples in
ROM, the user's in a sample RAM (up to 128 MB) loaded from disk at power-up; a program using RAM
samples does not play until they are loaded. Nord Stage: samples in fast flash (1 GB on the
Stage 4), a program only names a sample's place, so switching is instant. Yamaha Montage: 1.75 GB
of user flash for waveforms (3.8 GB on the Montage M). Korg Kronos: an instrument in RAM, or
streamed from the SSD when it does not fit. The pattern: one sample memory of a fixed size, filled
ahead of playing; presets point into it. Chosen with the user, four pick lists:
- **How a recording is held: at its own depth, lossless (chosen, recommended)**; or as floats, as
  now. 16-bit recordings stay 16-bit, 24-bit ones packed 24-bit: the whole library is 3.84 GB
  instead of 6.0 GB, bit for bit the same sound. The voice converts as it reads.
- **Size: half the free memory at start, at most the whole library (chosen, recommended), with a
  warning when the computer is too small** (the user's addition); or always 4 GB; or a setting.
  Taken once at start and never given back while playing. The user: "assume more sampled stuff
  will follow later, so not everything might fit into the RAM".
- **What fills it: what you choose (chosen, recommended)**: an instrument is read in when a sound
  that needs it is loaded, and stays; the last sound's instruments are read in at start. Or: a
  room's instruments when entering it; or everything at start.
- **When full: reuse the instrument played longest ago (chosen, recommended)**; or say "Memory
  full" and let the user unload one.

## D156. The instrument knob loads only when clicked (2026-11-15)

The user, on the real-time review's finding that turning a Sound slot's instrument knob loads
every instrument it passes: "dont load instruments while just browsing with the knob. only when i
click it and then the blinking starts. can you make it glowing at least in the s/w UI?" So turning
the instrument or style knob of a sampled slot only shows the choice; pressing the main knob, or
clicking the knob on the panel, loads it. Home now glows (a smooth pulse) instead of blinking.

## D157. A preset reaches the audio whole (2026-11-15)

From the real-time review, chosen by the user ("Presets arrive whole"). A preset load set every
parameter to its default and then to the preset's value, hundreds of separate changes, while the
audio thread read the parameters one by one each block: a block could play half the old sound
and half the new, switch a slot's type twice, or read the previous type's first value as an
instrument number and load an instrument no preset uses. Now every bulk edit (a preset, a saved
state, an undo step, the interface's type change) runs inside a `BulkEdit` scope that makes a
sequence number odd while it lasts. The audio thread reads the parameters into a second settings
buffer only while the number is even, and keeps what it read only if the number did not change
meanwhile (a seqlock); otherwise the last whole settings play one more block. A preset therefore
lands at most a block later (10.9 ms at 480 samples), and never mixed. The offline helpers that
read the patch's instruments now use their own copy of the settings, not the audio thread's.

## D158. Earlier sounds ring on after a preset change, Kronos-style (2026-11-15)

From the real-time review ("Ringing notes survive"): a preset change re-applied the new preset to
every sounding voice, so ringing notes morphed (type crossfades, new envelopes and filters) and a
sampled note stopped dead with a click. A first version kept each voice's own settings; the user
asked for "a proper investigation into side effects, edge cases, potential problems, bugs and
mitigations". The independent review found it short: the same key or a mono/legato note played
again morphed the old voice; a preset with fewer voices stole the old notes; the old notes went
through the new preset's effects and part modulators (global LFOs, global envelope, step, macros,
bend range); an evicted note faded with the new settings; eviction ignored sounds already silent; a
load that changed nothing used up a slot; a play- or part-mode change released held notes; user
files were dropped under a ringing note. The references: Korg Kronos "Smooth Sound Transitions"
keeps the current and the previous sound with their effects, a third stops the oldest, a Hold Time
(default 5 s, at least about half a second) after release, and only sustain, sostenuto, bend and note
gate reach the old sound; Yamaha Montage "Seamless Sound Switching" (a FIFO of two), Nord Stage,
Roland Fantom "Scene Remain" and Omnisphere's Live Mode keep the old sound's effect tails too.
Chosen with the user, four pick lists:
- **A previous sound as a whole, Kronos-style (chosen, recommended)**; or patch the per-voice version.
  A sound unit (`src/synth/v3/SoundUnit.*`) is everything a preset sets: both parts, their inserts,
  the master effects F1..F6, the mix and the master gain. The engine keeps four.
- **Two earlier sounds, like the Montage (chosen)**; one like the Kronos and Nord was recommended.
- **Five seconds of hold, the Kronos default (chosen)**; half a second was recommended, or 10 ms.
  After its keys and the pedal are up an earlier sound rings five seconds more, then fades over half
  a second; so does the oldest when a third earlier sound would ring or when a block takes over 75 %
  of its time; silent a quarter of a second, it ends at once.
- **Its own master effects, kept (chosen, recommended)**; or the new ones, faded.
Routing: a note-on goes to the sound played; its release to the sound that took the key; the
sustain pedal, pitch bend, All Notes Off and Reset Controllers to every sound; the mod wheel,
pressure and expression only to the sound played; All Sound Off ends every earlier sound. A unit
that starts playing a new sound is told where the pedal, bend and wheels are. A load whose settings
equal the last block's (a host restoring the state, the same preset again) keeps no earlier sound.
The user's own files a replaced sound used are let go only once that sound has ended. Memory: four
units of everything, about 38 MB of effect buffers each at 48 kHz, taken at start; an idle unit costs
no time. Tests: `tests/V3EarlierSoundTests.cpp` (held chord keeps its VA generator while the new
preset is FM, a new note plays the new sound, releases reach the old sound, the pedal holds it,
a third earlier sound fades the oldest, All Sound Off, a mono preset leaves a poly chord held).

## D159. JUCE's locks kept off the audio thread (2026-11-15)

The real-time review found five places where JUCE takes a lock, allocates or posts a message from
the audio thread; the user asked for them to be dealt with. JUCE stays: each has a workaround.
- **A message posted from processBlock** (AsyncUpdater for mapped controllers and learned chords:
  a lock, a possible allocation and PostMessage). Now one atomic flag that a 100 Hz message-thread
  timer reads. A mapped controller move reaches its parameter up to 10 ms later.
- **The on-screen keyboard's state** (MidiKeyboardState::processNextMidiBuffer on the audio thread
  shares a lock with mouse clicks, and may grow the host's MIDI buffer). The audio thread no longer
  touches it: clicked keys reach the audio through a lock-free queue, straight into the engine's
  own event array; keys the audio heard come back through another queue and the timer shows them.
- **The standalone player's MidiMessageCollector** (a lock shared with the MIDI thread; late
  messages squeezed into the end of the block). The MiniLab's keys, bend and pressure now go into
  the processor's own lock-free queue, timestamped on arrival, and play one block later at their
  exact distance apart. Other MIDI devices still go through JUCE's collector.
- **Host blocks longer than prepared**: the engine grew its buffers on the audio thread. Now it
  renders such a block in prepared-size pieces, each with its own events and beat position.
- **The parameter listener lock** (JUCE's VST3 wrapper takes it per parameter during automation):
  inherent to JUCE. CoolSynth's listener only stores atomics, and the panel polls instead of
  adding listeners, so the message thread rarely takes that lock while audio runs. No change.

## D160. No big memory wipes inside an audio block (2026-11-15)

From the real-time review, chosen by the user ("No big wipes in the audio"):
- Every note start wiped its voice's one-second live recording (three levels, about 0.6 MB at
  48 kHz, 1.2 MB at 96 kHz), though only a Grain slot set to Live reads it: a 32-note chord with
  unison wiped up to 37 MB in one block. The recording now counts the frames it has written since
  the note began, and anything older reads as silence: the same sound, no memory touched.
- All Sound Off and panic wiped every effect of all 18 effect slots (delay lines of 4 s, the grain
  buffer, the room: about 38 MB at 48 kHz) in one block; many hosts send All Sound Off when the
  transport stops. Each slot now remembers which of its effects played since its last wipe and
  wipes only those; a slot that stayed off costs nothing.

## D161. The allocation alarm and the audio scenario tests (2026-11-15)

From the real-time review, chosen by the user ("Allocation alarm in tests", "Scenario tests").
processBlock marks its thread for the block (`diag::AudioBlockScope`, one thread-local store); the
test program replaces operator new and counts every allocation made on a marked thread
(`tests/AudioThreadAlarm.cpp`; a self-test proves the replacement is the one in use). The plugin
and the app replace nothing. `tests/AudioScenarioTests.cpp` plays, through the whole processor:
every preset with a chord and a switch to the next preset while it still rings (120 switches);
All Sound Off and panic while chords and effects ring; 32 note-ons in one block on every preset;
blocks of 1, 37 and 129 samples and blocks longer than prepared (1024, 4096). Each fails on any
allocation or any NaN or infinity, and reports its worst block. First run: no allocation, no
broken value; worst block 1.6 ms over the switches, 5.9 ms with 32 note-ons on the heaviest preset
(a 256-sample block lasts 5.3 ms; the user's 480-sample block 10.9 ms); All Sound Off 0.05 ms.
Still to add: a click detector for the switches, once effect changes fade (the next step).

## D162. The sample memory keeps the memory check; a track mode kept possible (2026-11-15)

The user wondered whether a fixed 1 GB beats D155's memory check, given a possible 4-track
recorder inside CoolSynth, and asked how DAWs and workstations play several tracks, whether four
tracks need four memories, and whether the computer has the power. Found (details and sources in
`docs/sample-memory-and-tracks.md`):
- One sample memory per program: `SampleLibrary::instance()` is a static, so tracks in the app
  and plugin copies in one host process share it, an instrument on two tracks held once (the
  Digitakt II's 16 tracks share 400 MB the same way). A host that runs each copy in its own
  process (Bitwig's "Individually") gives each its own.
- DAWs compute every instrument track live and add them; Freeze renders a track to audio;
  ASIO-Guard and REAPER compute the tracks not armed ahead in a large buffer. Workstations
  (Kronos, Montage M, Digitakt) load everything with the song, so choosing a track only moves
  the keys: switching is instant.
- Measured here (Ryzen 7 3700X, 8 cores): one CoolSynth at 16 to 17 voices, worst block 2.64 ms
  of 10.88 ms, a quarter of a core; the heaviest preset with 32 note-ons 5.9 ms (D161). Four
  tracks on one core would drop out; on four cores they use about an eighth of the machine.
- A song holds every track's instruments at once, up to eight: Steinway 0.73 GB, Old Trombone
  about 0.3 GB, Cellos and Tenor Sax about 0.22 GB each, the library 3.84 GB.

Chosen by the user, three pick lists:
- **Size: the memory check (chosen, recommended)**, as D155: half the free memory at start, at
  most the whole library, a warning when the computer is too small; here 3.84 GB, everything in.
  Or a fixed 1 GB: the same on every computer, but Steinway and Trombone do not fit together,
  sound changes reload more often, and a 4-track song mostly would not fit unless the tracks not
  played become audio. Or the memory check with a setting to lower it.
- **Track mode: the door kept open (chosen, recommended).** Nothing built; decided after the
  engine, presets, arp and interface. Three rules hold from now on:
  1. One sample memory per program, shared by every engine in it.
  2. An instrument a loaded sound names is never reused while that sound is loaded, however long
     it has not played. When full, only instruments no loaded sound names are reused, played
     longest ago first; a sound (later a song) that needs more than the memory says which
     instrument does not fit, never a silent stall. This sharpens D155's "when full".
  3. The engine keeps no globals besides the sample memory, so engines can run side by side.
  Or never (CoolSynth stays one instrument; a DAW does tracks), or design it now (against "UI
  last").
- **The tracks not played, if it comes: all live, frozen on demand (chosen, recommended).** Each
  track its own engine on its own core; switching only moves the keys; a track can be frozen to
  audio to free its processor time. Needs parallel rendering in the app. Or only the played track
  live and the rest as audio (least processor, 1 GB would do, but leaving a track renders it,
  about 45 s per 3 minutes, and going back reloads its instruments); or no leaning yet.

The user noted that tiny boxes do four tracks: community firmwares for the M-VAVE FM-1 (12 voices,
a small dual-core chip), such as KARMA. Their tracks hold notes and their engines compute sound
from small tables, so tracks cost them nothing; what costs is each voice's sound, and CoolSynth's
costs more by choice. Kept for the track mode's design: one voice budget shared by all tracks, as
the FM-1 and the Kronos have, instead of sixteen per track.

## D163. The sample library pre-processed: FLAC decoded in parallel, in a container of our own (2026-11-15)

The user wanted the library pre-processed once, compressed with a fast lossless codec, with
pre-computed metadata, reusing what exists online if its quality holds ("otherwise we write it
ourselves"). Researched and measured on this PC (Ryzen 7 3700X): the library is 2,211 recordings,
3,913 MB at its own depth, 1,390 MB as today's FLAC (written by ffmpeg, some with odd block sizes).
libFLAC 1.4.3, which JUCE bundles (BSD, fuzzed by OSS-Fuzz), decodes 294 MB/s of 24-bit sound on
one core without JUCE's reader; one recording per task, the Steinway takes 2.62 s on one thread and
0.30 s on 16, the whole library 1.7 s (today: 3.5 s for the Steinway). Nothing permissive is both
smaller and faster: WavPack, ALAC, Monkey's Audio and TTA are slower or bigger, HISE's HLAC is GPL,
OptimFROG and TAK closed; a codec of our own would only pay as bit-packing (NCW, HLAC), 6 to 25 % bigger.
Chosen: **libFLAC, decoded in parallel, in a container of our own (recommended)**: one packed file
per style, a header and index (zones, loops, levels, each recording's offset and exact size in
memory), then its recordings re-encoded as FLAC with standard 4,096-frame blocks (the marimba
33 % smaller, the library 2.5 %), decoded straight into the sample memory on (cores minus two)
low-priority threads. Also offered: measuring dr_flac first (one download; under 50 ms to gain), or
our own bit-packing codec. Six 24-bit styles hold 16-bit sound; they stay 24-bit (lossless).

## D164. Multi-core (2026-11-15)

The user: "make sure this software is multi-core able and aware". Everything audible ran on the
audio thread. Researched: u-he Diva, Kontakt, Pianoteq and Pigments offer multi-core as an option,
most off by default in a DAW, where the host already spreads tracks over the cores; the standalone
is its own host with cores to spare. The natural parallel unit here is the sound unit (D158): the
sound played and the earlier ones are independent; summed in a fixed order the output stays bit
for bit the same. Windows: workers registered with MMCSS "Pro Audio" like JUCE's audio thread,
woken per block, the audio thread taking tasks too, so a late worker costs no more than one core.
Chosen, two pick lists:
- **Background now, sound units in parallel next (chosen, recommended)**: sample loading on (cores
  minus two) low-priority threads, the levelling and render tools run presets in parallel, the CPU
  detected (cores, threads, cache groups) and logged; then the sound units on separate cores with
  a setting Auto/Off. Or the parallel sound units first; or background only.
- **The plugin: Auto, like the standalone (chosen; Off with a setting was recommended, as Kontakt,
  Diva and Steinberg advise)**. Kept in mind: one pool for every CoolSynth in a host process, small
  (2 to 3 workers, the 32-thread MMCSS limit), serial when the host renders offline.

## D165. An effect type change fades (2026-11-15)

From the real-time review, chosen by the user ("Effect changes fade"). A preset change no longer
touches the old sound's effects (D158: its own sound unit keeps them). What was left: changing an
effect slot's type while playing switched at once, cutting the old effect's output and clearing
its memory in one block, a click. Now a slot (an insert or a master slot) fades: the old effect,
with the settings it last had, gives way to the dry signal over 10 ms, its memory is cleared, and
the new one comes in over 10 ms. The effect hears its input faded with it, so its delay lines fill
and empty gradually: fading only its output left a step where a chorus's delayed copy began 5 ms
in (measured 0.071 against the tone's own 0.016). Changed again mid-way, it turns back from where
it got to. An emptied slot drops its tail over the same 10 ms. A slot whose last block was silent
(a fresh sound's first block) switches at once, so a new sound's first note gets its effects
whole. The old effect's tail is not kept: keeping it would need a second set of effect memory per
slot. Test: a steady 220 Hz tone through a delay that becomes a chorus shows no step larger than
the tone's own (0.0149 against 0.0159, `V3InsertTypeChangeTests`).

## D166. The memory banks built (2026-11-15)

Carries out D155, D162 and D163. `tools/PackLibrary.cpp` packs the library once: each style one
`style.cspack` (its table, then every recording re-encoded as FLAC at level 8 with 4,096-frame
blocks, at its own depth and channel count), and `library.index` with every style's exact size in
memory. Each recording is decoded back and compared number for number before the pack is written.
Measured on this PC: 76 styles in 28.6 s on 14 threads, 0 failed; 3.65 GB in memory (mono kept
mono: 0.2 GB less than D155's 3.84 GB), 1,262 MB packed against 1,327 MB of today's FLAC files.
`SampleMemory` takes the memory once (the app at start, else the first ask), touched and locked a
16 MB slice at a time; `SampleLibrary` plans it from the index, reads a style with all cores but
two (one recording per task, low priority), places it in runs of 1 MB pages, and reuses pages only
from a style nothing asked for in two seconds and no note plays (each note counts itself a user;
an epoch scope around every audio block keeps memory a block may hold). A note keeps the style it
started with to its end. A style bigger than the memory, or one with no room next to what the
loaded sounds use, is said on the screen, not left silent. Measured with a chord playing in real
time at 44.1 kHz, 480 samples: the memory (3.8 GB, locked) in 2.0 s; the Steinway (734 MB) read in
0.46 s, where it took 3.64 s; worst block 4.2 ms of 10.9. Unpacked styles (the tests' stand-in
library) are still read from their manifests, also at their own depth.

## D167. Multi-core built (2026-11-15)

Carries out D164. Aware: `CpuLayout` reads the processor (here 8 cores, 16 threads, 2 cache groups,
one efficiency class) and logs it at start. Background: a style is read on all cores but two (D166);
the packer and the levelling tool work on all cores but two. Audio: `AudioWorkers`, one pool for the
whole program, three helpers (never more than the physical cores less two), each registered with
MMCSS "Pro Audio" like the audio thread, with flush-to-zero, asleep on an address until a block wakes
them. When more than one sound unit sounds (D158), each renders on its own core into its own buffer
and the buffers are added in unit order, so the output is the same bit for bit (tested). The audio
thread takes tasks too; the block's number and its next task are claimed together, so a worker that
wakes late cannot take a task of another block. A second CoolSynth in the same program renders on its
own thread while the first uses the helpers. Off while a host renders offline; Auto by default in the
app and the plugin (the user's choice); COOLSYNTH_MULTICORE=0 switches it off. Measured: 32 note-ons in
one block on every preset after a switch, worst block 3.3 ms instead of 6.9 ms; 120 switches 1.7 ms
instead of 2.0 ms (256-sample blocks, 5.3 ms each). The allocation alarm watches the helpers too.
The app's Options button opens a menu: Audio and MIDI settings, then Multi-core Auto or Off (kept in the
app's settings, Auto when never chosen; COOLSYNTH_MULTICORE=0 still wins), and the CPU it found. The plugin
stays on Auto until the real interface has a place for it. Still to do: the load per
core on the screen.

## D168. The same sound in every program: no stray randomness, no global counts (2026-11-15)

Found by running the levelling tool in parallel (D167): some presets measured differently than at
the re-level (D153). Bisected against the re-level build, rendering note for note:
- **Vinyl Choir differed from its 7th sample** (peak 2.6 dB lower): since the sound units (D158) a
  unit's prepare ended in a panic, whose voice reset draws the free-running random values again (LFO
  phases, drift, note randoms), so every preset started from other random values than it was
  auditioned and levelled with. Prepare now leaves freshly prepared voices alone; Vinyl Choir is the
  re-level's again, bit for bit, with and without its effects. Frozen Pluck matched bit for bit;
  Old Trombone within -138 dB (the native-depth playback of D166 rounds once, at the end).
- **Results that changed from run to run**, only with several processors in one program: the noise,
  grain, string and wavetable generators each took their random seed from a count shared by the
  whole program, and the sampled instruments' takes came round on one shared count. Against D162
  rule 3 (no globals besides the sample memory): two CoolSynths, or two tracks, would change each
  other's noise and take order. Now each voice and slot has its own seed, from the voice's (SlotMemory),
  and each part counts its own takes. A side effect: those generators' noise is another random
  sequence than before, the same kind of noise. The arpeggiator's random patterns are still seeded
  from the clock, on purpose: an arp plays differently each time.
- Checked on the way: no value is read before it is written (rendered after filling and freeing
  memory with a pattern: the same output).
Tools: the render tool takes --dirty-heap and --no-fx; the levelling tool --only and --threads.

The user asked for two things in the plan (nothing built yet):

- **Engine pills**: in the software sound browser only, each sound's row shows, right-aligned, small

  - Words: **technical short (chosen): VA, Sample, FM, WT, Grain, Physical, Noise**; or plain names
    (Analog, Sampled, FM, Wavetable, Granular, Modelled, Noise), recommended; or like Pigments
    (Virtual Analog, Sample, FM, Wavetable, Granular, Modal, Noise).
  - Noise: **only when it is the sound (chosen, recommended)**, when no other engine plays; or always.

## D170. The review's leftovers: broken values per stage, no hanging notes, softer steals, gliding reverb (2026-11-15)

Overnight, the user away (taken: the recommended option each time; the others listed). What the
real-time review (D161) left open:
- **NaN or infinity inside a stage.** Before: only the final output was checked and silenced, so a
  broken value in a filter's or a reverb's state stayed there and kept that part silent until a
  panic. Options: **check after every stage (each part's voices, each insert, each master slot),
  silence the bad samples and reset only that stage (chosen)**; keep the output check alone; reset
  the whole engine. Cost: one pass over the block per stage. The flight recorder writes "BROKEN
  VALUES in part A inserts: n samples, that stage was reset". Not covered by a test: nothing in the
  factory sounds makes a broken value to test with.
- **A flood of MIDI in one block** (1024 events) dropped whatever came last, a key's release too:
  a hanging note. Options: **keep the block's last 64 slots for what ends sound (note-off, All Notes
  Off, All Sound Off, Reset Controllers, the pedal's lift) and count what is dropped (chosen)**; a
  bigger list (still finite); dropping the oldest controller moves instead (more work per event).
  Tested: 1100 wheel moves then four releases at the block's end; before, 4 notes hung, now none.
  The recorder writes "MIDI FLOOD: n events found no room".
- **Text formatting on the audio thread**: the once-a-second summary formatted its peak with
  snprintf (locale, possible locks). Now the record carries a third number and the writer thread
  formats it. Options: **a third number (chosen)**; pack it into an existing field (none was free);
  leave it.
- **The steal fade** (a voice taken for a new note) was 1.5 ms, short enough to tick on low notes.
  Options: **5 ms (chosen)**, 3 ms, 10 ms. The SFZ format's default for the same job is 6 ms
  (sfzformat.com, off_time); JUCE forum posts report a couple of ms to 10 ms. Side effect: the new
  note on a stolen voice starts 3.5 ms later.
- **Reverb settings stepped**: a change of size, damping or pre-delay jumped at once, a click in a
  ringing tail. Now size and damping glide over about 20 ms and the pre-delay over about 50 ms,
  read between two samples so it can move smoothly (the usual fix for a delay time, CCRMA's
  delay-line interpolation pages; 20-50 ms smoothing as reported on the Renoise forum). Options:
  **glide (chosen)**; step (before); crossfade two reverbs (twice the cost). A fresh or reset reverb
  starts at its settings, so a preset sounds bit for bit as before.
- **Old tails coming back**: a chorus or reverb whose mix faded to nothing kept its last state, and
  the old tail played again when the mix came back. Now it is emptied when it falls silent.
  Options: **empty it (chosen)**; keep it (before).

## D171. The click detector, and effect fades without corners (2026-11-15)

Overnight, the user away (recommended options taken, the others listed). The tests now listen for
clicks (tests/ClickDetectorTests.cpp), through the whole processor, on the factory presets:
- **How a click is told**: roughness, the largest second difference (|x[n] - 2x[n-1] + x[n-2]|), in
  the 20-30 ms after an event, against the sound's own: in the 200 ms before, once settled after,
  and in the same render without the event (or with a second voice free, for a steal). A click is
  half again as rough (1.5x) and above -60 dB. Options: **second difference (chosen)**, it hears a
  step and a corner and little of a low tone; first difference (hears bass as roughness); a
  spectral flux measure (more code, no clearer). The test checks itself on a sine with a -34 dB step.
- **What it plays**: a preset change while a chord rings, on all 120 (worst 1.00x: the earlier sound
  is untouched, D158); an effect type change in a part insert and a master slot on every sixth
  preset, every type in turn (worst 0.99x); a stolen voice on all 120, one voice and a second key
  (worst 1.31x, Harp String); an instrument read in the background while an earlier sampled sound
  rings (1.00x; the stand-in recordings are short, so one of eight pairs overlaps). About 90 s of
  the test run. COOLSYNTH_CLICK_ONLY=name runs one preset, COOLSYNTH_CLICK_PROFILE=1 prints a
  finding in 2 ms frames.
- **Found and fixed**: an effect type change bent the waveform where the old effect's fade-out met
  the new one's fade-in (Wine Glasses, master reverb to LoFi, 1.90x). The straight-line gains rose
  into that point and fell after it, a corner. Now the gain is a raised cosine, flat where it
  starts and ends (1.00x). Options: **raised cosine (chosen)**; a longer straight fade (the corner
  smaller, not gone); a square root of the gain (tried: worse, 10.8x, it starts infinitely steep).
  On the way, a fade-in now fades the effect's output too, so what an effect makes of its own
  (LoFi's hiss and crackle) rises instead of starting at full level, and the dry signal takes
  1 - s*s, so a fade no longer dips by up to 2.5 dB halfway.
- **Not a click after all**: a steal on Future Chord looked rougher than the same note played
  fresh (1.81x), but the fresh note had empty chorus and reverb. Against the same render with a
  second voice free it is 0.85x; the test uses that reference.
Sources: the SFZ format's 6 ms default fade for stolen voices (sfzformat.com, off_time); delay-line
interpolation and smoothing (CCRMA, J. O. Smith, Physical Audio Signal Processing); the raised cosine
over a straight ramp, for a slope without a jump (linux-audio-dev, "Noiseless volume changes", 2013;
the Audacity manual, Fade and Crossfade).

## D172. The flight recorder: each thread's second, late helpers, memory (2026-11-15)

Overnight, the user away (recommended options taken, the others listed). The log now says, once a
second:
- **Each thread's share of the second**: the audio thread's blocks, and each helper's tasks (the
  pool times every task it gives out, D164). "audio thread 6.7% of the second, helpers shared 150
  blocks, worst wait for one 0.30 ms"; "helper 2 3.5% of the second, worst wake 0.01 ms". A helper
  line only in a second where helpers were used or one woke late. The same numbers are kept for
  the screen (SynthAudioProcessor::cpuLoad, step 6 of the night).
- **Late helpers**: a helper waking more than 1 ms after its block began counts as late ("LATE n
  times"). Options for the line: **1 ms (chosen)**, about a fifth of a 256-sample block; 0.5 ms
  (noisy on a loaded system); half the block (too late to warn).
- **Memory**: the working set, its peak, the committed memory and the page faults since the last
  line, read by the writer thread (GetProcessMemoryInfo; never the audio thread). A line when pages
  were faulted in or the working set moved by a megabyte, else every ten seconds. Page faults count
  soft ones too: Windows' PROCESS_MEMORY_COUNTERS does not tell hard from soft (Microsoft's psapi
  documentation); a split would need the performance counters. Options: **the process counters
  (chosen)**, one call, no setup; performance counters (hard faults apart, a query to set up and
  keep); nothing.
- Options for where the per-thread numbers come from: **timing inside the pool (chosen)**, exact and
  allocation-free; the system's thread times (GetThreadTimes, coarse at 15.6 ms ticks); none.
With two CoolSynths in one host the helpers are shared, so each sees the part of their work that
falls in its own seconds. Tested: a second of two overlapping sounds counts work on the audio thread
and on the helpers (here 6.66%, 0.47%, 3.48%, 1.70%).

## D175. A velocity curve for the keyboard (2026-11-15)

Overnight, the user away (recommended options taken, the others listed), as assumed before the
night: in Options, for every key that reaches the sound (the host's MIDI, the MiniLab, the screen),
Normal the default (as before). Soft: a light touch plays loud; Normal: as played; Hard: loud takes
a firm touch - the words and meaning makers use (Kawai's Light and Heavy, Yamaha's Soft, Roland;
Sound On Sound, "Which MIDI velocity curve should I use"). The shapes are power curves, v^0.6 and
v^1.6, that keep the softest and the hardest touch where they are: velocity 32 plays as 56 (Soft)
or 14 (Hard); never 0, which would end the note. Remembered like Multi-core: in the app's settings,
per computer for the plugin (D174).
- Options for the curves: **three, Soft, Normal, Hard (chosen)**; five (Light+ to Heavy+, as Kawai);
  a free amount (a knob, for the advanced editor later); Fixed (every note the same, for organs:
  left for later, a sound's own velocity amount does that per preset).
- Options for where: **all keyboards (chosen)**; the MiniLab alone (a host's keyboard has its own
  curve in the host, but many hosts have none).
- Options for the shape: **power curves (chosen)**, smooth, ends kept; a fixed table per curve (no
  gain); an S curve (keeps both ends less steep, for few keyboards).

## D176. Brightness and Space, knobs 7 and 8 (2026-11-15)

Overnight, the user away (recommended options taken, the others listed; ranges kept conservative,
as said before the night). Two global parameters, -100 % to +100 %, the middle "as the sound": a
preset sounds as designed, and every preset loads with both in the middle.
- **Brightness** moves the cutoff of every Ladder and SVF filter of the sound together, up to 1.5
  octaves either way (0.15 of the cutoff's ten-octave range), added like a modulation, so the
  filters ramp it per sample. Options for the range: **1.5 octaves (chosen)**; 1 (too little on a
  dark pad); 2.5 (a bass turns into a lead). Options for what it moves: **the filters (chosen)**;
  a tilt EQ on the output (works on sounds without a filter, but colours rather than opens);
  both. A sound without a Ladder or SVF (most recorded instruments) does not change: an EQ for
  those is for later, if the user wants it.
- **Space** makes every reverb, delay and room of the sound (part inserts and master slots) up
  to twice as wet or half as wet (their mix times 2^value, at most 100 %); the effects smooth
  their mix themselves. Options: **scale the wet share (chosen)**; add a reverb of its own when a
  sound has none (changes the sound's character); also lengthen the tails (size and feedback,
  risks a delay running away). A sound without any of the three does not change.
- In the middle the output is the same as before, bit for bit. The knobs reset with each preset,
  as every parameter does. Options: **reset (chosen)**, a preset is heard as designed; keep them
  across presets (a "performance" setting the user would have to remember).
Tested: brightness -1, 0, +1 on a ladder at 1 kHz (first-difference ratio 0.019, 0.027, 0.043);
a reverb's tail at space -1, 0, +1 (-46.9, -40.9, -34.8 dB); knobs 7 and 8 on every page; and the
click detector sweeping either knob end to end at once while a chord rings, every third preset
(worst 1.10x, Cellos with Space).
