## D215. What to do after 0.3.0 (2026-11-19)

The user ticked, from every open item (check marks, each item explained):

- Release and listening: the FL Studio knob test, the installer and Ableton check, the listening pass, and the verdicts on the sampled instruments and the Night Bus presets.
- Sounds and effects: the stutter effect (then the Pitch effect's Tune mode and formant control), the Museum's hoover and Juno-106, the wine glasses for the granular source.
- Engine: a lower CPU cost for the virtual analog oscillator, and freeing a sampled voice once its recording has ended.
- Fine-tuning and code: the ladder filter by ear, the sound-quality tests, the 'Your keyboard' line for any keyboard, and removing V2's old panel code.
- Not ticked: streaming samples from disk, and the recorder; both stay in TODO.md as not chosen. MPE: "never", removed from TODO.md.
- V2's old panel code, done on 2026-11-19: the whole of `src/ui` (SynthSection, HardwareKnob, HardwareFader, LedToggleButton, PianoBarComponent, SegmentedChoiceGroup and the headers only they used) removed; nothing outside it included any of it.
- The sound-quality tests, done on 2026-11-19 (`tests/V3ProcessorTests.cpp`, `tests/V3VoiceTests.cpp`): a 234 Hz sine through the ladder at 4 kHz, resonance 10 %, no Drive, measures 0.35 % total harmonic distortion (harmonics 2 to 8) at half scale and 1.5 % at full scale, bounded at 0.5 % and 2 %, and must grow with the level, as the transistor curve in every stage does. The amplifier's output, cycle by cycle, reaches its held level within a cycle of a 10, 50 or 200 ms attack, and is about 2.3 dB under it halfway (the default attack curve rises fast), bounded at -0.5 dB and under -1 dB.
- Freeing a sampled voice once its recording has ended, done on 2026-11-19 (`Voice::canEndEarly`, `src/synth/v3/Voice.cpp`): a voice ends before its release does only when its key is let go (a held key and the pedal keep it, so mono legato plays as before), every generator says it can make no sound until a new note (a one-shot recording that has ended, an empty slot; a loop never ends), its own output has stayed under -100 dB for 100 ms (above the ladder's -110 dB noise floor), and no ladder in it could start to sing by itself (resonance at or past the oscillation point, 0.9, or on the matrix; the SVF's Q stops at 40 and the comb's feedback at 0.98, so they only ring down). Measured: the strings recording two octaves up, released at 0.2 s into a 20 s release, frees its voice at 1.2 s instead of 20.2 s. What is cut is under -100 dB.

## D216. Controllers redesigned: one owner and one route per control (2026-11-19)

The FL Studio test of the released 0.3.0 failed: with the MiniLab driver Off the MiniLab stayed in its DAW program (knob 7 sent CC 116, the setup expected 19), and with FL's MIDI port set FL delivered every note twice (CoolSynth's log shows each note-on twice; pads switched a section and also played a note). The user asked whether the controller handling should be re-architected, "the right, proper way", cost no object, so that the VST3 works normally in all or most DAWs and in the standalone app, with the MiniLab 3 and with any controller. Two research passes (DAW side, hardware makers) and an analysis of CoolSynth's routes followed; the design is docs/design/CONTROLLERS.md.

- Findings: VST3 hands plugins no raw controller messages, hosts translate them through IMidiMapping unevenly (Ableton, Cubase, Bitwig, Studio One yes; Reaper doubtful; FL only through a numbered port, which doubles notes) (Steinberg's About MIDI; Image-Line forum threads); Ableton gives non-MPE instruments every note on channel 1 (Ableton's MPE notes for plugin makers); the DAWs' own mapping onto plugin parameters is the normal route (FL linking manual, Live 12 manual); CLAP remote control pages are the standard for page-following knobs (CLAP remote-controls header, clap-juce-extensions); Akai VIP and NI talk to their hardware directly and leave notes to the DAW; the MiniLab 3 has no program that keeps its controls off its main port, and no message switches its program (MiniLab 3 manual 1.0.5; Bitwig's MiniLab 3 extension; Arturia's FL and Ableton scripts); Windows MIDI Services makes ports multi-client on Windows 11 24H2 and later only. No JUCE library covers this; ReaLearn's split into neutral controller presets and main mappings, with control and feedback directions, is the model (ReaLearn wiki).
- Generic controllers in a DAW, the user's choice (options: the DAW's own mapping, recommended; the DAW's mapping plus CoolSynth's own learn, chosen; CoolSynth's learn as the main path): the DAW maps onto CoolSynth's parameters, in a deliberate order with the eight most useful first and proper edit gestures; CoolSynth's Learn stays as an extra, with an exact duplicate-note filter for FL's doubled delivery.
- Page-following knobs, the user's choice (options: a CLAP version with remote control pages, recommended and chosen; fake panel-knob parameters; page-following only with the MiniLab and in the app): CoolSynth also ships as CLAP, one remote control page per panel page, with suggest_page.
- The MiniLab's pads in a DAW, the user's choice (options: three layers together, recommended and chosen; scripts only; plugin-side timing guesses): the plugin drops every message the driver owns, exactly; CoolSynth tells the player once to set the track to channel 1 where the DAW merges channels; the FL and Ableton scripts stay optional, and the FL script's linking is fixed (FL had fallen back to its generic controller).
- The driver switched Off, the user's choice (options: ready setups for both MiniLab programs, recommended and chosen; ask for Shift + pad 3; both): a ready setup for the DAW program besides the Arturia program; the disconnect sent in both forms after clearing the screen.
- Also in the design: device-change notifications instead of listing devices every few seconds, a fallback when the port cannot be opened, and the standalone app keeping CoolSynth's own setups and Learn, where nothing stands between the controller and CoolSynth.
- The CLAP (C5), built on JUCE 9 (D217): clap-juce-extensions pinned at 6bde108, `CLAP_USE_JUCE_PARAMETER_RANGES DISCRETE` so stepped parameters keep their steps. The pages (`src/plugin/RemoteControlPages`) are Home's six, then each section's filled slots and their pages, each with the page's four knobs and then Glide, Brightness, Space and the master volume, so the right half of a controller stays put. A page's id comes from its section and name, so a host keeps its place when the list is rebuilt. Whenever the panel changes, the processor compares the pages: other pages (the layer, a slot's type) → `remoteControlsChanged`; another open page → `suggestRemoteControlsPage`. An empty slot has no page: the host keeps the page it had. The installer installs it in Common Files\CLAP (CLAP's own default path for every host, clap's `entry.h`), as its own tick box; the release gets a CLAP zip.

## D217. JUCE 9.0.5 (2026-11-19)

The user asked whether CoolSynth uses the latest JUCE and what is new. It used 8.0.12; current are 9.0.5 (2 November 2026) and the security releases 8.0.16 and 8.0.17 of the 8 line (JUCE's GitHub releases). The security fixes harden what CoolSynth reads from outside: malformed WAV and AIFF files, XML and ValueTree streams (a DAW's saved state), JSON (the controller setup), strings. JUCE 9 adds WinMM MIDI fixes, faster software rendering and Direct2D fixes; its promised native CLAP did not come (it waits for AudioProcessor v2; JUCE's 9 announcement), so clap-juce-extensions stays, whose JUCE 9 support is not confirmed. Licence terms are unchanged from JUCE 8.

- The user's choice (options: 8.0.17-security now, recommended; JUCE 9.0.5, chosen; 8.0.17 now and JUCE 9 later; stay on 8.0.12), "because I also want to support macOS. I have a Mac. I'll come back with that" (TODO J2).
- The switch works through JUCE's BREAKING_CHANGES.md from 8.0.12 on, then the full suite, then the CLAP build on JUCE 9.
- Done the same day. JUCE 9.0.5 needed only `LANGUAGES C CXX` in the project (its CMake builds C sources). Everything builds, the CLAP too, and the full suite passed (562 s). clap-juce-extensions 6bde108 already supports JUCE 9 (its PRs 178 and 183), so the doubt above is settled.
- The review round (J1b, an agent reading BREAKING_CHANGES.md, CHANGE_LIST.md and the JUCE diff against CoolSynth's code) found one deprecated call and one changed behaviour, both fixed:
  - The Save dialog's replace question used `AlertWindow::showOkCancelBox`, deprecated in JUCE 9: now `AlertWindow::showAsync` with `MessageBoxOptions`, each button with its own result code and role (Replace 1, destroy; Cancel 0, cancel). It behaves as before.
  - On Windows, `File::isAbsolutePath` now accepts a path starting with `/`. A project saved on a Mac holds `/Users/...` for a saved sound, and `juce::File` would then assert and guess a drive. `coolsynth::isFullPathHere` (`src/FullPath.h`) asks for a drive or a share on Windows; the state restore and the library pointer use it.
- Checked and unchanged: the stricter XML and JSON parsing (CoolSynth's own XML and controller.json are shallow, banks are its own text format), `ThreadPool::addJob` (every job returns nothing), `createEditor` now private (CoolSynth's public override still compiles), multi-touch off by default (CoolSynth has no touch code), MP3 among the basic formats (the library scans only WAV, AIFF and FLAC).
- Left for the user as a pick list (TODO J1b): `sendMessageNow`'s new result to notice a pulled-out MiniLab, Windows MIDI Services in JUCE, popup menus parented to the plugin window, `createEditorAndMakeActive` in the tests, the panel at 125 % and 150 %. The macOS notes went to TODO J2.
- Sources: JUCE's BREAKING_CHANGES.md and CHANGE_LIST.md in `external/JUCE`; the JUCE 9 announcement (forum.juce.com/t/juce-9-is-available-now/69175); clap-juce-extensions PRs 178 and 183 (github.com/free-audio/clap-juce-extensions).
- The user's choices on the review's optional items (2026-11-19, check marks; every item described with its cost): all four done, the hardware checks left for the DAW test.
  - Noticing an unplugged MiniLab at once, chosen (recommended): JUCE 9 tells a listener when a port it opened is gone (on the message thread, through an async update) and `sendMessageNow` says whether a message got out. Either marks the ports lost; the next timer tick drops them without talking to the device. A port lost while Windows still lists the MiniLab is opened again every two seconds, without the "in use by another program" notice. A WinMM sysex send reports success even when it fails (JUCE's `trySend`), so the port's disconnect notice is the one that counts.
  - The layer menu tied to the panel, chosen: `withTargetComponent(this)` before its screen area, which JUCE's PopupMenu docs ask for (the target sets the menu's scale on hi-DPI screens; 9.0.5 parents its native window to the plugin's).
  - The panel at 125 % and 150 %, chosen: a check by eye, added to the DAW test (TODO C6).
  - The editor the JUCE 9 way, chosen: the tests use `createEditorAndMakeActive`, and CoolSynth's `createEditor` override is private, as JUCE 9 made the base's.
- Windows MIDI Services in JUCE (options: leave it to Windows, recommended and chosen; JUCE's own backend): Windows 11 routes WinMM through Windows MIDI Services since February 2026, so ports are shared already (Microsoft's Windows Experience blog, 2026-02-17). JUCE's backend would add MIDI 2.0 only, at the cost of a NuGet package outside the official feed and the SDK runtime in the installer (JUCE's notes in juce_audio_devices.h).
- The DAW test (options: now with a new test installer, recommended; later, chosen): the user keeps it for later; the coding goes on meanwhile.

## D218. Any keyboard's size for the line over the screen's keys (2026-11-19)

The "Your keyboard" line (D203) showed only for the MiniLab 3. MIDI never says how many keys a keyboard has: no standard message carries it (MIDI.org forum, "how to detect number of piano keys"); programs ask for the size (Melodics, Synthesia), learn the lowest and highest key (NI's Hypha, MainStage's key range), or guess from the notes played.

- The user's choice (options: pick the size in Preferences, recommended and chosen; press the lowest and highest key; guess from the notes played; keep it MiniLab only): Preferences has a row YOUR KEYBOARD with None, 25, 37, 49, 61 and 88. It is kept with the controller setup in controller.json (`keyboardKeys`), for the computer; the MiniLab 3 connected is 25 keys whatever the row says.
- Where the line starts until a note says otherwise: C3 (48) for 25 and 37 keys, C2 (36) for 49 and 61, A0 (21) for 88. A note outside moves it by whole octaves, as little as holds it, as the MiniLab's did (D192), within four octaves and MIDI's 128 notes; another size starts at its home again.
- Which notes move it: with the MiniLab, only the MiniLab's own (its driver hears the device, not a DAW's played-back notes); with any other keyboard, every note that reaches CoolSynth from outside the screen (the app's MIDI inputs, the host). A DAW's played-back notes move it too, which no plugin can tell apart from playing.
- The screen's Oct buttons still move the screen's keys with another keyboard (only the MiniLab greys them out), so only the part of the line in sight is drawn, and a sliver at the edge goes unnamed.

## D219. A faster VA oscillator, bit for bit the same (2026-11-19)

The user chose a lower CPU cost for the virtual analog oscillator (D215). Quality first: the sound must not change, so every step was checked against a fingerprint of the VA's exact output in thirteen modes (sine, triangle, saw, pulse, two morphs, Supersaw, unison 4, sub, sync, FM, ring, AM; `COOLSYNTH_VA_FINGERPRINT=1`), every bit of every sample, the same before and after.

- The phase wrap: `x - std::floor(x)` is a library call with the build's SSE2 target (MSVC turns floor into one instruction only from SSE4.1 on; Microsoft's /arch documentation, mmozeiko's SSE floor benchmarks on GitHub). For a phase moving forward by under a cycle it is `x >= 1 ? x - 1 : x`, the same number; FM, which can move it back, keeps floor.
- The sample being built stays in registers while the unison copies add to it, with the same additions in the same order as into the ring; before, every copy stored it and the next loaded it again. A crossing's speed is worked out only at a crossing.
- Measured (eight-note chords, optimised Debug, this machine): one saw 1.71 % to 1.34 % of real time over the empty voices, seven saws 5.55 % to 4.69 %, the Supersaw preset 18.4 % to 17.7 % on average. The VA is now the smaller part of Supersaw: its ladder costs 5.8 %.
- The bigger steps left change the last bit of the numbers or other things, so they are the user's to choose: the unison copies side by side in SIMD lanes (sums in another order: a difference near -140 dB), the ladder, or building for AVX2 (faster everywhere, but no CPU from before 2013).
- The user's choice on what comes next (check marks; options: the ladder, recommended; the unison copies in SIMD lanes; building for AVX2; stop here): the first three, then the stutter effect.
- The ladder, bit for bit the same (its own fingerprint in eight cases: each mode, singing, Drive 24 dB, FM, a loud input past the rational's range): left and right fill two of a register's four lanes, so two stages now share one register, and `1/sqrt(x2)` past |x| 4.9 is worked out only when a lane needs it. Six divisions a solve instead of fifteen, five square roots only when needed. Its own cost 5.8 % to 4.8 % of real time: less than the count suggests, as each sample waits on the one before.
- The unison copies side by side, the user's choice (the sums in another order, a last-bit difference): without sync, FM or a sine in the shape and with two copies or more, the copies run four at a time in SSE registers. Every copy's phase, crossing test, value and products are its loop's, operation for operation; crossings (about once a cycle) go through the loop's own code in copy order; only the sum of the copies is taken in another order. Recorded before and compared after (`COOLSYNTH_VA_RECORD`, `COOLSYNTH_VA_COMPARE`) in eighteen modes: the unison ones differ by 134.8 to 138.4 dB under the peak, the rest (one copy, a sine, sync, FM) not at all. Seven saws on eight notes 4.7 % to 2.6 % of real time; Supersaw 17.7 % to 14.2 % on average, 27 % to 19 % in its worst block.
- Building for AVX2, tried and undone: with `/arch:AVX2` the output stayed bit for bit the same (no fused multiply-adds under /fp:precise; Microsoft's C++ blog on /fp:contract in VS 2022), and it was no faster: the 136 presets' mean 7.21 % of real time against 7.17 % with SSE2, single modules a few percent either way. The user asked to double-check: the objects were rebuilt with it, and Voice.obj held 925 ymm uses and no plain SSE, so AVX2 was in use; the hot code is chains of dependent scalar steps or hand-written 4-wide SSE, which AVX2 only re-encodes. Asked again (options: back to SSE2, recommended and chosen; 8-wide code with a run-time fallback; keep AVX2 with an installer check), the build stays SSE2, so every 64-bit Windows PC runs CoolSynth.

## D220. The stutter effect (2026-11-19)

The user chose the stutter effect next (D215; the third of the reference-artist research's top three, `docs/research/sound-references.md` section 6). Others were looked at first: Ableton's Beat Repeat (Live 12 reference manual: Interval, Offset, Chance, Gate, Grid, Variation, Pitch and Pitch Decay, Decay, the Mix, Insert and Gate modes, the Repeat button), iZotope's Stutter Edit 2 (its documentation: rates that move over a gesture, a buffer that can reverse) and dblue's Glitch (retrigger with a pitch step per repeat; KVR).

- How it starts, the user's choice (options: grid, chance and Hold, recommended and chosen; Hold only; grid and chance only): every Interval at its Offset with a Chance, as Beat Repeat; and at once while Hold is on, which an effect route puts on the mod wheel, a macro or the step modulator.
- Extras, the user's check marks (pitch drop and fade, recommended; roll; variation; reverse): all four. Pitch and Decay act from the second repeat on, as a tape slowed down; Roll halves each repeat at full; Variation picks the slice a few straight or triplet steps around Grid; Reverse is All or Alternate.
- Mode, the user's choice (all three, recommended and chosen; Insert only): Insert, Mix and Gate, as Beat Repeat.
- Presets, the user's choice (drafts for listening, recommended and chosen; none): three in Night Bus, marked as drafts: Cut-Up Chords (on the grid by chance), Wheel Stutter (Hold on the mod wheel), Glitch Pad (Hold on a step pattern, in Mix). The older Stutter Chords gates its volume with the step modulator and stays as it is. Levelled by the loudness tool; Wheel Stutter's Vowel macro moves the vocal wavetable only a little (0.12), as its vowels differ in level and a wider sweep made the sound 5 dB louder with every macro up (the D177 test allows 4).
- Built as a fifteenth effect type, at the end so saved patches keep their numbers (`src/synth/v3/StutterInsert.h`), twelve values in pages of four. A stutter lets one slice play through and keeps it, then repeats it; every repeat and every way in or out fades over at most 2 ms; reads between samples are cubic. Tested (`tests/V3StutterTests.cpp`): it catches on beat three as Beat Repeat's defaults say, each repeat equals the slice to 1e-6, Chance 0 never catches, Hold catches at once and lets go within the fade, the three modes, decay, roll, an octave's drop, reverse, variation keeping straight steps straight, and no step at the cuts larger than a 220 Hz sine's own at 1/16, 1/32 and 1/64.

## D221. The added noise: as tape and records make it (2026-11-19)

The user found LoFi's noise artificial at times and asked for an online investigation first (TODO 18). It is in `docs/research/noise-modelling.md`, with its sources: tape hiss has a constant level and a rising treble shaped by playback EQ, noise that follows the music is a known noise-reduction defect, crackle in the literature is dense, of many lengths and sizes and one-sided in stereo (Moliner and Välimäki, DAFx 2022), and RC-20, ChowTapeModel, u-he Satin and AudioThing make following the signal an option, Satin with an Auto Mute in silence. CoolSynth's LoFi gated its whole noise by the signal one for one (D52), clicked one-sample mono spikes, shaped its hiss flat to 6 kHz after the Bandwidth filter, and shared one seed between slots. The Noise generator was found in line with practice.

The user's choices (each researched option put with its consequences):
- The level (options: constant with a mute in silence, recommended and chosen; constant plus a Follow knob; tape-style breathing above 1.5 kHz; keep): steady while anything plays, fading out after about a second of silence, as Satin's Auto Mute. The presets with hiss or crackle are re-levelled (about 3 to 6 dB less Hiss).
- The crackle (options: richer procedural, recommended and chosen; recorded loops; a minimal fix; keep): clicks per channel or panned, of random lengths from 20 µs to 4 ms with filtered timbres, many small and few large, a quiet dense bed with a drifting rate, rare pops with a falling low thump, optionally a pattern repeating at 33 1/3 rpm.
- The hiss (options: tape-shaped before Bandwidth, recommended and chosen; tape-shaped after the crush; reshape only; keep): rising above the 1.3 to 2.3 kHz playback turnover, rolled off at 10 to 14 kHz, a little less below 500 Hz, added before the Bandwidth filter and the crush, so it dulls with the music and dithers the crush.
- Also chosen (check marks): each LoFi slot its own seed; modulation (asperity) noise riding on the signal about 40 to 50 dB under it, tied to Hiss as Satin does; a Brown colour in the Noise generator.
- Built the same day (`src/synth/LoFi.h`, `src/synth/v3/NoiseGenerator.h`; the details in the research page's last section): the noise's level steady and faded after a second of silence; hiss -4 dB under 500 Hz and +8 dB above 2.3 kHz (the +6 first tried measured gentler than tape), rolled off at 12 kHz, before Bandwidth and the crush, full Hiss still -33 dBFS, the same per hertz at any rate; modulation noise; the crackle model, its clicks' colour set in hertz so it sounds alike at 48 and 192 kHz (a first version set it in samples and lost 4 dB at 192 kHz); a seed per slot, restarted by reset; Brown, with older states and patches migrated (formats 15 and 14) so a saved Chip stays Chip. Tested in `tests/V3LoFiTests.cpp`, `tests/V3NoiseTests.cpp` and `tests/PatchStateTests.cpp`. The presets with hiss have Hiss and its routes at 0.6 (about 4.4 dB less, as a steady hiss is heard more); all wait for the user's ear.

## D222. Determinism and a processing budget (2026-11-19)

The user asked that the chain be predictable and deterministic and fit a budget, with a known answer when it would not (TODO 19). The research and the review of CoolSynth are in `docs/research/determinism-and-budget.md`: real-time hygiene is good and multi-core output is bit for bit, but nothing keeps a sound within a block's time (the modules' cost classes are read nowhere), the random arpeggiator is seeded from the clock, the overload fade reads the clock also when bouncing, offline notes are dropped while samples load, and random states run on from preparation. Hardware synths cap polyphony by the patch's cost (Access Virus, Korg opsix); Kontakt kills voices when the CPU gets critical.

The user's choices:
- The budget (options: a static cap plus a live emergency, recommended and chosen; the static cap only; dynamic shedding only; leave it): each patch's worst-case cost, measured per module with unison and oversampling, caps its voices to fit a share of a block, shown on the screen; live only, when a dropout would otherwise happen, the quietest released voice goes; never when bouncing.
- Randomness (options: seeded, the same every play, recommended and chosen; the same only when bouncing; leave it and document it): every random source seeded from the patch and its slot and restarted at a defined start (a reset, the transport starting); random arp patterns repeat from play to play unless an option asks for new ones.
- Bouncing (options: bounce exactly, recommended and chosen; only drop the load fade; leave it): offline, no load-based fade, and processing waits, with a time limit, until instruments and the player's own sounds are loaded instead of dropping notes.
- Checks and multi-core (check marks, all four chosen): a determinism test (every preset rendered twice, fresh and after a reset, bit for bit, also offline); a worst-case cost test (the slowest block and the 99th percentile per module and preset at the hardest settings, which also gives the cap its numbers); wider real-time checks (malloc, locks and file calls on the audio thread in the test build); multi-core by layer (layer A on a helper, B on the audio thread, summed in a fixed order), after the cost test shows the need.
- Built the same day: D224.

Also on 2026-11-19, for the Museum (TODO 6): the user allowed every download the hoover needs ("download everything you might need"). The Alpha Juno collection is on OneDrive, which refuses an unsigned download from a script; the user offered to fetch it.

## D223. The hoover's source: the Alpha Juno factory tone, decoded (2026-11-19)

The Museum's hoover (D103) had no published values. The user allowed the downloads it needs and fetched MKS50_Alpha_Juno_Collection-A.zip from the MKS-50 ZONE (OneDrive refused a script). Its FACTORYB.SYX is the factory tone group B of the MKS-50 and both Alpha Junos, a Roland bulk dump: 16 messages of four tones, each tone 32 bytes sent as nibbles, least significant first.

- The byte layout is Roland's own, from the MKS-50 owner's manual, section 3.2 ("*2 TONE data format" and its switch bits; ManualsLib's copy 695917, page 65, read from the scan, as the text layers of the copies are garbled). Bytes 0 to 2 hold six 4-bit fields (DCO, VCF and VCA aftertouch depth, VCF and envelope key follow, bender range); byte 3 the DCO LFO depth; bytes 4 to 20 the seventeen 7-bit values with switch bits b00 to b16 on top (chorus, the three envelope modes, the sub, saw and pulse waves, the high-pass); bytes 21 to 30 the name (6-bit characters) with b17 to b22 (range, sub level, noise level) and the chorus rate's seven bits on top.
- Checked: all 64 names of the bank decode correctly, and "What the" is tone 86, as the MKS-50 ZONE lists it. The open-source editor chriswareham/alphajuno (BSD) gives the 36 parameters' order and ranges (its doc/sysex.txt) and a hand-made hoover.pat, which differs from the factory tone (16' against 32', no noise against a little) and is not used.
- Kept: the decoder (`tools/museum/alphajuno_decode.py`) and the decoded tone with its origin (`resources/museum-candidates/sources/alpha-juno-what-the.txt`). The zip and the bank file are not in the repository. Next: the candidate itself, converted from these numbers as D104 asks, then the user's side-by-side.

## D224. Determinism and the budget, built (2026-11-19)

D222's choices, built and tested. The user then asked for the rest of D222 too ("it's not such a huge thing"), before their test pass and the next release.

- Determinism. The new test (`tests/DeterminismTests.cpp`) renders every preset (a chord held 0.75 s, then let go) in two fresh processors, then again after one reset and after two, live and bouncing, and compares every bit. At first 4 presets differed between fresh processors (the random arp, seeded from the clock) and 131 after a reset. The causes, each fixed:
  - Every random source restarts from its seed at a defined start: each module, LFO, chaos source, effect and arp keeps its start value, and `EngineV3::restart` (the host's reset) and `reseed` (the host's transport starting) take them back to it.
  - The arp is seeded from its sound, not the clock.
  - The effects rack: a fresh rack ramps its drive, chorus and reverb mixes up from zero over 4 ms; a cleared one now does the same, and its delay starts at its settings instead of gliding from where its time was left.
  - The LFOs draw their first random values at each reset, and preparing resets them more than once: each voice and layer now keeps its LFOs' state at the end of preparing as the start a reseed returns to.
  - A layer's sources for the effect routes (D179) ramped from the last render's values after a reset; a reset now clears them, as a fresh layer has them.
  - Found by rendering after a reset alone, after a silent render and after a chord: what still differed after silence was time, not notes.
  - Result: all 139 presets the same bit for bit, fresh, after one reset and after two, live and bouncing.
- The arps' New Patterns switch (`pA.arpNewPatterns`, `pB.arpNewPatterns`, the bank key `newPatterns`), off: random choices go on across defined starts instead of repeating. Wrongly built without a state version change at first: a state must name every parameter, so the app's saved state of version 15 was refused and it opened on its start sound. Fixed in D225 (state version 16, sound files 15).
- Bouncing: no load fade offline, and each block waits up to 10 s for the sampled instruments and the player's own sounds a playing layer names (`EngineV3::waitForSounds`); one that does not come is not waited for again until the next start.
- Wider real-time checks (`tests/AudioThreadAlarm.cpp`): the test program points the import tables of itself and of Microsoft's C and C++ runtimes at counting stand-ins for the heap, locks, waits, sleeps and files, as Clang's RealtimeSanitizer and Tracktion's rtcheck do on Linux and macOS (neither runs on Windows), and names the functions behind the first calls. It found two debug-build artefacts and no real call: the debug C runtime locks its heap even for free(nullptr), which every buffer view's empty heap block does, and the debug C++ library's iterator checks lock on every vector iterator (std::_Lockit). The watch's own free skips the null pointer, as the C standard makes it a no-op, and the C++ library's critical sections are not watched (its mutexes are slim reader/writer locks, still watched). Every audio scenario: no heap, lock, wait, sleep or file call.
- Measuring, the user's choice (options: the block timer now and a tracer once a slow block needs explaining, recommended and chosen; melatonin_perfetto now; Intel VTune): the worst-case cost test (`V3 worst-case cost` per module and effect, `V3 CPU report` per preset, `COOLSYNTH_CPU_REPORT=1`), sixteen voices at each module's hardest settings struck twice, the best of three runs. Per voice, in percent of a 256-sample block at 48 kHz on this computer (optimised Debug): the voice itself 0.35, VA 0.19 and 0.04 a unison copy, FM4 0.86, Grain 0.85, String 0.38, FM2 0.30, Ladder 0.56, Drive 0.48. The effects, at the 99th percentile: Pitch 3.8 (spectral 5.5, its slowest block 15), the Grain effect 0.7 at its defaults and 7.1 at its densest, the others under 1. The heaviest presets with sixteen keys struck twice: Trance Glass 52.5 % at the 99th percentile (59 % slowest), Furnace Drone 52.0 %, Vocal Mask 50.9 %. A voice's 99th percentile over sixteen was lost in the timer's noise, so the cap's table takes the averages; its estimates track the measured averages within about 15 % (Grain priced at its densest is over).
- The voice cap (`src/synth/v3/VoiceCost.h`), the user's choice of budget (options: 50 %, no preset cut, recommended and chosen; 75 %, almost never; 35 %, safer on slow computers, Granular Sky's layer A 14 of 16; no cap yet, the emergency only): a sound's voices and effects within half a block on this computer by the table, the effects first, the layers sharing the rest in proportion, never below one voice, the same live and bounced. No factory preset loses a voice at 50 % (a test keeps it so); three FM4 generators with two ladders gets about 11 of 16. The screen's info row shows "N of M voices".
- The live emergency: at 90 % of a block with no earlier sound left to fade, the quietest released voice fades out over the steal fade, one a block (`EngineV3::emergencyShare`, `Voice::fadeOut`); a held key's voice is never taken, and a bounce reads no load.
- Multi-core by layer, the user's choice (options: not now, recommended and chosen; build it now): the heaviest single sound stays under the deadline here; it waits in TODO 19 for slower computers.
- The determinism test's live pass ignores the measured load (`ignoreLoadForTesting`): live, a block finished late lets a sound or a released voice go, by design, so a busy computer changed Blanket Pad once while a build ran beside the test.
- The click detector now judges the random-arp presets, which it used to skip as varying from run to run (D168). Satellite Ping's four macros turned full up at once measured 1.54x (the limit 1.5x), a smooth brightening of a decaying ping with no spike, against a reference taken after the ping had died away. The user's choice (options: judge by level in the macro test, recommended and chosen; leave it not judged, on the user's list; smoother macros on every preset): the macro test also takes the sound's roughness for its level just after the turn. Every preset passes; the worst is Bowed Haze at 1.46x.
- Not tested by hand yet: the cap and the emergency with a heavy patch, a bounce in a DAW.

Also on 2026-11-19, parked by the user as TODO items 20 to 25: chip sounds and chiptune; presets for the legendary ARP and Oberheim sounds, or whether the bank's are close enough; presets that use the whole engine; profiling the heaviest possible sound with proper tools; making and keeping one's own sounds, as other plugins do it, in the plugin and the app alike; tempo (tap tempo, a metronome, MIDI clock), with the recorder and a sequencer.

## D225. The test pass begins; an MCP server in the app (2026-11-19)

The user's test pass of what changed since 0.3.0 began (`docs/test-pass.md`, the rounds' results and every finding). The user is the ears and hands; the first rounds showed how much time went into asking the user to set things and into instructions that were wrong (Slices on the "fourth" page, Home as pad 8, keys named without saying which). The user asked for a way for Claude to set and read the app directly: "start doing MCP now because we just need it".

- What the rounds found so far, besides what works: FL Studio's CoolSynth 0.3.0 held the MiniLab and had switched the MiniLab driver off for the whole computer; with the driver off and the MiniLab left in its DAW program the Arturia-program setup does not fit (knobs dead); the lights stay white after a replug; slicing by attacks spreads Sensitivity over only the knob's first 42 % and the detector ranks hats over kicks and missed a kick; the value display cuts off file names, so a kit and a file inside it look the same; the user's own samples play very quietly (a preset's level, files not levelled); the Home knob replaces an edited sound without asking; finding Mono took long. All in `docs/test-pass.md`; parked as TODO 26 to 28 where they are larger (the sample interface, playing one's own samples, the MCP server).
- The flight recorder now logs every knob change made in the app (the parameter's ID and its value as the screen shows it) and every file choice, so a note in the log can be matched to the settings it played with; not a preset's own values (logged by name) and not a host's automation.
- Test tools in the test program, for the user's test pass: `COOLSYNTH_ATTACKS_OF` (the attacks found in a file), `COOLSYNTH_USER_FOLDER` with `COOLSYNTH_STATE_FROM` (the app's saved state, played) and `COOLSYNTH_SET_STATE` with `COOLSYNTH_SET` (the app's saved state, set and written back).
- The MCP server. Research: no official C++ MCP SDK exists (zhoutk/mcp-cpp's README says so); community C++ SDKs (cxxmcp, gopher-mcp, zhoutk/mcp-cpp, cpp-mcp) call themselves release candidates or work in progress; nothing exists for JUCE apart from a documentation server and mcpsynthcontroller (Python and OSC, three commits, dummy reads). MCP's Streamable HTTP (2025-06-18) asks of a server: one endpoint for POST, a JSON object or an event stream in reply, 202 for notifications, GET answered or 405, the Origin header checked against DNS rebinding, localhost only; Claude Code connects to it with `claude mcp add --transport http`.
- The user's choices: how to build it (options: inside the app with no library, recommended and chosen; inside the app with a community SDK; a Python bridge with the official SDK and a socket to the app); where (options: the app with a Prefs switch, recommended and chosen; test builds only; the app and the plugins); which tools (all four offered: read and set settings, see the panel, play and measure, read the flight log).
- Built: `src/mcp/McpServer` (HTTP on 127.0.0.1, JSON-RPC, initialize with the client's protocol version among 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26 and 2024-11-05, tools/list, tools/call, ping; 202, 405, 403 for another origin, 400, 404) and `src/mcp/CoolSynthTools` (get_sound, list_parameters, set_values by screen text, set_slot_type with the type's defaults as the panel does, list_presets, load_preset, list_files, set_file, play with the loudest level and the most voices, screenshot of the window, read_log). Tools run on the message thread, the server waits up to 10 s; a job still queued when the app closes does nothing. Port 47613. Prefs has a CLAUDE (MCP) row, in the app only; off by default.
- Tested: `tests/McpServerTests.cpp` (the handshake, the tools on a processor, the HTTP answers, a request over a socket); on the running app: the handshake, get_sound, play (the voice cap: three FM4 generators and two ladders, sixteen keys, 13 voices), screenshot, read_log. Registered with Claude Code for this project (`claude mcp add --transport http coolsynth http://127.0.0.1:47613/mcp`, local scope): connected; its tools come with a new session.
- Also parked by the user: TODO 26 (the sample choice's interface; setting a value by clicking it), 27 (playing one's own samples, a big discussion), 28 (the MCP server, now started).
- The controller setup: a saved setup that names a ready one and holds nothing it has not (0.3.0 saved the MiniLab's Arturia-program numbers only) is that ready setup unchanged, so today's replaces it, with both programs. A setup the player changed stays. The lights after a replug: the MiniLab's screen and pads are sent again 1 s and 3 s after it connects.
- Slicing by attacks, the user's choices:
  - the detector (options: per-band with log-spaced bands, recommended and chosen; log bands only; only a better threshold), after Bello 2005, Böck 2012 and SuperFlux: flux in third-octave bands from 30 Hz, in three ranges (below 150 Hz, to 2 kHz, above), the strongest range at each hop, peaks under 15 % of the strongest left out. On a drum bar all eight hits are found, the kick after the snare too, kicks ranked first.
  - Sensitivity (options: by rank, recommended and chosen; by loudness in dB; by rank with locked slices), as Ableton's Simpler, Logic's Quick Sampler and Bitwig do: it keeps the strongest so many attacks, one more slice per step over the whole knob.
- A file's name, the user's choice (options: a folder line, then the file, recommended and chosen; one line with the name's end; a running marquee, which the Deluge's users asked to turn off): the knob's name line says where the file is (Kit: CST2, CST2/, Your files), its value line the name without the extension, shortened in the middle, on the panel and the MiniLab. Not built yet: one slow scroll of a long name after the knob rests.
- An edited sound, the user's choice (options: browse while edited and keep the edit to go back to, recommended and chosen; always browse, then press; load on turn with a recall): the big knob already browses and a press loads, so what was built is the recall, as Yamaha's edit recall: before the panel loads a sound into a layer or empties it, an edited sound is kept, and the list of rooms starts with "Back to your edit: name".
- Loudness. The user found the synth very quiet. Measured on the app: Basic Saw's saw peaks at -23.6 dBFS on one note, the user's loop in its Sample slot at -26.7 dBFS, so the quietness is the whole synth's, every preset levelled to -20 LUFS (D153). Online: virtual instruments are more often too loud than too quiet (Sound On Sound's "shouty presets", Production Expert on plugin loudness), gain-staging conventions sit around -18 dBFS, and hardware keeps programs even and gives a master volume. The user's choice (options: the Master can go above its top, recommended and chosen; +6 dB output for every sound, the first answer before the research; keep it as it is):
  - the Master Boost (`masterBoost`, 0 to +12 dB, at 0 by default), after the Master fader;
  - the screen's Master fader goes a quarter beyond its top for it, marked "0 dB" where the boost begins; the Master moved below its top, by the mouse or the MiniLab, takes the boost away;
  - it stays across sounds as the faders do, and a reset sets it to 0.
- The state versions: a state must name every parameter, so a new parameter always raises the version. The New Patterns switches (D224) and the Master Boost raise the app and project state to version 16 and the sound files to 15; older ones get them at their defaults (a test).

## D226. The panel rearranged for a bigger layer screen (2026-11-19)

The user asked for quick changes ahead of the interface redesign, laid out by them:

- The knob rows move down 75 px, still 132 px apart: a lower knob's name on two lines ends at the main screen's bezel.
- The faders are as tall as the two knob rows (196 px, were 330): a thumb at the top is level with the upper knobs' top, the track ends by the lower knobs' bottom, and their numbers and names sit on the lower knobs' lines.
- The layer screen takes the room freed above the knobs and the faders: from knob 1 to the Master, down to 26 px above the upper knobs (482 by 157 px, was 287 by 82).
- The layer screen shows each layer's chain, a placeholder until the redesign that uses the room. Research: Phase Plant draws its generators, effects and modulators as lanes with the signal flow's arrows; the Montage M's Navigation screen is an overview where tapping a block jumps to its editing. The user's choice (options: four rows with every slot, recommended and chosen; the sound path only, larger; a grid of lights without names):
  - a row per section in its pad's colour: Sound's G1-G3 joined by +, Shape's P1-P3 by arrows, Motion's envelopes and LFOs, the layer's six effects (four-letter names: Chor, Dly, Rvb) and M, the chain at the end;
  - an empty slot dashed, the slot the knobs are on outlined white, the sound's name beside LAYER A;
  - a click opens that layer's section on that slot, a second its type list (`InterfaceModel::openSlot`, tested).
- The layer screen's head, the user's request: LAYER A clicked opens the layer's menu (copy, swap, solo, empty), as a right click does; the sound's name or the panel elsewhere opens Home on that layer, its list on the sound it plays, to choose another (`InterfaceModel::openHomeFor`, tested). It used to pick the layer only.
- Its hints. Ableton Live keeps all hover help in one place, its Info View; nothing found on help beside the pointer. The user's choice (options: the layer screen's own hints on it, recommended and chosen; every hover hint there; as it was): with the mouse on the layer screen, what it points at takes the info row's place (the CPU stays), and the info row comes back when it leaves; knobs, faders, pads and buttons keep the main screen's hint line.

## D227. The MCP server reads the panel as text and clicks it by name (2026-11-19)

The user, after being asked to watch a knob run through file names: "You can do that automatically... you can simulate this", then asked for a way to read the screen without screenshots, or a picture of only the LED.

- Research: Playwright's MCP server reads web pages as accessibility snapshots, text with a reference per element, instead of screenshots, which cost far more tokens. JUCE has an accessibility layer, but the panel draws most of its parts itself.
- The user's choices, all four offered: the screens as text, recommended; a picture of one part; a click by name; a JUCE accessibility tree (its timing asked separately).
- `read_screen`: the panel describes itself line by line from what it draws (`PanelEditor::describeForMcp`): the main screen's header, trail, list or slots, page tabs, cards (with a name as drawn when shortened) and hint line, the main knob's roles, the layer screen's layers, chains and info row, knobs 1-8, faders, pads, buttons, the MiniLab's display and the keyboard line, then the names the click tool takes.
- `click`: a mouse click at the named part, so the panel's own hit areas are tested (list rows, tabs, slots, cards, a layer's name, a chain block, pads, knobs, Hold, Oct), or a fader moved to a value; it answers with the panel as text. Not by click: menus and cards that open over the panel.
- `screenshot` takes an area (the main screen, the layer screen, knobs, faders, pads, keys, the left column, or x,y,w,h) and a zoom of 1 to 3.
- Tested on a panel (`tests/McpServerTests.cpp`) and on the running app.
- Accessibility, the user's choice (options: with the real interface, recommended; the real controls now, chosen; all of it now): knobs 1-8 and the main knob are sliders with their name and value ("Knob 6: Glide", "0.0 ms"; a changed value is announced) and a press; the pads are buttons ("Pad 2: Sound", lit or not) and a press; the faders read their level in dB with the Master's boost; Prefs, Hold and Oct had theirs. Checked with Windows UI Automation (`tools/uia-check.ps1`): every name and value read, a pad pressed by it. The screens' drawn rows, slots, cards and chain blocks wait for the redesign (TODO 31).

The user, after the DAW round needed their hands for every step: "why am I doing your job". The user's choices (all three offered): a host of my own, recommended; pluginval and clap-validator; Zrythm by computer control.

- `tests/HostedPluginTests.cpp`: the test program hosts the built VST3 through JUCE's own VST3 hosting (`JUCE_PLUGINHOST_VST3` in the test program only), as a DAW does: found in its bundle as an instrument, made, played, a parameter moved by the host, bounced twice the same, its state given to a second one that sounds the same bit for bit, its panel opened. The test program now builds the VST3 first.
- `COOLSYNTH_TEST_HOST`: a plugin in a test host starts no MiniLab driver and writes no flight log, so tests leave the player's MiniLab and logs alone; it uses the tests' small sample library (`COOLSYNTH_LIBRARY`).

- clap-validator (free-audio, 0.4.1) on the CLAP found real faults, fixed and tested on every parameter in `tests/ParameterRoundTripTests.cpp`:
  - a typed value did not read back: "1.68 s" became 1.68 ms, "10 %" 100 %, "off" and "A 100%" nothing, as JUCE reads only the number; every float parameter and every slot parameter now reads a text by its own display (`src/parameters/TextToValue.h`), 7903 values checked;
  - a slot's new type renames its generic parameters, and the host was never told: CoolSynth now tells it (JUCE's parameter-info notice: the VST3 sends the titles again, the CLAP asks the host to rescan), at once after a state load on the main thread, otherwise on its timer;
  - left, as accepted: a new CoolSynth starts on Reese, not on the declared defaults (the user's choice, options: leave it, recommended and chosen; Reese's values as the defaults; start DAWs on the defaults); and a state saved again is the same except that a value on a curved range can come back a float step off (JUCE keeps the plain value and goes through the curve at every load). 30 of 44 pass, 11 do not apply.
- The MiniLab's fader 4 reached only 0 dB, so the boost was out of the hardware's reach. The user's choice (options: like the screen fader, recommended and chosen; Shift and fader 4 for the boost): a controller's fader 4 runs along the Master's whole travel, 0 dB at four fifths of its throw and +12 dB at its top, as a mixer fader has room above unity (Ableton's +6 dB, a PreSonus desk's +10). The screen's fader and any controller's go through the same model call (`InterfaceModel::moveMasterAlongTravel`).

## D231. The Chip generator, built (2026-11-20)

Built as designed (D230), the user asleep; the smaller choices taken as recommended, each with its options in `docs/overnight-2026-11-20.md`.

- `src/synth/v3/ChipGenerator.h`, the eleventh generator type (`generatorType::chip`, at the end, so saved sounds load as they were), and `src/synth/v3/ChipWaves.h`: 79 band-limited shapes (the triangles, saws, the AY's and YM's buzz through Ayumi's DAC tables, the SID's combined waves at 16 widths, ten named waves at 4 and 8 bits), about 7 MB, built when a layer is prepared.
- The pulse, the five noise registers and every jump the frame tables make are band-limited steps: at F7 the largest alias is at -86 dB, Raw -30 dB.
- Noise follows the key at 93 clocks a note period (the Game Boy's 7-bit mode 127), rounded to the rates each chip has: sixteen on the NES, 31 on the AY, any on the SID.
- Decay counts the chip's own envelope period (the NES's quarter frames, the Game Boy's 64 Hz, the SID's sixteen decay rates with its exponential steps; a step every N frames on the others).
- Output Chip: the NES's mixer curve and its 90 Hz and 14 kHz filters (nesdev.org; the 440 Hz high-pass an older page named is not there), the Game Boy's 28 Hz, reSID's external filter, the AY's and YM's logarithmic volume.
- Left out, with the reason: the SID's DC offset (constant on the chip; under CoolSynth's envelopes only thumps); the SID's own ring (a link carries no phase): Ring is the shared one. The NES triangle has no volume, as on the chip. The 6581's combined waves are half the 8580's.
- `tests/V3ChipTests.cpp`: every chip and wave at a saw's loudness (-15 to -3 dB), the aliases, the pulse widths, the registers' lengths (32767, 93, 127, 131071, 8388607) and pitches, an arpeggio a frame at a time, the decays, a kick, a hard restart, sync, the NES's high-pass, a width sweep without steps.
- Its cost in the voice cap: 0.33 % of a block a voice at its hardest (a combined wave between two widths with an arpeggio, or the NES's noise with its output filters), against the VA saw's 0.16 in the same run.
- A retrigger restarts the frame tables, as a tracker's new note does; a struck slot now also hears the new note's velocity (`Slot::strike`), for the kick to come.
- Arcade revoiced with it (D229), drafts for the user's ears, levelled to -20 LUFS (their masters 3 to 5 dB up: the chip's pulse swings ±0.6 where the VA's swings ±1): Coin Pulse, Boss Bass and Power-Up on the NES's pulse, SCC Lead on the SCC's 32-step pulse, Chip Chord on the chip's own arpeggio at a note a frame instead of the chord memory and the arp; Static Hit stays, already the NES's noise.
- The info row shows the tempo the sound uses: the host's whenever it gives one, also stopped (found by the tempo research).
- The heap watch's test failed in the 0.4.0 release run a second time (fixed in 76a0f39): a release build carries the C runtime inside it (/MT), so its malloc is no import and the debug allocation hook is not there. The watch now also counts HeapAlloc, HeapReAlloc and HeapFree in release builds (`tests/AudioThreadAlarm.cpp`); the volatiles of 787f9b6 were not the cause. The tag moved to 76a0f39 (the release unpublished until then), the run passed, and 0.4.0 was published on 2026-11-20 at 03:24 UTC with the installer, the VST3, CLAP and app zips and their checksums (J3 done).

## D232. The Kick generator (2026-11-20)

The kick generator the user chose (D229, TODO 30), researched online (`docs/research/kick-generator.md`: Werner, Abel and Smith's TR-808 bass drum model at DAFx 2014 and Werner's thesis, Synth Secrets on the 808 and 909, Kick 3, Punchbox, Metrum, DS Kick, Bitwig's kicks, Microtonic) and built while the user slept; each choice taken as recommended, with its options as a pick list in `docs/overnight-2026-11-20.md`.

- The design (options: one generator with a Model switch over shared knobs, recommended and taken; the 808 circuit model alone; a pitch-envelope kick alone): `src/synth/v3/KickGenerator.h`, the twelfth generator type, knobs Model (808, 909, Modern), Decay, Punch, Punch Time, Sigh, Click, Noise, Shape, Accent, Drive, Tone, Trigger (One Shot, Gate).
- The 808 (options: a struck resonator that does what the paper measured, recommended and taken; Werner's full circuit in wave digital filters; a pitch-envelope imitation): a decaying phasor rung by each hit, so a hit on a ringing drum adds to its ring and no two are alike; its pitch jumps up for Punch Time (the attack shift) and sighs down Sigh semitones over about a quarter second, both further the harder the hit (Accent); Shape clips it as the op-amps do; the trigger pulse leaks through as Click, its falling edge clipped as the pulse shaper does. The full circuit model stays for later, if the user's ears want more of it.
- 909 and Modern: an oscillator thrown up Punch semitones and falling to the note over Punch Time, the 909's shaped towards a rounded square with its attack pulse and noise burst, Modern a pure sine with a short bright transient; a hit on a ringing tail rises from where it is, without a step.
- Drive (options: a cubic soft clip with first-order antiderivative anti-aliasing, recommended and taken; tanh with the same, about three times the cost): the first version with tanh and a sine and a pitch each sample cost 0.9 % of a block a voice; with the pitch at every fourth sample, phasors and the cubic it costs 0.33.
- One Shot plays the tail out after the key (the voice waits, as for a slice, D207); Gate leaves it to the voice's envelope. A retrigger strikes the drum with the new note's velocity.
- Drive and Shape glide across each block and are mixed in from nothing (Drive over its first 3 dB, Shape by its amount), so a macro that throws them does not click and Drive at 0 dB is the drum untouched.
- The click tests: Succession 808's macros first put Tone on the third, which the tests take for a time macro (D177): reordered to the bank's order, Tone, Punch, Boom, Grit. And the Brightness and Space test, which checks every third preset, moved onto Polyrhythm Pad when Succession 808 was added; it flagged its Brightness sweep at 1.75 times, but the profile shows no spike, only a pad that stays brighter (options: give that test the macros' level rule of D224, recommended and taken; smooth Brightness in the engine; change Polyrhythm Pad). With the rule it reads 0.92.
- `tests/V3KickTests.cpp`: the 808 at 55 Hz sighs at 58.7 Hz and settles at 55.00; Decay is the time to -60 dB on all three; One Shot holds and lets go under -80 dB, Gate never holds; a hit on a ringing drum makes no step; a soft hit is quieter with a smaller sigh; driven to the top every model stays under 1.5 and plays the same twice.
- Succession 808 in Engine Room, a draft for the user's ears (the sketch of the research, levelled to -20 LUFS): the 808 model at C2 when C4 is played, Decay 1.3 s, Sigh 2.7, dark, driven, mono so each hit strikes the ringing drum. A correction to D229: no source found states that the theme's 808s are tuned to the bass, nor any of their settings; C minor at 140 BPM comes from published arrangements, so the preset is a sound-alike.


## D233. The SVF's SEM morph and SID characters (2026-11-20)

The SEM morph (D229, TODO 21) and the SID's filter as a character of the SVF (D230), researched online (`docs/research/svf-sem-sid.md`: the SEM's service notes and Sound On Sound, Simper's and Zavalishin's filter papers, reSID and libresidfp, the SID-Article, Plogue, inSIDious) and built while the user slept; each choice taken as recommended, with its options as a pick list in `docs/overnight-2026-11-20.md`.

- Mode gains three choices at the end (options: a Morph mode and value, recommended and taken; Mode itself continuous; an SEM character): LP>HP, where Morph (a new modulatable value) crossfades the low-pass into the high-pass through the notch at its middle, divided by the larger share so both ends stay at 0 dB; and the SID's LP+BP and BP+HP.
- Character (options: Clean, SID 6581, SID 8580 with a Curve, recommended and taken; one SID character with a chip switch; a SID processor): Clean is the filter as it was, line for line (a test compares every sample with the old code); the 8580 holds the cutoff between 30 Hz and 12.5 kHz and steps the resonance as the chip, 1/Q = 2^((4 - step) / 8); the 6581 reads the knob as the register an 8580 would need and then through reSID's measured 6581 curve (220 Hz at the bottom, 6 kHz falling to 4.6 kHz at the middle, 18 kHz at the top), steps the resonance as 1/Q = (15 - step) / 8 with the last step held at Q 16 (D38), bends each integrator's cutoff with what it integrated last (one-sided distortion, a cutoff that climbs with level), clips its stages unevenly and leaks a little of the dry signal (about -50 dB). Curve moves either chip's cutoff darker or brighter (a quarter octave on the 8580, an octave on the 6581), as chips differ.
- Saved sounds and DAW projects: Mode's longer list changes what a stored value means, so the processor state goes to version 17 and the patch to 16, and an older SVF slot's Mode is rescaled (`migrateSvfModes`), its Morph and Character set to 0; bank presets name their mode and are untouched. A DAW's automation of an SVF's Mode would need redrawing.
- The voice cap prices the 6581 on its own (`svf6581`, 0.40, measured against the clean SVF in the same run); Clean and the 8580 keep the SVF's 0.17.
- `tests/V3SvfTests.cpp`: Clean bit for bit as before in its four modes; the morph from LP through the notch (under -40 dB at the cutoff, 0 dB either side, at a quarter the notch at the cutoff times root 3) to HP; the sums; the 8580's range and steps; the 6581's curve, floor and drop, Curve's brightness, a clean quiet sine and a second harmonic on a loud one; every character and new mode bounded at full resonance and drive; the 6581's top step rings down; an older sound keeps its mode.
