## D236. The 808 as its circuit, built (2026-11-20)

Built while the user was away, who had said to start with what needs no permission, the 808 first. Each decision was taken as recommended and is in `docs/overnight-2026-11-20.md` as its pick list, for review.

The Kick generator's 808 (D232's struck resonator) is replaced by Werner's circuit, as chosen in D235: `src/synth/v3/Tr808BassDrum.h` on `src/synth/v3/CircuitWdf.h`, a wave digital filter whose R-type root holds every element of a sub-circuit, its scattering from modified nodal analysis, op-amps as nullors, the transistors and diodes solved at the root by Newton's method. The four sub-circuits are the thesis's; at 96 kHz the output is within 2.5 % rms of ngspice renders of the whole schematic (1.2 % accented), the sigh within 0.25 Hz, the decay within 3 %.

- R164 (options: 47 kOhm as in the DAFx 2014 paper and the service notes, recommended and taken; the thesis table's 4.7 kOhm with VR6 held stable): with 4.7 kOhm the loop gain is about 8 and the drum oscillates.
- chowdsp_wdf (options: our own root for every element, chowdsp_wdf not added, recommended and taken; chowdsp_wdf for the pulse shaper and output filter alone): the op-amps and devices tie each sub-circuit into one root, so its adaptors had nothing to do; it was never downloaded.
- Q44 (options: an ideal emitter follower, recommended and taken; a transistor like Q43).
- Oversampling (options: none, the voices run at 96 kHz, recommended and taken; 2x inside the circuit).
- The knobs (options: each on the part that does its job, recommended and taken; the 808's own controls only): Decay on VR6 through a table measured on the model (`decayForSeconds`), extended past 500 kOhm to 740 kOhm for the knob's 10 s; Punch on R166, Punch Time on C38, Sigh on R161, Click on the trigger's width (0.1, 1, 10 ms), Accent on the trigger's height (4 to 14 V), Shape on the op-amps' rails (15 to 4 V, a soft clip outside the loop), Tone on VR5; the key on C41 and C42.
- The Tone knob (options: VR5 turned as the C-taper pot the service notes' parts list gives, fitted to Michael Fischer's recordings, a quarter of VR5 left at mid-travel, recommended and taken; linear as the thesis parametrises it): `Tr808BassDrum::toneForKnob`; VR6 is B taper, so Decay stays linear in VR6 ([parts list](https://www.manualslib.com/manual/1217113/Roland-Tr-808.html?page=16)).
- The shortest decay (options: the circuit's floor, 0.17 s at the stock tuning, recommended and taken; a fade after the circuit for shorter settings).
- A hit on a ringing drum (options: for the 808, no steeper than the same hit on a silent drum plus the ring's slope, recommended and taken; a higher fixed limit for every model; a softer 808 attack): the tapered Tone's attack reached 0.0201 against the old limit of 0.02.
- The accent's level (options: the circuit's, the kick test now expecting a soft hit under 0.8 of a full one, recommended and taken; the output scaled with velocity on top).
- The voice cap (options: the 808 priced at its 99th percentile, 6.6 % a voice, as the spiking effects are, recommended and taken; at its average, 3.1 %): `voiceCost::Costs::kick808`, beside the 909's and Modern's 0.41.
- The solver (options: 100 nV with a start from the last samples, recommended and taken; 10 nV): the start is a fixed-point step while every junction is off, a parabola through the last three samples after a sample that solved at once, else the last voltages; a step under 20 uV is taken without evaluating the junctions again. Half the hit's work, the comparison with SPICE unchanged.

Against Fischer's 25 recorded bass drums (analysis only, `COOLSYNTH_808_FISCHER` in `tests/V3Tr808Tests.cpp`): the tail's pitch 49.6 to 50.4 Hz recorded against 49.3 to 49.6 modelled, the sigh within about 1 Hz from Decay 5 up; the recorded unit rings 25 to 35 % longer at the long marks (a part's tolerance; the Decay knob reads seconds and reaches both); the model's first 20 ms about 15 % duller at every mark, left for the user's ears.

Also: the rest state is solved once for the process (`Tr808BassDrum::warmUp`, from `Part::prepare`), so a slot switching to the Kick on the audio thread only copies it; the per-sample loops run on plain pointers, as checked indexing cost more than the arithmetic in the Debug build the cap is measured in. Succession 808 levelled again (master 0.5 to 2.0 dB). Left for the user's ears: the 808 circuit, Succession 808, the 909 and Modern.

## D237. The 909 to become its circuit too (2026-11-20)

The user asked whether the Kick's 909 is built on the 808. It is not: D232's 909 is a behavioural model (a sine with a pitch envelope, Shape bending it toward a square, a click pulse and a noise burst), and the real TR-909 kick is not 808-based either: Roland built it as a small analog voice, a triangle VCO swept by a pitch envelope, a shaper toward a sine, a VCA with its own decay, an attack of a pulse and filtered noise ([Roland](https://www.roland.com/id/products/rc_tr-909/features/), [CDM](https://cdm.link/2020/05/make-a-909-kick-on-the-make-noise-0-coast-and-more-drum-modeling-fun/), [Tiptop BD909](https://www.kraftmusic.com/tiptop-audio-bd909-analog-bass-drum-module.html)). Today's Shape is also the wrong way round: a sine pushed toward a square, where the 909 rounds a triangle toward a sine.

- The 909 engine (options: its circuit stage by stage as the 808, checked against SPICE and a real 909's recordings, recommended and chosen; a behavioural model from its stages, a triangle through a fitted shaper; keep it until heard). The user: engine work first, and check online implementations before designing. The research is `docs/research/kick-909-circuit.md`: no paper models any 909 voice; operator (Rust, AGPL) is the one circuit-level model, mw909 (JUCE, AGPL) the best write-up with measurements; real machines measure 49.5 Hz and a 62 ms hold where the schematic gives 56 Hz and 47 to 50 ms.

The user's choices on the research's questions:
- Where (options: replace today's 909, recommended and chosen; a fourth Model, 909 Circuit). No factory preset uses the 909.
- The oscillator (options: a hybrid, the analog blocks in `CircuitWdf.h` and the integrator and comparator as an exact triangle flipped at the true crossing with polyBLAMP corners, recommended and chosen; everything in the WDF solver, two states switched at the nearest sample, oversampled; a behavioural phase counter).
- How far (options: the whole drum, recommended and chosen: pitch envelope, oscillator, diode shaper with Q12, decay envelope with its hold and accent clamp, the attack's click and noise, the accent buffer; the body only; the oscillator and shaper only).
- Calibration (options: stock values, then only the uncertain parts fitted to recordings, recommended and chosen; stock only; fitted freely).
- Key tracking (options: C14 scaled with the note, recommended and chosen; the offset R27; none).
- References and downloads, all outside git and for analysis only (options, several allowed: the Rob Roy 1995 set, recommended and chosen; an RC4558 SPICE macromodel, recommended and chosen; the service notes PDF and Whittle's and Fraser's mod notes, chosen; the AudioRealism set, chosen once its terms are confirmed).
- Licences (options: facts only from the AGPL and GPL projects, MIT code credited where useful, recommended and chosen; our own code throughout).
- The knobs (options: each on its part, recommended and chosen, each default the stock 909: Punch Time on TUNE, Punch on the sweep's depth, Decay on VR5 through a measured table, Click on ATTACK, Accent on the accent voltage, Shape on the drive mod at R52, Noise on the noise branch; Tone stays the low-pass after the drum, Sigh has no 909 part; the 909's own knobs only).

## D238. The 909 as its circuit, built (2026-11-20)

Built as the user chose in D237, in a session of its own while the user walked through the night's work. The Kick generator's 909 is now the TR-909 bass drum's circuit, `src/synth/v3/Tr909BassDrum.h` on `CircuitWdf.h`, read from the service notes' voicing board (page 11, read at 1200 dpi; the PDF, TI's RC4558 model, Rob Roy's 1995 set and AudioRealism's pack downloaded outside git for analysis only, as allowed). The SPICE netlist is `tools/kick909/bassdrum.cir`, with `render.py`, `inspect.py`, `compare.py`, `recording.py` and `reduce.py` beside it; the references are in `tests/data/kick909`. Stage by stage within 1 % of ngspice (the pulse, C9 and the control voltage, C8, C12, Q9's emitter), the sweep within 0.12 semitones, the output's envelope within 1 dB. The decisions taken on the way, each recommended and taken:

- The schematic's readings (options: as drawn, checked at 1200 dpi, recommended and taken; the research's readings): Q10 is a PNP (the oscillator only runs so; the drawing's default note), R8 returns to Q4's collector (checked again at 800 dpi), D8's clamp is soft (R36 and R22 have 8.2 kOhm behind them), Q12's and Q6's emitters sit on the mixer's virtual ground. The trigger is 5 V for 2 ms, as the service notes' page 5 says.
- The op-amp model for SPICE (options: TI's RC4558 macromodel, the M5218L being a 4558-class part, recommended and taken; an ideal op-amp, which made a poor comparator and was dropped). The C++ model's op-amps are ideal, the comparator swinging to +-13 V as the RC4558 does.
- The transistors and diodes (options: generic Ebers-Moll, Is 10 fA, betaF 300 or 250, betaR 5, and the 1N4148's diode model for the 1SS133, recommended and taken; fitted models): no published models exist for the 2SC2603, 2SA1115 or 1SS133.
- How the drum is cut into circuits (options: at its stiff points, the front, the pitch envelope, the hold, the click, the decay after the hold, the attack VCA and the body, with the oscillator exact beside them, recommended and taken; one circuit for the whole drum, far costlier). The comparator's forcing (D12 into R49, R50) and C8's charging through D9 stay inside one circuit each, as a sample's delay would make them unstable.
- The solver (options: what the 909 needed, recommended and taken): PNP transistors; a diode pair as one port; source stepping when a sample's Newton fails on a hard edge; the parabola start after solves of up to three steps (which also made the 808 cheaper, 0.78 to 0.67 steps a sample, its comparison with SPICE unchanged); a 10 MOhm leak where nodes meet only junctions (Q7's collector, Q11's base, D12), as SPICE's gmin.
- The pitch (options: the stock circuit, C14 following the key, recommended and taken; an effective comparator threshold fitted to 49.4 Hz, which would need a swing past the rails; R27 fitted): SPICE settles at 57.7 Hz, the real units measured (Rob Roy's, mw909's, AudioRealism's) at 49.2 to 49.9 Hz, and nothing in the drawing accounts for it; with the key setting the pitch only the sweep's depth in semitones remains, and the model's sits between Rob Roy's 909 and AudioRealism's.
- The decay (options: C11's leak fitted, 330 kOhm, recommended and taken; Q12's gain fitted; the stock circuit, whose decay lasts 1.85 times the recordings'): as drawn, C11, charged to the accent at Q9's emitter, feeds C8 after the hold; but C11 is an electrolytic whose + is on R44's side, so that charge holds it reverse-biased, and such capacitors leak. With 330 kOhm the times to -20 dB fall within 0 to 10 % of Rob Roy's and those to -40 dB within 5 to 10 %, at every Decay mark; AudioRealism's unit, not used for the fit, agrees within about 15 %.
- The hold (options: stock, recommended and taken; Q3's gain fitted): the model's time to -10 dB matches the recordings at every Decay.
- The accent (options: the DAC from 2 V for a normal hit to 4 V for a full accent, as the service notes' scope drawings show, recommended and taken; fitted to AudioRealism's eleven levels; the whole 0 to 5 V): AudioRealism's body rises 6.8 dB from accent 0 to 100, the model's 7.8 dB from 2 to 4 V; the pack does not say whether its accent 0 is a normal hit, so the drawing's span stays.
- The Decay knob (options: the circuit's reach, 0.15 to 0.5 s on the stock pot and up to 1.05 s with VR5 bent to 10 MOhm, longer settings staying there, recommended and taken; C8 scaled for longer decays; a fade after the drum).
- The output (options: a 10 Hz DC block after the mixer, as the drum leaves the machine through a capacitor, recommended and taken; none, which leaves Q12's base current as a slow thump): Q12's base current reaches the mixer with its signal.
- The noise (options: a seeded white source with the 4006s' density, recommended and taken; the 31-bit shift register at its clock): the register's period is hours, inaudible.
- The knobs (D237's mapping): Punch Time is the sweep's time constant through TUNE (6.8 ms, TUNE fully down, at the default 6 ms; up to about 109 ms), Punch the sweep's depth through R26 (330 kOhm at the default 12, double the depth each 12 more), Click turns ATTACK, Noise and Shape bend R10 and R52 from their stock values at 0.
- The voice cap (options: the 909 priced at its 99th percentile, 33.3 % of a block a voice, as the 808 is, recommended and taken; at its average, 10.7 %; made cheaper first): `voiceCost::Costs::kick909`. Its work is in the hit, the first 20 ms near 28 % of real time a voice, 9 % once the hold is over; under the 50 % budget a 909 sound plays one voice, as the machine has one bass drum circuit. No factory preset uses the 909.
- Kick tests (options: the 909's Decay checked as the time to -60 dB, as its table is measured, recommended and taken; the old steady-rate check, which a held and then accelerating decay cannot meet).

Against AudioRealism's pack (`COOLSYNTH_909_AUDIOREALISM`, its terms confirmed: free to use, not to be redistributed), the first 6 ms show where the click goes wrong: both real units stay within -0.15 of the body's peak during the trigger and then strike (-1.1 at ATTACK 0, a spike of -1.9 at full); the model, as SPICE, ramps to -0.4 and -0.7 during the trigger, as R8 holds Q9's emitter at the accent and C11 passes that step to the attack's VCA. SPICE with R8 to ground matches the units' quiet trigger and their decay with no leak fitted, but ATTACK then does nothing; so the real units differ around C11, an electrolytic held reverse-biased, in a way the drawing does not show. Details in `docs/research/kick-909-circuit.md`.

The user's answers to the three left open: Decay past 1 s stays at the circuit's reach (recommended); the click's path through C11 to be investigated now (recommended); the voice cap's price to wait until the 909 is made cheaper (the user's choice over pricing it now at its 99th percentile, or at its average), the 33.3 % standing meanwhile. Left for the user's ears: both circuits.

## D239. The 909's R8 read to -15 V (2026-11-20)

Investigating the 909's click (D238), with the user's answers on the way. The full account is in `docs/research/kick-909-circuit.md` ("R8 to -15 V" and "The click, read again").

- R8 (options as first put: R8 to -15 V with the fitted leak dropped, recommended and chosen; the drawing with the 330 kOhm leak): the service notes print R8, Q9's emitter resistor, to Q4's collector, and so did the model; operator reads it to -15 V. With -15 V, Q9 is a working follower, C11 sits forward-biased as its + marking says, both real units' quiet 2 ms trigger appears and the accent spans 6.0 dB (the real 6.0 to 6.8; as printed 9.3), all with nothing fitted. The netlist (`tools/kick909/bassdrum.cir`) and the model changed, the SPICE references were rendered again, and the decay after the hold became an RC followed exactly (no circuit), which is also cheaper.
- R8 again, once the decays were measured (options: R8 to -15 V and keep digging, recommended and chosen; back to the drawing and the leak, which matched every decay within about 15 %; -15 V plus a fitted tail): long decays land on the recordings, but short decays' tails fall faster (Decay 0: -40 dB at 90 ms against 150) and long ones reach -10 dB later (165 ms against 110 to 155). The Kick's Decay table was measured again (`decay909ForSeconds`: 0.1 to 1.13 s), so its knob still lands on the time to -60 dB.
- The envelope check against SPICE now traces each output through its own half-cycles (within 0.2 dB, the limit 0.5 dB), as the ideal comparator's 1.9 % higher pitch drifts fixed windows' phase on the steeper decay.

Still open and being investigated, as the user chose: the click, which reaches the output some 20 dB weaker than the real units' in SPICE under every reading tried, and the decay's shape at the short and long ends.

## D240. The 909 checked against both open models: its click and its triangle (2026-11-20)

The user asked for every assumption to be checked: each reading of `tools/kick909/bassdrum.cir` and `Tr909BassDrum.h` against shoreline's (operator's new name) and mw909's readings and measurements, and against the user's colour scan of the service notes and the block diagram (the trigger is 5 V for 2 ms from latches IC1 and IC10; each drum's accent a 0 to 5 V D/A; the pulse generator Q8, Q9 fed by the trigger alone). Neither gets the click from the drawing as printed: shoreline reads Q8 as a switch, mw909 fits a pulse to its recordings. Each difference was tried in SPICE against both real units. The full account is in `docs/research/kick-909-circuit.md` ("Checked against shoreline and mw909").

- The click (options: Q8 read as a PNP switch and R495 kept, recommended and chosen; Q8 as an NPN follower, as before). With R6 from +15 V to the base and R5 to the pulse node, a PNP is the textbook high-side switch: on at rest, holding C5 near +15 V, off while the pulse lasts, snapping back at its end; the scans cannot show the symbol's arrow. R495 (150 kOhm from Q6's base to ground) is printed "SN up to 371499 surface mounted", read as a part added on the foil side of early boards and in the layout of later ones. With both, SPICE and the model give both units' quiet trigger and their spike at the body's start, peak against body 1.17, 1.36 and 1.90 at ATTACK 0, half and full against AudioRealism's 1.15, 1.34 and 1.91, nothing fitted.
- The triangle (options: an effective comparator threshold near 4.9 V, recommended and chosen; the drawing's 4.16 V). As drawn the triangle peaks at 4.16 V: SPICE settles at 58 Hz and the tone is too round (H3 -40 dB against the units' -26). R50, printed 47 kOhm, as 60 kOhm gives 49.6 Hz and H2 to H7 of -22.5, -26.4, -39.9, -39.8, -65, -40.8 dB against AudioRealism's -27.9, -25.9, -44.6, -41.3, -46.9, -41.6: two separate measurements agree on one value, as mw909 found (4.884 V). The one fitted part. The Kick still sets the pitch by C14, so in it this is the timbre.
- H2 (options: investigate after the above with no fit unless a physical cause shows, recommended and chosen; leave it). H2 does not move with Q12's betaF or betaR or with a diode mismatch; only with how far Q12's collector swings (R43 as 22 Ohm gives -29.5 dB). R43 is printed 47 Ohm and nothing points elsewhere, so it stays: open, 6 dB over the units'.
- The decay: mw909 measured its unit's DECAY pot at 39 k, 165 k, 284 k, 447 k and 1.299 M at the five marks, far from a clean audio taper. With those the model's decay lands on AudioRealism's at every mark (Decay half: 105, 165, 230 ms to -10, -20, -40 dB against 110, 170, 225; full: 165, 285, 445 against 155, 275, 470; Decay 0's -40 dB stays short, 115 against 150). The recordings comparisons now use them; the Kick's Decay knob maps seconds through its own table, measured again (0.1 to 1.06 s).
- C12 while the attack rests: with R495, Q6 turns off once C12 falls under about 2.5 V, so the attack circuit rests sooner (cheaper); C12 then drains through R42 and R495 as an exact RC and the circuit takes it back on the next hit.
- Checked and kept: Q10 a PNP (mw909's opposite polarity would latch the loop); R36 to +15 V (with mw909; shoreline returns it to Q4's collector); Q12's emitter on the mixer (with shoreline; the other way in SPICE is no better on the decay and worse on the harmonics); Q2's emitter straight on +15 V (mw909 adds 100 Ohm, not on the drawing); the hold from the circuit (its time to -10 dB matches the recordings); the accent from 2 to 4 V (7.5 dB on the peak, 5.9 on the body, against the units' 6.0 and 6.8).
- Left open: the noise generator's clock and so its level (mine 380 kHz; shoreline reads 137.7 kHz, 4.4 dB hotter; mw909 455 kHz; the service notes give none), and H2.

## D241. Next after the 909: the TB-303, then the whole 808 (2026-11-20)

Seeing how much one instrument had taken, the user asked whether the whole 808 could come into the synth. Both of CoolSynth's circuit drums are bass drums only: a TR-808 has 16 sounds, all analog; a TR-909 has eleven, seven of them analog circuits and four (the hats and cymbals) 6-bit samples in ROM. The 808's other voices fall into about five circuit families: the bridged-T toms, congas, rim shot and claves (the bass drum's machinery); the snare (two bridged-T networks and noise); the bank of six Schmitt-trigger oscillators that the cowbell, cymbal and both hats filter; the clap and maracas. The cymbal and the cowbell have published circuit models (Werner, Abel and Smith, ICMC 2014 and AES 2014), as the bass drum has.

- The whole 808 (options: yes, staged, after the 909 step, chosen; only a few voices; not now).
- Its shape (options: one kit generator, each key an instrument; separate generators like the Kick; both, chosen).
- Its fidelity (options: circuit with SPICE and recordings spot-checked; a full audit as the 909's; behavioural): the user answered with its use, house music. Its fidelity and order are to be put again when its stage starts.
- What the Chicago knob-turning sound is: the TB-303 Bass Line (Phuture's "Acid Tracks"), not the 808, whose knobs are each voice's level, tone, decay and tuning. CoolSynth's Acid preset is a sound-alike (a VA saw into the driven Ladder, with the step sequencer's slides).
- What comes next (options: the TB-303 as its circuit, recommended; the whole 808 as chosen; both, the 303 first, chosen).
- The 909 made cheaper and priced (D238): postponed by the user until after the whole 808. Its 33.3 % price stands meanwhile. The largest saving found so far: since Q8 is a PNP (D240) the click circuit never comes to rest.

The order: the TB-303 as its circuit (TODO 32), the whole 808 (TODO 33), then the 909's cost and price (TODO 30).

## D242. The TB-303 generator, from Open303 (2026-11-20)

The first of D241's order, built on the research in `docs/research/tb-303-circuit.md`. The user's choices, each put as a pick list:
- The reference (options: SPICE and published numbers, recommended and chosen; an owner's recordings first; by ear only). No free recordings of a real 303 at known settings exist.
- How it is built (options: our own circuit model in `CircuitWdf.h`, recommended; TriggerFish's BSD code ported; Open303's engine, chosen). Open303 is a tuned sound-alike, so the checks are its outputs against published figures, and it is corrected where it misses them.
- Where it lives (options: a TB-303 generator, recommended and chosen; the diode ladder as a filter; both).
- Where Open303's code lives (options: vendored in `external/open303`, recommended; a patched submodule; rewritten into our own files, chosen): `src/synth/v3/Tb303Generator.h`, Open303's MIT notice kept in it, credited in the README.
- The accent (options: on or off at a velocity threshold as the 303's, recommended and chosen; scaled by velocity): on at velocity 100 of 127, Open303's threshold.
- The knobs (options: the 303's own and the slide, recommended and chosen; with the Devil Fish's extras): Waveform, Cutoff (314 to 2394 Hz, the 303's range as Open303 measured it), Resonance, Env Mod, Decay (200 to 2000 ms), Accent, Slide.

Taken on the way, each by the user's rule or as the only way:
- A legato hook in the engine (`Slot::legato`, called from `Voice::legato`): the layer in legato only retargeted the pitch, so a generator could not know it slid, nor hear the slid note's accent. A correction to D241 and TODO 32: CoolSynth's 16-step sequencer's slides move a modulation value, not notes; a 303 slide is a legato note (from MIDI or the arpeggiator).
- Real time (the codebase's rule): Open303's two wavetables (some 210 kB each, per instance) became one shared bank built at `Part::prepare`; its note list (a `std::list`, allocating on every note) gave way to the layer's.
- Oversampling (options: the core at 2x inside the voice's 96 kHz, recommended and taken; 4x, as Open303 runs at 44.1 kHz): 192 kHz sits near Open303's own 176.4 kHz; at 4x the harmonics agree within 0.1 to 0.5 dB.
- Corrected toward the published figures: the slide's time constant, 12 ms in Open303, is the 303's 22 ms (100 kOhm and 220 nF, C35; its corner measured at 7.2 to 7.5 Hz), Slide now the time to 95 %, 66 ms by default.
- Checked and kept: the filter matches Stinchcombe's analysis within 0.1 dB across seven octaves; it does not self-oscillate, as a stock 303's does not; the square's duty is 46.9 % (the x0xb0x's about 45 %).
- The output level: a held note with the filter open peaks near 0.9, an accent near 1.06.
- The Acid preset (Basement) now plays the TB-303 in legato, levelled to -20 LUFS (master +4.5 dB); its macros are Cutoff, Squelch (resonance and env mod), Decay and Wobble.
- The voice cap (options: priced at the average over its voices in its hardest settings, 1.36 % of a block a voice, as the steady generators are, recommended and taken; at its 99th percentile, 1.69 %): about seven VA voices, chiefly its 2x core and the twelfth-order quarter-band filter.

Left: the user's ears; a check against a real 303's recordings when an owner's turn up; the accent's sweep (Open303's 15 ms against Whittle's 47 kOhm and 1 uF and TriggerFish's 47 kOhm and 33 nF, to settle from the service notes); whether the 303's own step sequencer (accent and slide per step) comes into the layer's.

## D245. The listening pass's first answers: three presets out, one in, the repeated-key click (2026-11-21)

The bank's one voice per key stays.

## D246. FL Studio links the MiniLab to our script by itself: the script claims the MiniLab's hardware ID (2026-11-21)

- The user asked for FL to pick our MiniLab script by itself (TODO C4). FL's documentation (the scripting API's
  script metadata, the MIDI settings page) says a script is linked by a matching device name or hardware ID, and
  nothing on which of two matching scripts wins.
- Found in FL Studio 2025 with the MiniLab: FL linked ours by the port name while it did not know the MiniLab's ID.
  The moment it first learned the ID (the MiniLab's answer to an identity request, sent here from FL's script
  console), it loaded Arturia's own MiniLab 3 script in place of ours, which had been chosen by hand. Once FL knows
  the ID, a stored choice stays: generic, Arturia's or ours, through restarts and repeated answers. Identity
  requests from outside FL changed nothing.
- Done: our script's header claims the same ID (`supportedHardwareIds=00 20 6B 02 00 04 04`, Arturia's family and
  model bytes, the firmware left out) beside the port names. It cannot do worse than before: without it, the first ID
  always handed the MiniLab to Arturia's.
- Not yet seen: the first ID with both scripts claiming it. FL keeps learned IDs where they could not be found (not
  its registry keys, not its files), and the MiniLab's other ports do not answer identity requests. To check on a
  fresh FL. The README tells users who used the MiniLab in FL before to pick CoolSynth MiniLab 3 once.

## D247. Ableton Live loads the MiniLab's control surface by itself (2026-11-21)

- The user asked for the same in Ableton. Live loads a control surface by itself when its script's
  `get_capabilities` names the device (USB vendor and product IDs, model name) and sets `auto_load`; read from Live's
  own scripts (`_Framework/Capabilities`, `MiniLab_3`: vendor 7285, product 8715, "Minilab3"). Ours had none.
- Found in Live 12.4.16 here: the MiniLab is product 0x020B (523) under Arturia's USB MIDI driver; 0x220B (8715) is
  the other ID in that driver's list and the only one Live's MiniLab_3 claims. Claiming 8715 alone, Live loaded
  nothing at startup or after the user replugged the MiniLab. With both, Live put CoolSynth_MiniLab in an empty slot
  at startup.
- A script that claims a device starts only after Live has asked it for its identity through the slot's output: with
  output None it was assigned but never created. So the script now has an output port too (`SCRIPT` only: no track
  MIDI goes there, and the script sends nothing; CoolSynth's driver ignores the MiniLab's identity answer).
- Of two scripts claiming the MiniLab, Live loaded one, the first by name (tried with throwaway scripts named before
  and after ours). Ours sorts before MiniLab_3; whether Live's own scripts sort with the user's is not known, and on
  this computer no built-in script matches the MiniLab to try it. Where the MiniLab is 0x220B (likely the Mac), to
  check. The README tells users with an earlier control surface on the MiniLab to choose ours once.
- Checked by the user's hands afterwards: the pads switch sections and play no note, the keys play. But only once
  the track was armed: Live arms the selected track by itself only when a control surface does it (Live's own
  MiniLab_3 does, ours did not, and with ours stopped Live did not either). Options: add auto-arm, recommended and
  chosen; leave it to the player. Ours now does what Live's own `ableton.v2` AutoArmComponent does: the selected
  MIDI track gets `implicit_arm`; not while a Push app runs, and not while Exclusive Arm is on and another track is
  armed by hand; again whenever tracks, their arm, input or freezing change. Seen in Live: the arm follows the
  selected MIDI track, an audio track is left alone.
- Also found: Live shows a plugin's parameters on its panel by itself only up to 64 (Live manual, "Using
  Plug-Ins"); CoolSynth has many more, so its panel starts empty and Configure adds what is turned (checked: the
  first macro and Brightness came in). Live lists no VST3 presets in its browser (only VST2 and AU ones), so the
  sounds are chosen in CoolSynth's own panel there. The macros reach Live as "A Macro 1" to "A Macro 4"; the user
  chose to give hosts the sounds' macro names (D248).

## D248. Hosts see the sounds' macro names (2026-11-21)

- The user's choice (options: to the to-do list, recommended; research and do it now, chosen; leave it).
- Researched: VST3 renames through `restartComponent(kParamTitlesChanged)`, CLAP through a parameter-info rescan;
  JUCE 9.0.5 sends the first for changed titles only and clap-juce-extensions the second, both from
  `updateHostDisplay(...withParameterInfoChanged(true))`, already used for slot names (D228). Hosts: REAPER clears
  its parameter info on the VST3 notice (its changelog, 6.57); Ableton Live follows renames at run time but ignores
  them inside `setState` (Steinberg developer forum, 2025; Serum 2's 2.0.21 notes a workaround for Live); Studio One
  follows them; Bitwig's remote controls keep static names; FL Studio and current Cubase are not documented. Surge
  XT does the same for its macros, keeping the number ("M1: ...").
- Built: `MacroParameter` names itself "A1 Bite" from the playing layer's sound, "A Macro 1" for an empty layer or a
  sound without names; the plugin's 100 Hz timer sets the names and tells the host. A loaded state tells a CLAP host
  at once and a VST3 host from the timer, after the load, so Live takes the names of a reopened project (the slot
  names of D228 now go the same way).
- Seen in the DAWs: Ableton Live 12.4 (VST3) shows "A1 Bite" and "A2 Detune" on its panel and turns them into "A1
  Bright" and "A2 Squelch" when the panel loads Pluck Drop. FL Studio 2025 (CLAP) lists "Controls/A1 Bite" and,
  after the switch, names the knob "Controls/A1 Bright" in its hint bar; its parameter list kept the old names on
  screen. Not seen: a reopened project in Live (saving is off in this Live), FL's VST3, Reaper, Cubase.
- Bitwig Studio 6.1 (CLAP), the same day: its remote controls and parameter list show "A1 Bite" and turn into "A1
  Bright" after the switch, within a moment. Zrythm 1.0 (CLAP and VST3): a new CoolSynth lists "A1 Bite", but the
  names stay as loaded after a switch, and a project keeps the names it was saved with (the 20 November project
  still says "A Macro 1"): Zrythm reads names once.

## D249. Bitwig: a CoolSynth MiniLab 3 controller script (2026-11-21)

- Found when the user installed Bitwig Studio 6.1 (trial): Bitwig set up its own Arturia MiniLab 3 controller by
  itself (its Controllers extension finds the MiniLab by the port name "Minilab3 MIDI"). Keys played, the pads
  switched CoolSynth's sections and the screen stayed CoolSynth's, but that script launches clips with the pads: one
  press had started the transport and recorded a clip into the armed track, unseen.
- Options: our own Bitwig script, recommended and chosen; the README telling users to remove Bitwig's; leave it.
- Built: `resources/daw-scripts/bitwig/CoolSynth/CoolSynthMiniLab3.control.js`, a JavaScript controller script (API
  17) on the MiniLab's input only, found by the same port name. Its note input takes every message but channel 10's
  notes (the pads), so the keys, knobs, bend, wheel and pedal reach the tracks and the pads do not; it sends nothing.
  The installer copies it to `Documents\Bitwig Studio\Controller Scripts\CoolSynth`.
- Seen: with both scripts installed Bitwig sets up neither by itself and offers both under the detected MiniLab, also
  after a restart; with ours chosen, the user's keys played, the pads switched sections, and Bitwig neither started
  nor recorded anything. Not seen: what Bitwig does on a computer that never had its own set up (it may still take
  its own); the README says to choose ours there.
- Also seen in Bitwig: the CLAP's remote control pages (D216) follow the panel (Macros, then Arp when the panel
  showed Arp) and list every page. The Arp page's four knobs all read "Layer A ..." there, the names cut before the
  part that tells them apart. Options: shorten them, recommended and chosen; leave them. The arp's parameters are now
  "A Arp Rate", "B Arp Gate" and so on, as "A Glide" is; their IDs stay, so projects and automation keep working.

## D250. The whole 808: its direction (2026-11-21)

The whole TR-808 (TODO 33) resumes after 0.5.0, now that the kicks passed the user's ears, with its direction
chosen first as D241 and D243 asked. Searched online again (`docs/research/tr-808-voices.md`, "How others model
them"): papers exist for the bass drum, the cymbal and the cowbell only; Roland's ACB and AudioRealism's ADM claim
component models and publish nothing; TS-808, Mz808 and WDR-8 read the circuits and tune them to samples; Plaits,
Cherry Audio's Transistor 808 and iO-808 are behavioural. Nobody publishes a cost per voice, and nothing open is
checked against a real 808 beyond the bass drum. The user's choices, each the recommended option:

- Fidelity (options: every voice its full circuit as the bass drum, each stage against ngspice and then Fischer's
  recordings, recommended and chosen; full circuits with the six-oscillator bank by Werner's block method; block
  models for every voice, as Werner's papers; behavioural sound-alikes fitted to the recordings).
  - Estimated, not measured: 2 to 7 % of a block per instrument while it rings, nothing at rest. A whole kit ringing
    at once could pass a sound's 50 % budget, so each circuit rests between hits and the kit's price is measured.
- The voices (options: all sixteen sounds from the eleven circuits, recommended and chosen; the house set only, the
  clap, hats, cowbell, congas, rim shot and claves).
- The kit in a layer (options: three family generators that fill one layer's three slots, recommended and chosen;
  one Kit generator past the slot's 12 knobs; a kit where each hit is its own voice, as the Sample generator's kits;
  separate generators only, kits as sounds of layers).
  - 808 Drums (bass drum, toms and congas, rim shot and claves), 808 Snare & Clap (snare, clap, maracas), 808 Metal
    (cowbell, cymbal and hats on six oscillators that run all the time).
  - Each instrument at its General MIDI drum key, one circuit each (a tom and its conga share one, as on the
    machine), struck while it rings; the closed hat cuts the open. The 808's 22 knobs fit the slots' 36.
  - Each family also plays alone: D241's kit and separate generators at once. The engine needs a generator that
    reads keys as instruments; layer B stays free.
- The kit preset (options: one stock 808 Kit in Studio 1982, every knob at 12 o'clock where the service notes' page
  14 measures each voice, macros Tone, Decay, Tune and Accent, recommended and chosen; a stock and a house kit; none
  yet).
- The order (options: out from the bass drum, recommended and chosen; house's voices first; the published models
  first): the bass drum's gaps, the rest of 808 Drums (toms, congas, rim shot, claves), 808 Metal (cowbell, hats,
  cymbal), then 808 Snare & Clap (snare, clap, maracas).
- The bass drum's gaps (options: first, recommended and chosen; with the toms; after the kit; leave them): the attack
  dull with its click 3 to 6 dB weak, Decay 0 out of reach, the sigh settling too fast, Punch Time's glide past
  6 ms. The toms, congas, rim shot and claves take the same kind of trigger into a bridged-T, so a fault there would
  carry into them.
- The 909's open work (options: after the 808 as D241 ordered, recommended and chosen; before the 808; after the 808,
  its cost only): made cheaper and priced, then its second harmonic.

Each voice is still designed with the user (its knobs, bends and checks as options) before it is built.


## D251. The 808 bass drum's gaps, found and closed (2026-11-21)

The whole 808's first step (D250): the bass drum's gaps against two real units (TODO 30,
`docs/research/kick-808-recordings.md`). Each traced in ngspice and the model, the service notes read for the trigger
(the user's PDF, pp. 4, 8, 9), searched online first (Werner's thesis ramps the trigger's edges only to ease its
solver; ChowKick and Mz808 drive an ideal step; the 808's parts are 2SC945(P), 2SA733(P) and 1S2473, with no
published model of the 2SC945's reverse gain). The user's choices:

- The trigger stage (options: as drawn, Q32's follower and R113, Q40's PNP switch with R154, R155 and Q39 letting go
  after the pulse, with the pulse shaper and the envelope's input, recommended and chosen; Q40 alone behind an ideal
  line; an ideal one-way source; steeper edges only). The trigger node drains slowly when the pulse ends instead of
  being driven to zero, which pulls the first lobe back as the units do.
- The diodes (options: the standard 1N4148, Is 2.52 nA with its emission coefficient 1.752, recommended and chosen;
  the thesis's, which keeps the Is without the coefficient and turns on at 0.3 V; fitted to the recordings).
- The voice's amp guard (options: a voice whose generators all start each note at rest opens at once from silence,
  recommended and chosen; keep the 0.5 ms guard, which faded in 3.7 dB of the click; a shorter guard for every sound).
  The Kick's 808 and 909 say so (`startsFromRest`); the attack knob's bottom (0.5 ms) then means at once, a longer
  attack is kept, and a key struck again while the voice sounds keeps the guard.
- DECAY 0 (options: the Decay table measured again from VR6 = 0, recommended and chosen; keep its stop at 25 kOhm;
  also a fade below). Under 25 kOhm the last 20 dB are C43's slow component, so VR6 = 0 stands in the table at its
  body's time to -40 dB scaled to 60 dB, 0.117 s.
- Punch Time past 6 ms (the user's earlier choice: a glide, not the held beep). Options first: a capacitor on Q43's
  base charged through a diode and 10 kOhm, recommended and chosen; the same with C38 longer; keep the plateau. In
  SPICE that bend held Q42's collector at 5 V instead of 11 for the whole envelope (halving the retrigger) and
  reshaped the first lobe at any size, so it was put again (options: a bend beside Q43, a second transistor across
  R165 whose base capacitor the trigger charges through a diode and drains through its base and 1 MOhm, recommended
  and chosen; Q43's base charged by one packet, which still reshaped the first lobe; keep the plateau for now). Its
  size scales the capacitor up and the charging resistor down; the glide comes halfway down in 7.8 ms times Punch
  Time over 6 ms, up to some 0.65 s. A fixed capacitor with a longer drain stopped at 35 ms (Q43B's own base current
  drains it first).
- The TONE taper (options: fitted again to both units after the fixes, recommended and chosen; keep the old fit;
  a textbook C taper): about 7 % of VR5 left at mid-travel (b = 190), against the old fit's 26 %.
- The sigh, if still faster than both units after the fixes (options: record it, recommended; fit C39 within its
  tolerance, chosen; investigate further). C39 at 1.2 times, the top of its tolerance: the slowest sigh, and the
  body's 2nd and 3rd harmonics within 0.1 dB of both units (they were 1.6 to 3.3 dB low). Tried and not enough on
  their own: Q43's saturation current and reverse gain, R159.
- The price (options: made cheaper first, then priced, recommended and chosen; priced at once at 14.3 % a voice; at
  its 6.2 % average). Cheaper without changing the sound: a stock resonator without the glide's devices, the bent one
  running only past 6 ms (the ringing state passed across as the knob crosses), and the envelope generator split out
  of the front again, Q41's base the front's load as a diode (in saturation its base current is Is (1 / betaF +
  1 / betaR) e^(vbe / VT)). The hit's first 30 ms went from 16 to 18 % of real time to 4.5 to 9.7, the tail from 4.6
  to 5 % to 2 to 3.8. Priced at its 99th percentile at its hardest (glide on): 11.0 % of a block a voice, its average
  6.3 (before: 6.6 and 3.1; with the trigger stage before the split, 14.3).
- Succession 808 (options: Tone 0.35 to 0.17, which leaves VR5 as the user passed it, recommended and chosen; keep
  0.35, about twice as bright in its tone corner under the new taper; revoice it by ear). Levelled again.

Taken on the way, each by the codebase's rules or as the only way:
- The ngspice netlist (`tools/kick808/bassdrum.cir`) has the trigger stage as drawn, the standard 1N4148, the glide
  bend (absent at 0), C39 and C38 as parameters, single-pole 3 MHz op-amps (an ideal one cannot pass the edge) and
  junction capacitances for its solver; its trigger rises half a sample in, where the model's step falls.
- The model drops the thesis's solver aids: C0 at Q41's base (the envelope a sample late) and C40's alpha transform
  (the spike 7 % low). Q44 loads its base as the real one does, (betaF + 1)(R173 || R174). Every stage within 1.5 %
  of ngspice (the accented hit 0.7 %); the trigger's edges may take up to 75 Newton steps, every sample solves.
- The Kick test of the 808's decay keeps the stock Sigh: Sigh moves R161, part of the bridged-T's damping.
- Found by the determinism tests: the Punch Time knob's 6 ms arrives as 6.0000005 ms, which had switched the glide
  on at a vanishing size, so the default drum ran the bent resonator; within 0.01 % of 6 ms is now stock. A rest
  starts from the stock resonator and the stock tuning, as a fresh drum does. A 909 hit after a reset still differs
  from a fresh one (TODO 30; no preset uses the 909).
- Recordings: Audiorealism's 808 pack copied into `tools/references/audiorealism-808` and the service notes into
  `tools/references/tr808-service` (both git-ignored, analysis only).

Measured again through the plugin on all 25 settings of each unit: the click within -1.2 to +2.4 dB of Fischer's and
0.9 dB of Audiorealism's (before 3.2 to 6.2 dB weak), the first lobe within 0.17 ms, DECAY 0 at 94 ms to -40 dB
(units 77 and 98), the sigh within 2 ms of Fischer's unit. Left open: both ends of TONE about 1 dB darker than the
units, Audiorealism's slower and shallower sigh, Fischer's DECAY 0 shorter than the circuit's floor.


## D252. 808 Drums: its design (2026-11-21)

The second family of the whole 808 (D250): the bass drum, the low, mid and high tom or conga (one circuit a pair, as
on the machine, its SW8-like switch set by the key), the rim shot or claves. Read from the service notes (p. 6, the
circuit text; p. 9, the drawing; p. 14, each voice's frequencies and decay at three TUNING positions: LT 80 to
100 Hz, MT 120 to 160, HT 165 to 220, MC 250 to 310, HC 370 to 455, claves 2.5 kHz, rim shot 455 and 1667 Hz; the
low conga's row repeats the high tom's). The toms and congas take the bass drum's kind of trigger (a PNP switch,
Q51 for the low pair) into a multi-feedback bridged-T whose pitch drop comes from the diode pair D80, D81 beside the
TUNING pot; the low tom adds pink noise. The user's choices:

- How notes reach the circuits (options: a kit voice, a layer mode where one voice holds the family generators,
  each note strikes its instrument in that voice, note-offs are ignored and nothing is stolen, recommended and
  chosen; the layer's mono mode through the strike hooks; a voice per key).
- The two knobs past the 808's own ten (bass drum Level, Tone, Decay; each pair's Level and Tuning; rim shot/claves
  Level) (options: Accent and a kit Tune, recommended; Accent and Punch Time, the bass drum's glide, chosen; Accent and
  a decay bend for the toms). Accent moves each hit's trigger between 4 and 14 V with its velocity, as the Kick's.
- The keys (options: General MIDI with Roland's TR-8S alternates, recommended and chosen: BD 36 and 35, RS 37, LT 43
  and 41, MT 47 and 45, HT 50 and 48, LC 64, MC 63, HC 62, claves 75; one key each).
  [TR-8S MIDI implementation chart](https://static.roland.com/assets/media/pdf/TR-8S_MIDIImpleChart_eng02_W.pdf).
- The checks (options: each circuit stage by stage against ngspice, then Fischer's and Audiorealism's recordings and
  page 14, recommended and chosen; ngspice and page 14 only).

## D253. 808 Drums: built (2026-11-21)

Built as designed (D252): the Kit play mode (`Part.h`), the 808 Drums generator (`Tr808DrumsGenerator.h`) on the
bass drum's circuit and two new ones, the toms and congas (`Tr808Tom.h`) and the rim shot and claves
(`Tr808RimShot.h`), each checked stage by stage against ngspice (`tools/tr808`, `tests/V3Tr808DrumsTests.cpp`),
then against Fischer's and Audiorealism's recordings and page 14 (`docs/research/808-drums.md`). The user's
choices while building:

- The claves as drawn ring on: R313 (390 kOhm) feeds back past the point of oscillation (options: fit R313 to both
  units' decay, recommended and chosen, 500 kOhm; fit R312 instead, which moves the rim shot too; keep 390 kOhm).
- The toms and congas rang 3 to 28 % longer than both units (options: fit each pair's feedback resistor to both
  units' tom and conga decays, recommended and chosen, R230 865 kOhm, R259 865k, R286 880k; keep 820 kOhm; look
  for the cause first).
- R324, which the service notes leave unprinted (options: fit it to the units' rim shot over their claves,
  recommended and chosen, 130 kOhm; a builder's 220 kOhm).
- The 1S188FM germanium pair, with no published model (options: a typical germanium diode, Is 2 uA, emission 1.3,
  10 Ohm, recommended and chosen; fit it to both units; a silicon 1N4148).

Found while building, each the circuit as drawn or its parts' own data, so no choice:

- The resonators' op-amps keep their gain-bandwidth, a single pole at 3 MHz as in the references: with ideal
  op-amps the high conga was 14 % off its reference and the rim shot 10 %. `CircuitWdf.h` gained a
  transconductance and `addOpAmp`.
- And their output swing, +-14 V, the uPC4558's typical into 10 kOhm: at full accent the rim shot's and the
  claves' bridged-Ts would otherwise swing past the +-15 V rails. The references clamp each op-amp's inner node, the
  model the same (`addClampPair`, a diode pair past an offset).
- The pink noise bus at 130 mV rms over the later boards' band (C202 across R130), through IC24b's 273 Hz low-pass.
- In the Kit mode a strike that lands while the voice's note waits to start (two hits in one block) waits with it;
  each instrument has its own copy of the common trigger stage, so hits at once do not load each other.
- The key does not tune the drums, nor do the slot's pitch and the bend (the Kick is where tuning lives, D252).
- All three toms take the pink noise through their gates (Q52, Q55, Q58); D252 named only the low tom's.

Checked: the toms and congas within 2 % of ngspice at every stage, accented too; the rim shot within 4 % (9 % at
full accent, its swing VCA switching inside a sample in the first 3 ms); the claves 3 cents under their reference
(the bilinear transform's warping at 2.5 kHz). Against both units the pitch lies within 1 to 4 % at the TUNING's
ends and middle, the decays within some 6 %, the unaccented pitch drop as theirs; page 14's unaccented amplitudes
from 20 % under (mid conga) to 27 % over (mid tom). Left open: at full accent every voice runs 1.1 to 4.5 dB over
page 14's accented amplitudes (the service notes' text says each voice is in proportion to its trigger; the toms'
germanium pair is not), with no recording at a known accent level to judge it by.

Priced (`VoiceCost.h`): in the Kit mode the whole kit in its one voice, 54.5 % of a block at its 99th percentile
with every instrument struck at once (average 18.4 %); in the other modes a voice holds one instrument, priced at
the dearest, the rim shot and claves, 16.1 % (the toms 9.4 to 9.6 %, the bass drum 8.4 %).

- The accent's top, found while checking (options: keep the 4 to 14 V trigger the service notes give and the
  circuits as drawn, the gap recorded, recommended and chosen; cap the accent at 12 V, where the bass drum meets
  page 14, for every 808 sound; find an accented recording at a stated AC LEVEL first).

## D254. 808 Metal: its design (2026-11-22)

The third family of the whole 808 (D250): the cowbell, the cymbal, the open hat and the closed hat, read from the
service notes (p. 6, the circuit text; p. 13, the voicing board; p. 14, the table). All four share one bank of six
square-wave oscillators on an HD14584 Schmitt trigger (about 205, 304, 370 and 523 Hz as drawn, the cowbell's two
set by TM1 and TM2 to 540 and 800 Hz; the cymbal paper's figures,
[Werner, Abel, Smith, ICMC-SMC 2014](https://speech.di.uoa.gr/ICMC-SMC-2014/images/VOL_2/1453.pdf)). The cowbell
gates its two through Q14 and Q15 into IC2's band-pass, its envelope falling abruptly after the attack (R82 and C34
beside C9); the cymbal splits all six into three bands by IC3's filters, the highest gated by Q16 and short, the
next by Q17 with the Decay, mixed by the Tone (VR4); the open hat gates IC3's high band through Q27 into the
high-pass Q26, the closed hat the same source through Q30 into Q31, and the closed hat cuts the open through Q23
and R173. The voicing board narrows the trigger to 7 to 14 V (IC2, pins 1 to 3). The user's choices:

- The keys (options: General MIDI with Roland's TR-8S alternates, recommended and chosen: closed hat 42 and 44,
  open hat 46, cymbal 49, 51 and 57, cowbell 56; one key each).
- The five knobs past the 808's own seven (cowbell Level; cymbal Level, Tone, Decay; open hat Level, Decay; closed
  hat Level) (options: Accent, Tune of the six oscillators together, the cowbell's pitch by its trimmers, the closed
  hat's and the cowbell's decay, recommended and chosen; Accent and four decay and tone bends, the oscillators at the
  808's pitch; Accent and Tune only).
- Where the oscillators start when the kit's voice starts from silence (options: phases from the voice's seeded
  random numbers, then running freely across every hit, recommended and chosen; fixed phases, then free; reset at
  every hit).
- The checks (options: each circuit stage by stage against ngspice, then Fischer's and Audiorealism's recordings and
  page 14, any fit put to the user first, recommended and chosen; ngspice and page 14 only).

## D255. 808 Metal: built (2026-11-22)

Built as designed (D254): the 808 Metal generator (`Tr808MetalGenerator.h`, generator type 14) on the voicing
board's shared parts (`Tr808MetalBank.h`: the six oscillators, IC3's two bands, IC2a's narrowed trigger) and three
circuits, the cowbell (`Tr808Cowbell.h`), the cymbal (`Tr808Cymbal.h`) and both hats (`Tr808Hats.h`), each checked
stage by stage against ngspice (`tools/tr808/metal.cir`, `tests/V3Tr808MetalTests.cpp`), then against Fischer's and
Audiorealism's recordings and page 14 (`docs/research/808-metal.md`). The user's choices while building:

- The sample rate the circuits run at (options: the voice rate, 96 kHz at a 48 kHz host, each resonant filter
  prewarped at its peak, spectra within 0.4 dB (cowbell) to 0.8 to 1.1 dB (cymbal, hats) of ngspice and the kit at
  67 % of a block, recommended and chosen; twice the voice rate, decimated by a short half-band keeping the two
  samples' latency, within 0.1 to 0.35 dB but at some 120 to 135 % of a block, more than real time when all four
  ring; the voice rate now and twice it after a cost pass).
- When the five swing VCAs on IC3's bands run (options: all while any instrument sounds, exact, each VCA's coupling
  capacitor following the bands as on the machine, recommended and chosen; each only while its own instrument
  sounds, from the settled state at a hit from rest, cheaper alone (6 to 21 % against 25 to 40 % of a block) but the
  closed hat 0.4 dB quieter from 1 to 5 ms; the kit's price is the same either way).
- The cowbell's tuning (options: the units' 554 and 825 Hz, the mean of Fischer's 558 and 824 and Audiorealism's 550
  and 825, recommended and chosen; the service notes' 540 and 800). TM1 and TM2 are factory trimmers; the notes' tuning
  lies half a semitone down on CB Pitch, whose top is now +2 semitones (TM1's reach).
- The open hat's decay, 6 to 32 % longer than both units across OH DECAY and longer at full accent (options: wait for
  the accent check, recommended and chosen; fit C62 now as if both units were unaccented; keep as drawn for good).
  The user asked whether the recordings' accent can be deduced from voices we trust (2026-11-22): it can, from what
  the accent does that a recording's gain cannot; TODO 33.

Found while building, each the circuit as drawn, its reference's own model or the only way, so no choice:

- A transistor and a diode in series with nothing else at the node between them (Q5 into R122 and D2, Q14 and Q15
  into D3 and D4, Q18 into D12, Q19's emitter feeding only D6 to D8) tie the nonlinear ports' currents: the root's
  matrix is singular and its solve meaningless (the cowbell's envelope charged twenty times too slowly, the cymbal
  diverged). Their junction capacitances, as the references have them (4 pF collector, 8 pF emitter), untie them.
  `CircuitWdf.h` gained `isWellPosed` (the inversion used to go on silently) and `adoptSharedState`.
- metal.cir's first version shorted the main board's trigger transistor Q31's base to the closed hat's follower
  Q31's (one node name for both).
- The oscillators as Werner, Abel and Smith take the HD14584 (VT+ 2.7 V, VT- 2.1 V at 5 V: a duty of 47.98 %,
  205.3, 304.4, 369.6, 522.7 Hz), their edges exact in time and band-limited; IC2a solved at each hit's level.
- The VCAs' coupling capacitors at a start from silence where the running bank holds them, measured on the model
  (-0.390 V for C32 and C33 within 2 s, -0.526 V on the high band, -0.514 V on the low; ngspice -0.524 and -0.515).
  Started instead from the circuit's operating point, a hat's first millisecond came out 1.5 dB quiet.
- Each envelope runs with its trigger's parts (the switch, Q19 and its diodes, Q21 and Q24, Q28) only around a hit,
  then without them, the state passed across (as the bass drum's glide, D251). Seen from the closed hat, Q23's base
  is a saturated transistor's, as it is whenever the closed hat reaches it.
- IC5's op-amps without their rails: at full accent they reach 6.5 V of 14. IC2b keeps them (12.5 V at full accent).
- The emitter followers Q25, Q26, Q31 ideal: their offsets do not reach the outputs, their gain is within 0.2 %.

Checked: IC2a within 0.3 % of ngspice, IC3's bands within 4 %, every envelope within 3 %, the cowbell within 2 % at
its output; the cymbal's and the hats' outputs within 0.8 dB in level and 0.9 dB rms in third-octave bands (sample by
sample 14 to 43 %, the swing VCAs switching a fraction of a sample apart; at 192 kHz within 1 to 9 %). The closed hat
cuts the open one by 30 dB. Against both units: the cowbell's tones as theirs (fitted), its decay 106 to 126 ms
against 83 and 106 to 129; the closed hat 39 to 43 ms against 42 to 47; the cymbal's decays across TONE and DECAY
within the spread its own oscillator phasings give (some 15 %); the spectra within 1 to 5 dB, the cymbal 3 to 4 dB
strong from 2.5 to 5 kHz. Left open: the open hat's decay (above); unaccented, every voice 20 to 35 % under page 14's
amplitude.

Priced (`VoiceCost.h`): in the Kit mode the whole kit in its one voice, 70.1 % of a block at its 99th percentile with
every instrument struck at once (68.5 and 72.4 in two runs; average 52 %); in the other modes a voice holds one
instrument but runs the bank and the five VCAs, priced at the dearest, the cowbell, 48.6 %. A voice grows by some 27
kB (the 808 Metal is 131 kB, the 808 Drums 122).

## D256. 808 Snare & Clap: its design (2026-11-22)

The last family of the whole 808 (D250): the snare, the hand clap and the maracas (D255 is kept for 808 Metal's
build). Read from the service notes (p. 6, the circuit text and the clap's generating cycle, Fig. 13; p. 9, the
drawing; p. 14, the table). The snare: two bridged-Ts around IC14 rung by the trigger through Q46, their balance on
VR8 (TONE), and the snappy, white noise through a swing VCA (Q48) whose envelope Q47 and VR9 (SNAPPY) set, a
high-pass (C66, C67, Q49), all mixed in IC13 with VR7 (LEVEL). The clap: white noise through IC21's band-pass into
two VCAs, a long "reverb" envelope through Q70 and IC23's (AN6912) sawtooth envelope of short bursts into IC22
(BA662), mixed in IC19; the maracas share the circuit through SW12, the noise gated by Q65 into the high-pass Q68.
Page 9's "portion changed" lowers the snare: C58 0.027 to 0.056 uF and C61 0.0068 to 0.015 uF (one capacitor of
each bridged-T), R191 8.2 to 10 kOhm, R200 47 kOhm to a link; and on the clap a 10 kOhm from the -15 V line to Q70's
emitter. As drawn the snare rings near 250 and 500 Hz (page 14's 238 and 476), changed near 173 and 336 Hz; both
units here ring at 172 Hz and 332 to 340 Hz, so they carry the change, as do Roland's ACB remakes and most 808s; early
units (to about mid-1981) ring high
([Norgatronics, 808 Snare - Mutations](https://norgatronics.blogspot.com/2021/11/808-snare-mutations.html), after
Kurt Werner's figures). The user's choices:

- The snare (options: the changed values, recommended and chosen; both as a two-way knob; the early values as
  drawn).
- The keys (options: General MIDI with alternates, recommended and chosen: snare 38 and 40, clap 39, maracas 70;
  one key each). The clap's and maracas' key sets SW12, as a conga's sets its pair's switch.
- The knobs past the 808's own four (snare Level, Tone, Snappy; clap/maracas Level) (options: Accent and six
  bends, recommended and chosen: snare Tune (both shells' capacitors together), snare Decay (the shells' damping),
  Snappy Decay, clap Decay (the reverb envelope), clap Spread (the bursts' timing), maracas Decay, one knob free;
  Accent and three bends; Accent only).
- The checks (options: each circuit stage by stage against ngspice, then Fischer's and Audiorealism's recordings and
  page 14, any fit put to the user first, recommended and chosen; ngspice and page 14 only).

## D257. 808 Snare & Clap: built (2026-11-22)

Built as designed (D256): the 808 Snare & Clap generator (`Tr808SnareClapGenerator.h`, generator type 15) on two
circuits, the snare in its changed version (`Tr808Snare.h`) and the clap or maracas with SW12 (`Tr808Clap.h`), each
checked stage by stage against ngspice (`tools/tr808/snare.cir`, `clap.cir`, `tests/V3Tr808SnareClapTests.cpp`), then
against Fischer's and Audiorealism's recordings and page 14 (`docs/research/808-snare-clap.md`). The parts the notes do
not describe come from their makers' data or their clones': the AN6912 as LM339-like open-collector comparators
(Panasonic's AN1339/AN6912N sheet), the BA662, whose Rohm sheet is not online, as Open Music Labs' reverse-engineered
schematic and Alfa's AS662D clone draw it, a standard transconductance amplifier. The user's choices while building:

- IC23's timing capacitor C140, printed 0.0027 uF (options: 0.027 uF, page 6's 30 ms window and Fig. 13's three claps
  and a fourth, the fourth at 30.7 ms as both units' at 30.7 to 31.5, recommended and chosen; 0.0027 uF as printed, a
  4 ms window, one clap and a smeared second; 0.021 uF, a hobby model's fit to one recording).
- The sawtooth's C144, drawn 0.47 uF (options: 0.39 uF, inside an electrolytic's 20 %, the claps every 11.1 ms, the
  mean of Audiorealism's 10.5 and Fischer's 11.6, recommended and chosen; 0.47 uF as drawn, every 13 ms; 0.36 uF,
  Audiorealism's unit alone).
- The C.P. OFFSET trimmer TM3 (options: the service notes' adjustment, each clap falling from 6 to 0.5 Vpp before
  the next, 0.62 of its travel, recommended and chosen; fitted to both units' depth, some 0.61; the trimmer's middle,
  each clap falling 7 dB, the claps smearing).
- IC21's band-pass, as drawn 3 to 8 dB brighter than both units above 2 kHz in the claps and the tail alike and
  centred at 1.27 kHz against their 0.93 to 1.19, while the snappy and the maracas on the same noise bus and output
  match both within 1 to 2 dB (options: C127 0.82 nF (drawn 1.8) and C128, C129 5.2 nF (+10 %), every octave from
  500 Hz to 16 kHz within about 1 dB of both units, centred at 1.14 kHz, recommended and chosen; C127 alone at 0.68
  nF, 1.6 dB rms, the centre left high; IC21 as drawn).

Found while building, each the circuit as drawn, its reference's own model or the method's limit, so no choice:

- The snare as changed needed no fit: both shells within 0.3 % of both units' pitch, their decays within 4 %, the
  TONE pot's balance (linear) within about 1 dB at all five marks.
- At rest Q70's base sits at the C.P. OFFSET's voltage, which D68 and D69 reach near no bias: the clap is silent
  between hits and its reverb dies away toward it over seconds, as both units' (-59 dB at a second, -67 to -72 at
  two). The model keeps both diodes once the trigger has passed.
- Q72's collector, R374 and the BA662's mirror tie their currents: Q72's junction capacitance, as the reference has
  it, unties them (`isWellPosed`, D255).
- The capacitors a diode or a switch charges within a sample (C139, C143, C135, C140, C141) and the small junction
  capacitances take the backward Euler rule: the bilinear transform overshot (C139 to 4.9 V against 2.8) and the
  diode held the overshoot. The rest state solves R348's chain open: it carries no current at rest, and passed back
  and forth a sample apart it crept by leakage.
- IC23's switches change at the sample where their inputs cross, the sample solved again with the new switch; a
  millivolt's lean toward on keeps pins 2 and 13 low at rest, as page 6 has them.
- Q68's high-pass is prewarped to its 10.6 kHz resonance (3.7 % low at 96 kHz otherwise); the maracas' peak then lies
  at 10.4 kHz against the units' 10.5 to 10.7.
- Made cheaper without changing the sound: the maracas split in two circuits joined at Q66's base (its base current,
  a few uA into C134's 33 nF, a sample late), 59 to 36 % of a block alone; a quiet part stops being solved and holds
  its last output, so nothing steps at the mixer, and a part rung down by the generator's -80 dB starts its next hit
  from rest.

Checked: the snare within 1.5 % of ngspice at every stage unaccented (the snappy's high-pass 6 %), the clap within
4 % (IC23's pins 2 and 5, switching inside a sample, 5.4 %), the maracas within 1.6 % up to Q68's high-pass; the nodes
after a switch inside a sample (the accented snappy, the maracas' high-pass) by their envelopes, within 3 to 7 %.
Against both units: the claps at 0.8 to 1.8, 11.8 to 12.8, 22.8 to 24 and 31 to 33 ms (Fischer 0.7, 12.0, 23.9, 31.4;
Audiorealism 0.5, 9.5 to 10.8, 21.2 to 22, 31.2 to 32.8), down 40 dB 92 to 123 ms after the peak (the units 100 to
149); the snappy over the shells within about 2 dB of both from SNAPPY's middle up, between them at its quarter; the
maracas' peak and decay as theirs. Page 14 at full level: the snare 3.0 Vpp (3), the clap 5.4 (6), the maracas 4.5
(3). Left open: the clap's late tail 4 to 12 dB under both units after 120 ms and the accented snare 2.8 dB over page
14, both waiting for the accent check (TODO 33); neither recording set gives its accent level.

Priced (`VoiceCost.h`): in the Kit mode the whole kit in its one voice, 49.0 % of a block at its 99th percentile (51.5
and 47.3 in two runs; average 26.4 %); in the other modes a voice holds one instrument, priced at the dearest, the
maracas, 32.1 %. The generator is 92 kB, under the 808 Drums' 122.

## D258. The 808's accent: what the recordings were struck at, and the AC LEVEL's top (2026-11-22)

TODO 33's accent check, the user's idea: each recording set's accent deduced from what the accent does that no gain
can (`docs/research/808-accent.md`). The toms' and congas' pitch drop, measured as the gf180-parasynth project
measures it (its published per-file results for 808 From Mars, a real 808 at three accent levels; its method read and
reimplemented), read through the model at the hit's own pitch; the bass drum's half cycles, sigh and harmonics; every
voice's accented over normal amplitude against page 14, which states its settings (AC LEVEL fully counter-clockwise,
then ACCENT fully clockwise).

Found:
- Fischer's and Audiorealism's hits read 4.5 to 5 V on the toms, congas and bass drum alike: Audiorealism's "accented"
  hits were struck with the AC LEVEL near its bottom.
- 808 From Mars's three levels read 4.7, 8.2 and 11.7 V on all three toms across eleven TUNING marks; its congas'
  only accented level 11.7 V. The model's drop follows the TUNING and the accent as the real one does.
- Page 14's ratios are met at 10.3 to 12.2 V by the bass drum, snare, toms, congas and claves (the rim shot 9.1 V).
- So a real 808's top accent acts as about 11.5 V in these circuits, not the 14 V of the service notes' text. The
  open hat's decay (D255) and the clap's tail (D257) are no accent effect: the units are as good as unaccented.
- On the way: the rim shot's two rings some 6 dB off balance against both units, and a low thump in the claves.

The user's choices (options, the recommended first, all taken):
- The Accent knob's top (options: 11.5 V; keep 14 V as drawn; 11.5 V and the bottom raised to 4.5 V): 11.5 V, in the
  808 Drums, 808 Snare & Clap and 808 Metal, and the Kick's 808 the same (the user: "that should be the same as the
  rest"). The trigger is 4 + 7.5 x Accent x velocity volts (`Tr808BassDrum::triggerFor`). There the drums lie within
  -0.5 to +1.2 dB of page 14's ratios, against +0.8 to +3.3 dB at 14 V.
- Succession 808 (options: its Accent to 0.8, the trigger it was passed at; keep 0.6, softer hard hits): 0.8. The
  Kick's level calibration stays; its default hit peaks some 0.86 times as high.
- The open hat's decay (options: fit a part to both units; keep as drawn): fit.
- The clap's tail (options: fit the reverb to both units; keep as drawn): fit.
- The rim shot's balance and the claves' thump (options: look into both and fit; note them for later; keep): look
  into both and fit.

Asked by the user: were earlier fits made for what the accent explains? Checked at the units' 4.5 to 5 V, none was:
the toms' and congas' decays move 1.2 % at most between 4 and 4.7 V (D252's resistors moved them 10 to 30 %), the
claves' 1 % (R313), the rim shot over the claves 0.6 dB (R324, still between the units), the clap's claps not at all
from 4 to 8 V (C140, C144).

The open hat, fitted on the circuits in the test runner (`COOLSYNTH_808_OH_FIT`): C60 brings the middle marks onto the
units, R134 the shortest, R139 only the longest. The user's choice (options: C60 8 uF and R134 82 kOhm, log error
4.6 %; C60 alone at 8 uF, 7.6 %, the shortest 14 % long): both. 76, 209, 339, 457, 550 ms against the units' 71 to 77,
190 to 216, 339 to 344, 464 to 481, 529 to 530 (as drawn 93, 251, 406, 541, 568). `tools/tr808/metal.cir` takes the
same values and its open hat references were rendered again.

Found while fitting: the engine's overload rule (D222) cuts Kit-mode voices, which count as released from their
strike. CoolSynthRender reads the load as a live host does; at 96 kHz the 808 Metal's strike block takes 3.4 ms of
its 2.67 even alone, so its hits stop after 6 ms (at 48 kHz they ring out); under load claps stopped after 7 to 445
ms, and one render crashed. Not fixed here: a task of its own.

The clap's tail, fitted through the generator in the test runner (`COOLSYNTH_808_CLAP_FIT`, clear of the overload
rule): as drawn 6 to 8 dB under both units from 150 to 500 ms at 4 V. C139 alone fits (C138 or R349 alone make it
worse; C139 and C138 together, as CP Decay scales them, leave 4 dB at 150 ms). The user's choice (options: C139
39 nF; 36 nF; C139 and C138 times 1.74): 39 nF, the tail from 150 ms to a second within about 2 dB of both (rms 1.7
dB, 7.0 as drawn), the claps and the first 100 ms unmoved. `tools/tr808/clap.cir` takes it; the clap's references were
rendered again, the maracas' unchanged by it.
